# Changelog

All notable changes to Spiral Pool are documented in this file.

The format follows [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.1.0/).
Versioning follows `MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH` - patch releases are applied in-place on the same tag.

---

## [v3.0.0] - 2026-09-21 - Spiral Covenant

Payout-routing, automation and node-currency release. Each block pays the wallet address configured for its coin, and an operator who wants individual rigs paid at their own addresses can turn on worker-name payout, which ships off; merge-mined blocks pay the configured aux address on every aux chain either way. Stratum V2 was rebuilt against the specification in five steps — real block templates, the Noise handshake, a persisted authority key miners can pin, message conformance, vardiff and extended channels, and finally interoperability, which the Stratum Reference Implementation's translator and mining client now demonstrate by mining against the pool — and it **stays off unless the operator opts in**. Three additions an operator sees: a read-only miner portal, an operator-only block explorer, and miner automation (sleep windows, power schedules, auto-restart). Six coin daemons move: Litecoin, Fractal Bitcoin and Bitcoin II past consensus changes that have **already activated** on their networks, eCash and Syscoin for maintenance, and Bitcoin Silver from a 2024 source build to upstream's Core 31.1 release binaries; DigiByte and Namecoin stay where they are. Downloads for the three coins that publish no checksum file are now verified against a pinned SHA256, and the install refuses to proceed on a mismatch. Sentinel's auto-restart defect and sixty-nine pre-existing bugs are fixed. One was found on the first Linux test install: a solo Bitcoin Silver or Bitcoin Cash II install set up no wallet and no RPC password. Nine more came from the second test install and a review of the steps before its sync screen. Among them: generated wallet addresses are now confirmed by the coin's own daemon before they are saved, the pool wallet no longer drops out after a reboot, and a fresh install's dashboard no longer waits for the chain to catch up after a reboot. **No database migrations.** There is **one config change**: the first v3.0 `upgrade.sh` comments out `port_v2` unless the operator chooses to keep Stratum V2 (see below). Coin daemons change only when the operator runs `coin-upgrade.sh`, and none of the six needs a config change — though a legacy (BDB) wallet stops the Bitcoin II and Bitcoin Silver upgrades until it is migrated. What was not tested is listed under **Tests**, and what is deferred under **Planned**, at the end of this section.

### New features in v3.0.0

Everything an operator or a miner can see that did not exist in v2.7.1. Each line links to the section that documents it in full, further down.

| Feature | What it is | Ships |
|---|---|---|
| **Read-only miner portal** | A page a miner can be given without handing over the dashboard: their own workers, shares and hashrate, and nothing else. The list of every active address now requires the admin key. | on |
| **Block explorer (operator only)** | `/explorer` reads the pool's own coin nodes rather than a third party — chain state, the last ten blocks with the pool's own marked, and lookups by block, transaction or address. Works on pruned nodes, and says what a pruned node cannot answer. | on |
| **Miner automation** | `/automation` — sleep windows, power schedules and auto-restart, run by Sentinel. Weekday-scoped rules that may cross midnight. | on, no rules by default |
| **DigiDollar stats** | Network-wide DigiDollar health on the Management tab: collateralisation, supply, DGB locked, oracle price and age, active positions. Requires an **unpruned** DigiByte node — see that section for why a pruned node is never asked. | on where applicable |
| **Block payout audit** | Sentinel reads each found block's coinbase and alerts if it paid an address this pool is not configured to use — the one event a solo pool cannot afford to get wrong, and the one nothing previously checked. | on |
| **Coin daemon version alerts** | Sentinel says when a coin daemon is behind the version this release installs, when upstream publishes something newer than that, and — new — when the check itself has stopped working, instead of reading silence as good news. | on |
| **Worker-name payout** | Individual rigs paid at their own addresses instead of one address per coin. | **off** |
| **Stratum V2** | Rebuilt against the specification in five steps. Verified against the Stratum Reference Implementation, with a NerdQAxe++ on a live node, and on BTC regtest, where a V2 share mined to the network target advanced the chain tip. Ships disabled: testing covers one miner on one coin. | **off** |
| **Pinned download checksums** | The three coins that publish no checksum file are verified against a pinned SHA256, and the install refuses to proceed on a mismatch. | on |

Two of these are deliberately off. Stratum V2 stays off because the testing behind it is one miner, one firmware and one coin — not because no firmware has been tried — and worker-name payout stays off because turning it on changes where money goes.

### Changed — payouts
- **A miner can be paid at the address in its own worker name — off by default.** Previously the job manager held one reward address per coin. `CoinPool`'s authorize callback overwrote it with `SetSoloMinerAddress` and forced a job refresh, so the shared job paid whichever miner had authorized last — for every miner connected to that coin. Jobs now carry the coinbase split around the reward output (`CoinBase2Prefix` / `CoinBase2Suffix`) and a `PayoutScript` builder, and `Job.CoinBase2For(address)` rebuilds `coinb2` for one address. The address is used in four places: each `mining.notify`, share validation's merkle root, block assembly and the emergency block WAL. So a share validates against the coinbase that miner actually hashed, and a found block pays that miner. A worker name that is not a valid address for the coin pays the configured wallet. On the multi-coin smart port the payout comes from `resolveWallet` (per-coin wallet map, else the configured wallet), never the worker name. Payout scripts are cached per address, capped at 10,000 entries. Jobs are sent only to authorized sessions, and the post-authorize job applies the session's difficulty, because before authorize the payout address is unknown. `SetSoloMinerAddress`/`GetSoloMinerAddress` are removed. All of it is gated on `stratum.payoutFromWorkerName`, which ships **false**: every block pays the configured wallet whatever a miner calls itself, and worker names are labels only. The default is off because the switch is reachable by anyone who can reach the stratum port — with it on, a miner takes the block reward simply by naming its worker after its own address, which is appropriate for a pool serving third-party miners and not for a single-operator pool. `spiralctl mining payout` reports the current mode; `spiralctl mining payout worker` turns it on after a confirmation that says plainly what it allows, and `spiralctl mining payout wallet` turns it back off. That confirmation names **every** stratum port the setting opens, because it said "the stratum port" in the singular while a pool running V2 and TLS serves three, and the switch is gated in the shared job manager rather than per-port — so an operator who had secured the V1 port and taken that for the whole exposure would have been wrong. It also says where the address comes from on each: a worker name on V1, and the channel's user identity on V2. Both write the setting into the existing config with comments and ordering intact, and both print the restart needed to apply it. The MOTD and the post-install command list name the new command. (`cmd/spiralctl/cmd/payout.go`, `cmd/spiralctl/cmd/mining.go`, `internal/config/config.go`, `install.sh`, `upgrade.sh`.) (`pkg/protocol/protocol.go`, `internal/jobs/manager.go`, `internal/shares/validator.go`, `internal/shares/validator_v2.go`, `internal/stratum/server.go`, `internal/stratum/v1/handler.go`, `internal/scheduler/multiserver.go`, `internal/pool/coinpool.go`, `internal/pool/pool.go`.)
- The single-operator notices say what the pool now does: blocks pay the configured wallet by default, and with worker-name payout turned on a valid address in a worker name is paid instead, while invalid names, the smart port and every merge-mined aux chain still pay the configured wallet. (`README.md`, `WARNINGS.md`, `TERMS.md` §5D, `docs/reference/MINER_SUPPORT.md`.)

### Changed — merge mining
- **Aux blocks for DOGE, PEP, NMC and SYS were requested with `getauxblock`, which pays a key from the aux node's own wallet instead of `mergeMining.auxChains[].address`.** Only Fractal Bitcoin and Myriad used `createauxblock <address>`. Every aux chain now uses `createauxblock` with the configured address; each pinned aux daemon's source was checked for it, and Syscoin 5.1.0 does not define `getauxblock` at all. `DaemonClient.GetAuxBlock`, the `CreateAuxBlockCoin` interface and `UseCreateAuxBlock` are removed. **Operators should confirm each configured aux address is one they control.** (`internal/auxpow/manager.go`, `internal/daemon/client.go`, `internal/coin/coin.go`, `internal/coin/fractalbtc.go`, `internal/coin/myriad.go`, `internal/config/config.go`.)

### Changed — Stratum V2 is opt-in
- **Earlier installers configured and opened a Stratum V2 port for every coin without asking.** The V2 implementation is not production-ready (see **Planned**), so it is now off unless the operator asks for it.
- **`install.sh`:** asks *Enable Stratum V2 ports? [y/N]*, default No. On No, each coin's `port_v2` is written commented out, and none of the V2 `ufw` rules (the single-coin one or the 16 multi-coin ones) are added. V2 URLs are left out of the install summary and the credentials file. The answer is recorded as `ENABLE_V2_STRATUM` in `coins.env`, and add-coin mode reuses it. The runtime helper scripts install.sh writes check the same flag.
- **`upgrade.sh`:** a one-time `migrate_stratum_v2_opt_in` runs before services restart and asks *Keep Stratum V2 ports open? [y/N]*. Anything but yes, including `--auto` and runs with no terminal, does three things:
  - comments out `port_v2` in `config.yaml`, backed up to `config.yaml.bak.stratumv2` first
  - removes the V2 `ufw` rules
  - records `ENABLE_V2_STRATUM=false`

  Stratum V1 and TLS ports are untouched. A marker file (`config/.migrated-stratum-v2-opt-in`) makes it run once, so an operator who re-enables V2 afterwards keeps it; fresh v3.0 installs write the marker and skip it.
- **`scripts/linux/pool-mode.sh`:** its 16 V2 `ufw` rules, and the `listenV2` → `port_v2` rewrite when converting single-coin to multi-coin, now follow `ENABLE_V2_STRATUM`.
- **Windows:** `install-windows.ps1` documents Stratum V2 as unsupported, yet it checked the V2 port for conflicts, forwarded it through `netsh portproxy` and opened a firewall rule for it. It no longer does any of the three, and re-running the installer removes the old rule, since it recreates every `Spiral Pool*` rule. `scripts/windows/configure-firewall.ps1` and `configure-coin-firewall.ps1` open V2 ports only when `docker\.env` sets `STRATUM_V2_ENABLED=true`; `configure-coin-firewall.ps1` removes the coin's existing rules first, so an old V2 rule goes away. `wsl2-stratum-proxy.ps1` forwards V2 ports only when `/spiralpool/config/coins.env` inside WSL2 has `ENABLE_V2_STRATUM=true`, and otherwise deletes the V2 firewall rules its earlier runs created.
- Docs: opt-in note under the ports table in `docs/reference/REFERENCE.md`, and a v3.0.0 section in `docs/setup/UPGRADE_GUIDE.md`.

### Added — Stratum V2 difficulty negotiation, and validation against real SV2 firmware

- **A V2 channel opened at one number from config, whatever the device was.** The V1 path reads the `mining.subscribe` user agent and hands it to the Spiral Router, so a lottery miner and an S21 do not start on the same difficulty — the whole reason the router exists. V2 has no user agent, nothing replaced it, and every channel took `stratum.difficulty.initial` regardless. V2 does carry something better than a user agent, though: `OpenMiningChannel` states the device's own nominal hashrate, and the pool validated that field for NaN and infinity, logged it, and then ignored it. Channels now open at `hashrate × targetTime ÷ 2³²` — the relation the router already uses, which holds for every algorithm so no algorithm branch is needed — and a miner that declares no usable hashrate falls back to the router on the `SetupConnection` vendor string. The selection is injected into the V2 server rather than implemented there, the way `Pipeline` already is, because the router lives in the V1 stratum package and reaching for it directly would be an import cycle; the fallback goes through a new `Server.GetInitialDifficultyForUserAgent` so a Scrypt pool's profiles are not answered out of SHA-256d's. **This is the pool's opening offer only.** A miner's `max_target` floor is applied afterwards and still wins, because the specification says the pool MUST respect it, and a test asserts exactly that so difficulty selection cannot later be "improved" into a spec violation. A pool that wires no selector behaves exactly as before, which is also asserted. (`internal/stratum/v2/server.go`, `internal/stratum/server.go`, `internal/pool/coinpool.go`, `internal/stratum/v2/difficulty_negotiation_test.go`.)

- **A channel's difficulty could not be attributed to its source.** Neither the pool's choice nor the floor requested by the device was logged, only the difficulty that resulted from the two. An operator seeing a value that matched nothing in their config had no way to tell which side had set it. Opening a channel now logs the vendor, the declared hashrate, the difficulty the pool chose, whether the miner sent a `max_target`, whether that clamped the result, and the final difficulty. When a miner's floor overrides the pool for the first time, the log also states the cause: under V2 the miner declares the easiest target it will accept, the specification requires the pool to honour it, `stratum.difficulty.initial` and the Spiral Router cannot lower it, and the setting to change is on the miner. It notes that for solo mining the difficulty is cosmetic, since share difficulty does not affect the probability of finding a block. This message is emitted once per server, not once per channel.

- **What real firmware found.** The first SV2 connection from actual hardware — a NerdQAxe++ on a DigiByte pool — handshook, opened an extended channel, took jobs, and had **every share rejected** with `reason: low-difficulty, actualDiff: 0`. `actualDiff: 0` is not "share too easy"; it means the pool rebuilt a header the miner had never hashed. The cause was that the test pool's config carried no `version_rolling` section, so `VersionRollingAllowed` was false and the pool advertised `version_rolling_allowed: false` — while the BM1370 rolls the version field in hardware and does it anyway. The pool then dropped the rolled bits and hashed the base version. Neither side is wrong in isolation and neither logs anything alarming; the mismatch only exists between them. Enabling version rolling fixed the reconstruction, at which point the rejections changed to `stale`: the channel had opened at 50,000, which on a 4.98 TH/s miner is ~43 seconds per share against a chain producing a block every 15 seconds, so every share was found against a job two or three generations dead. Where that 50,000 came from was never established — it matched nothing in the config, and once difficulty selection was wired to the declared hashrate it did not recur, with the miner's `max_target` present but not binding. It is recorded as unexplained rather than attributed to the `max_target` clamp, which was the first theory and which the logging then contradicted. Adding the missing vardiff section produced accepted shares. Both were gaps in that pool's hand-written config rather than defects in this release; `install.sh` writes both sections. They are recorded because the failure produced no error on either side, and because the diagnosis generalises: `actualDiff: 0` indicates header reconstruction, not difficulty.

- **A config the pool cannot load drops it to single-coin V1, and says so once.** The same test pool had a `coins:` array but the older `daemon:` block instead of `nodes:`, which fails V2 validation — so `main()` logged one `warn`, fell back to V1, and ran there indefinitely. V1 is a supported mode and mines correctly, which is why the fallback goes unnoticed: the pool works, `wait-for-node.sh` announces "Detected V2 (multi-coin) configuration", and `spiralctl config validate` reports `✓ All checks passed`, because it checks YAML syntax rather than whether the pool would load the file as intended. What is silently lost is what the V2 runtime provides: Stratum V2, multi-coin, and per-coin node failover. Worker-name payout is **not** among them, despite `payout_from_worker_name` appearing zero times in `pool.go` — the field sits on the shared `StratumConfig`, and `pool.go` hands that struct to the job manager whole, so the switch is honoured on both paths. It is named here because counting a grep's hits in one file is what suggested otherwise, and that inference was wrong: a value passed wholesale is invisible to a search for its name. Not fixed here: making `config validate` report the fallback is the right answer and is a change to a validator during release validation. Recorded so the next person reads one `warn` line as the whole story it is.

### Added — read-only miner portal
- **Miners can look up their own mining by wallet address, with no dashboard login.** `http://<pool>:4000/portal` is a static page served by the stratum API on its existing public port; the same port that already serves the per-address pool endpoints. It calls one new endpoint, `GET /api/portal/{address}`, which returns the address's activity on every pool:
  - hashrate and share count over the last 24 hours
  - last share
  - each worker's hashrate and online state over the last 15 minutes
  - blocks the address found (up to 50 per coin), with status, confirmation progress, reward and worker

  Pools where the address has neither shares nor blocks are omitted. Both API servers serve it: the multi-coin server searches every registered pool, and the single-coin V1 server searches its one pool. Blocks are read with a new `PostgresDB.GetBlocksByMiner`.
- **Hardening:**
  - The address must match the API's existing wallet-address pattern before any query runs.
  - Lookups get their own per-IP limit of 2 per second, on top of the general API limit, because each lookup queries every pool. A single lookup across all pools is bounded to 10 seconds.
  - Database errors return a generic 500.
  - The page, script and stylesheet are embedded in the binary and served with `Content-Security-Policy: default-src 'none'; script-src 'self'; style-src 'self'; connect-src 'self'` (plus `frame-ancestors 'none'`). The page has no inline script, and the script writes every API value with `textContent`.
- **Limits worth knowing:**
  - The 24-hour figures cover shares the pool still holds, and shares are trimmed to 15 minutes whenever the pool restarts.
  - A worker whose name is not a valid address mines to the operator's wallet, and its blocks are recorded under that worker name, so they do not show up in a lookup of the operator's wallet address.
  - The list of every active address, `/api/pools/{id}/miners`, now needs the admin key (see **Fixed — dashboard and pool API**), so a wallet can be looked up only by someone who already knows it.

  (`internal/api/portal.go`, `internal/api/portal/`, `internal/api/server.go`, `internal/api/server_v2.go`, `internal/database/postgres.go`.)

### Added — block explorer (operator only)
- **A block explorer in the dashboard reads the pool's own coin nodes, and works on pruned nodes.** `/explorer` is linked from the ⛓ button beside Settings and requires the dashboard's session login; API keys are not accepted, because address scans are heavy. For any enabled coin it shows:
  - **the node:** chain, blocks and headers, whether it is pruned and from which height, size on disk, mempool
  - **the last ten blocks,** marking those this pool found
  - **a block by height or hash:** its header is always shown, since pruned nodes keep every header. Its transactions and coinbase outputs are shown while the node still has the block's data; otherwise the page says the data was pruned and from which height the node keeps blocks.
  - **a transaction:** from the mempool, or from a named block. A pruned node has no transaction index, so transactions are opened from their block.
  - **an address's unspent outputs and balance,** from `scantxoutset`, which reads the UTXO set and so works pruned or not
- **Hardening:**
  - Heights, block hashes and transaction IDs are validated before any RPC call.
  - Addresses must be plain base58/bech32/CashAddr characters, so user input cannot extend the `addr(...)` descriptor handed to `scantxoutset`.
  - One address scan per coin runs at a time (a second gets 409), with a 300-second timeout. `coin_rpc` gained an optional `timeout` argument, default unchanged at 10 seconds.
  - The page's CSP allows only same-origin script, and the script writes node data with `textContent`.
  - Daemons built on Bitcoin Core 0.14 (Dogecoin 1.14) take a boolean `verbose` and no block-hash argument; both calls fall back for them.
- **Upgrade fix:** `upgrade.sh`'s `update_dashboard` copied only `static/css` and `static/templates`, so an upgraded install would have served the explorer page without its script. It now copies `static/js` as well. (`src/dashboard/explorer.py`, `src/dashboard/dashboard.py`, `src/dashboard/templates/explorer.html`, `src/dashboard/static/js/explorer.js`, `src/dashboard/static/css/explorer.css`, `upgrade.sh`.)

### Added — block payout audit

- **Sentinel now checks that a found block's coinbase actually paid this pool.** A solo pool finds a block every few weeks or months, and that block is the entire product — yet nothing verified where its coinbase paid. An address edited in config that a running stratum never picked up, a coin configured with another coin's address, a wallet reloaded under a different label: each gives a pool that mines perfectly, alerts nothing, and pays somebody else. The failure is invisible precisely because it can only appear on the rarest event the pool has, and by the time it does the block is on the chain and cannot be taken back. Each block the pool records is read once via `getblock`, and if no coinbase output pays an address the pool is configured to use, `block_payout_mismatch` names what was paid, what was expected, and what to check. Zero-value outputs are counted as commitments rather than payments, because that is what they are — the SegWit commitment and, on DigiByte, the DigiDollar oracle bundle. Both daemon reply shapes are read: Core 22+ reports a single `address`, older daemons a list of `addresses`, and this pool runs both.

- **It is deliberately silent whenever it cannot be sure.** Per-worker payout (`payout_from_worker_name`) and a multi-port `wallet_map` both mean a block legitimately pays an address other than the coin's configured one, so either suspends the audit without asking the node anything. A coin still carrying a `YOUR_..._ADDRESS` placeholder is not a finding but an unfinished setup. A node that cannot be read leaves the block **unaudited rather than marked bad**, so the check retries instead of reporting a misdirected block because a daemon was briefly down — the same distinction between "no news" and "could not ask" that this release draws in the coin version checks. Each block is audited once, and the cooldown is keyed per block hash, so a mismatch on one block can never mute the alert for the next. Mutation-checked twice: accepting every block, and treating an unreadable node as a real answer, each fail exactly the assertions that exist to catch them. (`src/sentinel/SpiralSentinel.py`, `scripts/spiralctl.sh`, `tests/test_block_payout_audit.py`.)

### Added — DigiDollar stats (DigiByte)

- **The dashboard now reports DigiDollar's network health from the pool's own DigiByte node.** DigiDollar activated on DGB mainnet at block 23,869,440 on 17 July 2026, and Spiral Pool already mined it correctly — `digibyte.go` requests the `digidollar-oracle` getblocktemplate rule and `daemon/client.go` copies the MuSig2 oracle price bundle into the coinbase — but nothing surfaced what the system it was helping secure actually looked like. A card on the Management tab now shows collateralisation and health tier, DD supply in circulation, DGB locked as collateral, the oracle price and how many blocks old it is, the count of active positions, the DCA tier, and any minting restriction. It is hidden entirely on a pool that runs no DigiByte node. (`src/dashboard/dashboard.py`, `src/dashboard/templates/dashboard.html`, `tests/test_digidollar_stats.py`.)

- **A pruned node is asked only what it can answer cheaply — which is more than none of it.** Four of DigiDollar's read-only RPCs route through `GetDigiDollarRpcTotals` or `GetDigiDollarRpcSystemHealth`, and both fall back to `ForceFlushStateToDisk()` plus a full UTXO-set scan **while holding `cs_main`** when the DigiDollar stats index is absent — which it always is on a pruned node, because the index syncs from genesis and so cannot start below a prune point (DigiByte `init.cpp`: `-prune set -> setting -digidollarstatsindex=0`; forcing it back on makes the index refuse to start rather than run). `cs_main` is the lock `getblocktemplate` needs, so on a mining node that scan stops block production for as long as it runs. Those four — `getdigidollarstats`, `getprotectionstatus`, `getdcamultiplier` and `calculatecollateralrequirement` — are never sent to a pruned node, and the first test asserts each is absent from the call log. The last two reach the scan through a helper rather than directly, which a first reading of `digidollar.cpp` missed; the classification was redone by following the call graph rather than grepping each function body. `getoracleprice` and `getdigidollardeploymentinfo` touch neither, so a pruned node answers both for nothing, and the panel shows them: oracle price, whether it is stale, its age in blocks, the number of active oracles, activation status and the current MuSig2 session state. That happens to be the half a mining operator has most use for — this pool copies the oracle price bundle into its coinbases, so a stale oracle is not a market curiosity but the reason a block would carry no bundle at all. Supply and collateralisation are **omitted rather than zeroed**, since a zero supply reads as a collapsed stablecoin. Spiral Pool offers DGB pruning and this release's own guidance recommends it, so the pruned path is the one most operators will see, not an edge case.

- **"Could not read" is never rendered as "zero".** The four ways the answer can be unavailable — node unreachable, node pruned, DigiDollar not active on this chain, stats index still syncing — are distinct reasons with the daemon's own message attached, rather than a panel of zeros that reads like a collapsed stablecoin. This is the same distinction the coin version checks gained in this release, applied at a second site.

- **`coin_rpc` gained an opt-in `raise_error`.** It returns `None` for every failure by design, which is right for callers that need a value or nothing and wrong for one that must tell "not active on this chain" from "node is down". `raise_error=True` raises `CoinRPCError` carrying the daemon's message; the default is unchanged, so no existing caller is affected, and a test asserts exactly that.

### Added — miner automation (sleep windows, power schedules, auto-restart)
- **A new Automation page (`/automation`, the ⏰ button beside ⛓) sets schedules that Sentinel runs.** Each rule covers chosen weekdays from a start to an end time in Sentinel's display timezone; a window that ends before it starts runs past midnight. A rule does one of two things:
  - puts its miners to sleep for the window, and wakes them when it ends
  - switches them to a power mode (low, normal or high) or a power target in watts

  When windows overlap, the first rule in the list wins. Outside every power window a miner keeps its last mode.
- **How Sentinel applies the rules:**
  - A power mode is sent again when a miner comes back online, since a reboot resets it.
  - A failed action is retried every 5 minutes and raises the new `automation_failed` alert.
  - The list of miners it has put to sleep is saved, so a restarted Sentinel still wakes them.
  - Rules run on the HA master only, even when the pool API is unreachable.
- **What each miner family can do** (full table in `docs/reference/MINER_SUPPORT.md`):

  | Family | Sleep / wake | Power |
  |---|---|---|
  | Bitaxe / AxeOS | pause / resume (ESP-Miner 2.14.0+) | – |
  | NerdQAxe | – | low / normal (eco or default frequency and voltage) |
  | Avalon Nano 3S, Q, Mini 3 | none | low / normal / high |
  | Stock Antminer | yes | low / normal |
  | Braiins OS, LuxOS, Vnish | yes | power target in watts |
  | Whatsminer (API v3) | yes | low / normal / high |
  | Elphapex, Goldshell | – | – |

  **None of these commands has been run against a real miner.** Each follows vendor documentation or several independent public descriptions, and is tested against mock devices built from them.
- **Credentials:** a miner's API login is stored from the page. It is write-only and kept in `device_credentials.json` (mode 0600) in the shared data directory. Without one, Sentinel uses the firmware's default login.
- **Auto-restart settings:** the page can override Sentinel's offline threshold and cooldown, which are then picked up without a restart. It adds an optional restart after a miner has been below its expected hashrate for a set number of minutes, and can exclude individual miners.
- **Sleeping miners:** a miner asleep on a schedule raises no offline, zombie or degradation alerts and is never auto-restarted. Celebrations also stay quiet during sleep and low-power windows.
- **Avalon schedules:** on first start, Nano 3S and Avalon Q schedules move from the dashboard's Avalon scheduler to automation rules (efficiency → low, balanced → normal, high → high). The old file is first backed up as `avalon_schedules.json.pre-automation`. Other Avalon models stay on the old scheduler, which now refuses Nano 3S and Q schedules and points to the page.
- **Avalons cannot sleep.** The first real-hardware test, on an Avalon Nano 3S (firmware MM319, build 25061101), accepted `power low` and `power normal`, but refused sleep and wake with `Unknown option: softoff` and `softon`. Those are the industrial Avalons' commands. The Nano's `ascset 0,help` lists no standby option, and `workmode`, `worklevel` and `work_mode_lvl` offer none. Sleep is therefore off for every Avalon. The Automation page, `info` and rule validation no longer offer it, and the driver never sends `softoff`/`softon`. The three power modes and restart stay, and all four were accepted by the Nano. The Avalon Q and Mini 3 run the same home firmware and were not tested; they are treated the same. (`src/sentinel/miner_automation.py`, `src/sentinel/miner_control.py`, `src/dashboard/static/js/automation.js`, `src/dashboard/templates/automation.html`, `docs/reference/MINER_SUPPORT.md`.)
- **Testing a miner directly:** `sudo spiralctl miner control <IP> info|sleep|wake|restart|power <low|normal|high|watts>` sends one command at once, through the same drivers and stored login Sentinel uses, and prints the miner's answer. `info` shows what the pool can do with that miner and whether a login is stored. `OK` means the miner accepted the command, not that it took effect. The miner's type, port and Avalon model come from Sentinel's `miners.json` and `automation.json`, and `--type`, `--model` and `--port` override them. It runs as the pool user and never prints the stored password. (`src/sentinel/miner_control.py`, `scripts/spiralctl.sh`, `docs/reference/MINER_SUPPORT.md`.)
- **Not supported yet:**
  - Elphapex and Goldshell sleep and power modes (public descriptions conflict or are unconfirmed)
  - NerdQAxe sleep (its shutdown cannot be woken remotely), and NerdQAxe power modes with OTP enabled
  - Whatsminer API v2
  - the Avalon Mini 3's heater and night modes
- `miner_automation.py` (rules, shared with the dashboard) and `miner_control.py` (device commands) are installed next to `SpiralSentinel.py` by `install.sh`, `upgrade.sh` and the Sentinel image; the dashboard and its image get `miner_automation.py`. (`src/sentinel/miner_automation.py`, `src/sentinel/miner_control.py`, `src/sentinel/SpiralSentinel.py`, `src/dashboard/dashboard.py`, `src/dashboard/templates/automation.html`, `src/dashboard/static/js/automation.js`, `src/dashboard/static/css/automation.css`, `install.sh`, `upgrade.sh`, `docker/Dockerfile.sentinel`, `docker/Dockerfile.dashboard`.)

### Fixed — Sentinel auto-restart
- **Auto-restart skipped miners it should have restarted.** It looked a miner up by its configured name in a hard-coded list of 15 types.
  - A miner whose display name polling had replaced with its worker name was never found.
  - bitaxe, nerdaxe, nerdoctaxe, qaxe, qaxeplus, canaan, ebang, gekkoscience, ipollo, epic, elphapex, braiins, vnish and luxos miners were never checked at all.

  It now finds the miner by IP, and the zombie stratum kick and reboot use the same lookup.
- **Several families could not be restarted at all:** Avalon, stock Antminer (the old request had no digest auth), Braiins OS, LuxOS, Vnish, Whatsminer and Elphapex. They now restart through `miner_control.py`. If that fails, the previous method is still tried.

### Fixed — Stratum V2 now mines real blocks (rebuild step 1 of 5)
- **A V2 share could never form a valid block, and a V2 block was never submitted.** The V2 server had its own job source (`JobManagerAdapter`) that polled `getblocktemplate` every 30 seconds, apart from the job manager and blind to ZMQ. It built each job's merkle root from the template's transactions with no coinbase leaf, so no header a V2 miner hashed could be a block. Its validator (`v2.ShareValidator`) checked shares against that root, and on a block hit it logged `Block solution found!` where the submission was left as the comment `// Block submission would go here`. It also never recorded V2 shares.
- **V2 standard channels now run on the V1 pipeline.**
  - Channels mine the job manager's jobs, pushed from the same callback that feeds V1, so new blocks reach V2 miners when they reach V1 miners.
  - Each channel is assigned a unique 12-byte extranonce prefix, filling the coinbase's reserved extranonce space. The channel is sent the merkle root of its own coinbase: `CoinBase1 || prefix || CoinBase2For(address)`, paying the address in its user identity (`address.worker`, split at the last dot as V1 does).
  - Shares are built with that prefix and address and handed to `CoinPool.HandleMultiPortShare`. That is the same validation, block submission, WAL, metrics, share pipeline and aux-block path the smart port uses.
  - Jobs on a new prevhash are sent as future jobs followed by `SetNewPrevHash`; refreshes on the same prevhash are sent active.
  - A client's `max_target` is honoured, answering `max-target-out-of-range`. `SubmitShares.Error` codes now follow the spec (`invalid-job-id`, `stale-share`, `difficulty-too-low`); previously every rejection read `difficulty-target-not-met`.
  - Removed: `JobManagerAdapter`, `v2.ShareValidator`, the unused `V1toV2JobAdapter`, `DifficultyToNBits`/`TargetToNBits` and the "accept every share when no handler is set" fallback. `shares.ShareMerkleRoot` and `ValidatorV2.ShareTarget` are new exports the V2 server uses.

  (`internal/stratum/v2/server.go`, `session.go`, `adapter.go`, `types.go`; `internal/pool/coinpool.go`; `internal/shares/validator.go`, `validator_v2.go`.)
- V2 sessions are still not counted in connected-miner totals, and their rejected shares log as "Multi-port share rejected". Steps 2–4 of the rebuild follow.

### Fixed — pre-existing bugs
- **An orphaned block kept the miners celebrating it for the rest of the two hours.** A block find starts `block-celebrate.sh` for a configurable duration, two hours by default, and nothing could end it early: the script took `--test`, `--duration`, `--miners`, `--scan`, `--force` and `--list`, and finished on its deadline, on quiet hours, or on a signal. Orphaning was none of those. Sentinel detects the orphan and alerts on it within minutes, so the pool knew the reward was gone while the lights went on announcing it. The script now records which blocks a celebration stands for and takes `--stop [--block HASH]`, and Sentinel withdraws a block when it orphans. The block-level accounting is the point rather than an embellishment: a later block *extends* the running celebration instead of restarting it, so the lights can stand for several blocks at once, and an unconditional stop would switch them off on a block that is still perfectly good. A withdrawal only ends the celebration if it was the last block standing. `--stop` sends `TERM` so the existing trap restores every LED it changed, rather than `KILL`, which would leave the miners lit in celebration colours — the same stranded-LED state this release already fixed once. A guard fails the suite if a celebration trigger does not record its block hash, or if an orphan alert site does not withdraw one: the first version of this fix wired only one of the three orphan sites and none of the seven triggers, which leaves celebrations starting, orphans alerting, and the lights simply never going out, with nothing anywhere complaining. (`scripts/linux/block-celebrate.sh`, `src/sentinel/SpiralSentinel.py`, `tests/test_celebration_stop.sh`, `tests/test_script_integrity.sh`.)
- **Orphan detection stalled the monitor loop by asking the pool API which coin it was, once per block.** `check_for_orphans` read the coin as `block.get("coin", get_primary_coin() or "UNKNOWN")`. Python evaluates a `.get()` default **eagerly**, so `get_primary_coin()` ran for every block even though the fallback was never used -- multi-coin pools always send `coin`. That call reaches `get_enabled_coins()`, whose manually-configured branch (`wallet_address` and `pool_id` both set, which is any hand-configured Sentinel) called `auto_detect_pool_coin()` **without caching the result**, unlike the two sibling branches either side of it. `auto_detect_pool_coin()` is an HTTP GET to `/api/pools` with `timeout=10`. The method fetches up to 50 blocks and runs once per pool, so a three-coin pool issued up to 150 requests per orphan-check cycle. Against a healthy localhost that is merely wasteful; when the pool API is slow or down it is up to ten seconds each, stalling the monitor loop for minutes inside a single call -- precisely when the pool is unwell and Sentinel is most needed. Confirmed by stack dump: `check_for_orphans -> get_primary_coin -> get_enabled_coins -> auto_detect_pool_coin -> socket.create_connection`, blocking. The fallback is now resolved at most once per call and only when a block actually lacks a coin, and the uncached branch caches like its siblings. (`src/sentinel/SpiralSentinel.py`.)
- **A multi-coin pool silently disabled its own orphan detection.** `known_block_statuses` is one dict shared by every pool, and the end of *every* `check_for_orphans` call pruned it to the newest 100 entries. `fetch_pool_blocks` returns up to 50 per pool and the method runs once per pool, so three coins overflow 100 within a **single cycle**: each pool evicted the entries of the pool checked before it. Next cycle those blocks were no longer tracked, so they took the "haven't seen this before" branch, were recorded afresh, and their `pending -> orphaned` transition was never compared -- the alert simply never fired. The prune discards the oldest `first_seen` first, which is exactly the long-pending blocks most likely to orphan, and the whole failure is silent: no exception, no log line, no duplicate alert to notice. Pruning is now per pool, and every bucket is pruned rather than only the current one so entries restored from a persisted state file without a pool tag stay bounded. Proved end to end before fixing: with three coins at 50 blocks each, an orphan on the first-checked pool produced `alerts=[]`. (`src/sentinel/SpiralSentinel.py`, `tests/test_orphan_detection_scale.py`.)
- **A solved block whose hash missed its share target was thrown away as "low difficulty".** Both validators checked the share target before the block target, so when a miner's share difficulty is above the network difficulty — a low-difficulty chain, regtest, or vardiff climbing past the network — a block was rejected and never submitted. A hash that meets the network target is now never rejected for difficulty. (`internal/shares/validator.go`, `validator_v2.go`.)
- **The Sentinel rejection-spike kick crashed the monitoring pass.** `ALL_MINER_TYPES` was read before it was assigned in the same function, so the first spike raised `UnboundLocalError`: the stratum kick never ran and the rest of that cycle was lost. It now finds the miner with `find_miner()`, like the zombie path. (`src/sentinel/SpiralSentinel.py`.)
- **Every orphan alert showed the block as orphaned *before* it was found.** `create_block_orphaned_embed` printed `found_at` verbatim -- it arrives as an ISO-8601 UTC string straight from the pool API -- while `orphaned_at` is passed by the caller as a datetime already converted to the configured display timezone and was rendered with `strftime`. So a DigiByte block found at `2026-09-18T23:32:07.750926Z` and orphaned twenty-two minutes later read as *Found At `2026-09-18T23:32:07.750926Z`* above *Orphaned At `2026-09-18 19:54:05`*: the same two events the journal records, four hours apart in the wrong direction on an `America/New_York` install. Nothing was wrong with the detection or the ordering behind it, which made the alert misleading rather than merely untidy -- an operator reading it has no way to tell a display artefact from a clock problem on the node. A new `fmt_display_time()` sits beside `get_display_tz()` and `local_now()` and normalizes an ISO-8601 string, a datetime or a Unix timestamp to the configured `display_timezone`; it treats naive and offset-less values as UTC because that is what the pool API emits, appends the zone abbreviation so the reading is unambiguous, and returns an unparseable value unchanged rather than raising inside an alert path. It pads or truncates the fractional seconds to six digits before parsing, which is not cosmetic: `datetime.fromisoformat` accepts only three or six fractional digits before Python 3.11, while `created` is a Go `time.Time` whose JSON encoding trims trailing zeros and so emits one to six. The obvious `fromisoformat` call therefore parsed the observed `.750926` but fell back to the raw UTC string for a one-, two-, four- or five-digit fraction, leaving the defect in place on whichever alerts happened to land on one -- invisible on the Docker image, which ships Python 3.14, and on any developer machine recent enough, but live on a bare-metal install running a distro `python3` of 3.9 or 3.10. Both fields now go through it. The identical `isinstance(str)` passthrough was also present on the `orphaned_at` branch, where every current caller passes a datetime and so never reached it. (`src/sentinel/SpiralSentinel.py`.)
- **`install.sh` never installed `ha_manager.py`.** A fresh install's Sentinel ran without HA failover, importing nothing and reporting nothing; only `upgrade.sh` copied the module. (`install.sh`.)
- **Saving settings could replace a device password with `***REDACTED***`.** `GET /api/config` returns that placeholder in place of every device password, and `POST /api/config` stored whatever it was given, so a round trip through the settings page locked Sentinel and the Automation page out of the miner. The placeholder is now replaced with the stored password for that device type and IP, or dropped. (`src/dashboard/dashboard.py`.)
- **A downed Bitcoin II node had no automated wallet recovery.** With the daemon unreachable, `upgrade.sh` attempts SQLite recovery only for the coins on its descriptor list, then falls back to `-salvagewallet`. Bitcoin II 31.1 is built on Bitcoin Core 31.1, and Core 30 removed BDB legacy wallets and that option: it was not on the list, so SQLite recovery was skipped, and the salvage fallback its daemon no longer accepts could not work either. Bitcoin Silver hits the same base with this release. Both are now on the list. (`upgrade.sh`.)
- **A solo Bitcoin Silver or Bitcoin Cash II install set up no wallet and no RPC password.** Both coins are offered in the installer's solo menu, but the solo path's wallet setup had no entry for either, so the install skipped the address prompt and also the RPC password generation in the same block. Multi-coin installs were not affected. Found on the first Linux test install of Bitcoin Silver:
  - the node's config was written with an empty `rpcpassword=`
  - the daemon then falls back to cookie authentication, so it rejected every RPC call the pool made, logging `incorrect password attempt` every two seconds
  - the installer waited on an RPC that could never answer, up to its 10-minute limit
  - no payout address was recorded

  Both coins now have solo entries with the same prompts and address checks as multi-coin mode, including option [2] to generate a wallet. A coin the solo menu offers without an entry now stops the install at the prompt, before anything is installed, instead of being skipped silently. As a backstop, each of the 30 daemon configs `install.sh` can write (15 native, 15 Docker) now runs `ensure_rpc_password` first, which generates the password with a warning if it is still empty. (`install.sh`.)
- **A generated wallet address was not reliably confirmed by the daemon before it was saved.** The pool already refuses to start with an address its daemon calls invalid, so no block could pay one, but the address generators should catch it first:
  - `spiralpool-wallet` asked the daemon whether the address belonged to the pool wallet. When the daemon gave no answer, which includes rejecting the address as invalid, it only warned ("daemon too old") and saved the address anyway.
  - The same check refused a manually entered address, the fallback when generation fails, because an address from the operator's own wallet is by design not in the node's wallet.
  - An address pulled from the HA primary skipped the checks entirely.
  - Four other paths that create addresses checked only the format: the post-sync fallback in `wait-for-node.sh` (primary coin and merge-mined aux chains), `pool-mode.sh`'s generate option for BCH2, BC2 and BTCS, and the dashboard's Generate wallet button.

  Every path now asks the daemon with `validateaddress`, and nothing is written unless it says the address is valid. A generated address must also be owned by the pool wallet, via `getaddressinfo` on that wallet, or `validateaddress`'s `ismine` on older daemons. An invalid address, a foreign one, or no answer stops without writing the config. A manual or HA-pulled address only has to be valid; if the daemon cannot be reached for one of those, the generator warns, and the pool's own startup check still applies. (`install.sh`, `scripts/linux/wait-for-node.sh`, `scripts/linux/pool-mode.sh`, `src/dashboard/dashboard.py`.)
- **Generating a wallet during install printed `tee: '': No such file or directory`.** `start_services` piped the wallet generator through `tee -a "$LOG_FILE"`, but the installer has no log file: `LOG_FILE` is set only inside the scripts it generates. The error appeared just before the sync screen, which then cleared it. The pipe is removed. (`install.sh`.)
- **The sync status shown before the live view left out Bitcoin Cash II and Bitcoin Silver.** A solo install of either printed an empty status box. Both are now listed. (`install.sh`.)
- **The install-time wallet backup could report success without a backup.** It counted a backup as made whenever the CLI's output lacked the words "error" or "unknown", so `command not found` passed, and Bitcoin Cash called `bitcoin-cli`, which exists only when Bitcoin is also installed. A backup now counts only when the CLI succeeds and the file is there, and Bitcoin Cash uses `bitcoin-cli-bch`. Separately, the generator's `sudo chown`/`chmod` on the backup directory now use `sudo -n`: the installer runs the generator as the pool user, who has no sudo rights for those commands, and `sudo` must not stop to ask for a password there. That prompt was not reproduced; the change removes the possibility. (`install.sh`.)
- **The pool wallet was unloaded after every reboot or daemon restart.** A wallet created over RPC stays loaded only until the daemon stops, unless it is also saved to the daemon's `settings.json`, and no path that creates one saved it. Found on the second test install: the VM rebooted five minutes after `pool-btcs` was created, and `listwallets` then returned `[]`. Mining was unaffected, since the coinbase pays the configured address and the keys stay on disk, but every wallet RPC against it failed, including the dashboard balance and `upgrade.sh`'s wallet backup. The health monitor's per-coin daemon check now loads `pool-<coin>` whenever it exists on disk and is not loaded, with `load_on_startup` so the daemon loads it itself from then on. Daemons too old for that argument get it loaded by name after each restart instead. A wallet that will not load is logged once, not every minute. This repairs existing installs as well as new ones. (`install.sh`.)
- **Upgrades never updated `wait-for-node.sh` or `health-monitor.sh`.** Both live in `/spiralpool/bin` and were written only by `install.sh`, so no fix to either reached an upgraded install. `upgrade.sh` now replaces `wait-for-node.sh` with the release copy, and `health-monitor.sh` with the script extracted from `install.sh`. It does so only when they differ, never with a copy that is empty, lacks the monitor's shebang and main check, or fails `bash -n`, and restarts a running health monitor. (`upgrade.sh`.)
- **A fresh install's dashboard stayed down after a reboot until the chain caught up.** The unit `install.sh` writes ordered the dashboard `After=spiralstratum.service`, and stratum stays "activating" while `wait-for-node.sh` waits for its node. Found on the second test install: after a reboot the dashboard did not start for four minutes, until Bitcoin Silver caught up. The health monitor, finding it down, blocked on its own restart attempt for the same four minutes and checked nothing else. On a chain that takes longer to catch up, the dashboard would stay dark that much longer. Upgraded installs were not affected: `upgrade.sh`'s template never had the ordering. The ordering is removed; the dashboard already handles the pool API being down. (`install.sh`.)
- **The health monitor restarted services that were still starting.** It counted anything not "active" as down. A unit still running its start steps reports "activating", so the monitor stopped healthy starts:
  - During install, `start_services` started the monitor before the dashboard and Sentinel, so its first check found them not yet started. It now starts last.
  - After a reboot on the second test install, with the ordering fix above in place, the monitor still restarted the dashboard 5 seconds before its post-start check finished.

  A unit that is "activating" is now left alone, for the dashboard and Sentinel, stratum (whose `wait-for-node.sh` can take minutes), and the coin daemons. (`install.sh`.)
- **gunicorn logged `Control server error: Permission denied: '/home/spiraluser'` on every dashboard start.** gunicorn 26 opens a control socket under `$HOME` by default, which `ProtectHome=yes` blocks. Nothing uses the socket; both dashboard units now pass `--no-control-socket`. Their post-start check also wrote `grep -q "\-\-certfile"`, which systemd warned about (`Ignoring unknown escape sequences`) on every reload; it is now `grep -q -- --certfile`. (`install.sh`, `scripts/linux/systemd/spiraldash.service`.)
- **The regtest harness could take down a real pool on the same machine.** Found before running `regtest-btcs.sh` on the test VM, which is also a live pool:
  - Every regtest config and script logged in to PostgreSQL as `spiralstratum`, the role `install.sh` creates for the pool with a generated password. When that login failed, the harness ran `ALTER USER spiralstratum WITH PASSWORD 'spiralstratum'`, which would lock the real pool out of its database the next time it connected.
  - Its start-up and clean-up `pkill "spiralpool.*config"` also matches the real `/spiralpool/bin/spiralstratum -config ...`.

  The harness now uses its own `spiralregtest` login, and reassigns a regtest database left by an older run to that login. It kills only its own checkout's pool binary. Nothing had been run against the VM. (`scripts/linux/regtest.sh`, `regtest-bc2.sh`, `regtest-dgb.sh`, `config/regtest/config-*-regtest.yaml`.)
- **The pool refused every Bitcoin Silver regtest address, so `regtest-btcs.sh` could never run.** `bitcoinsilverd -regtest` hands out `bcrt1q…` addresses. The config check accepted only mainnet prefixes (`B`, `3`, `bs1q`, `bs1p`), and the BTCS decoder only the `bs` bech32 prefix, so the pool exited at start-up with `BTCS address must start with 'B'…` and the harness reported `Pool stratum not listening after 30s`. The config check dates from v2.5.0. Both now also accept regtest addresses; the config check takes the regtest `m`, `n` and `2` prefixes the decoder already allowed. The P2WPKH output script is the same for either prefix. (`internal/coin/btcs.go`, `internal/config/v2.go`.)
- **A block candidate dropped before submission was never recorded, and logged an error every time.** In the multi-coin pool, the block row is written just before the daemon submit. A candidate rejected before that point — a stale race, a duplicate candidate, a moved chain tip, or a block that could not be rebuilt — skipped the insert, then still ran the status update that turns `submitting` into the final status. There was no row to update, so each one logged `Block status update affected no rows` and `Failed to update block status in database`, and the orphaned candidate appeared nowhere in the database. The single-coin pool has always inserted the row with its final status instead. The multi-coin pool now does the same, which also recovers a block whose pre-submit insert failed: it used to be submitted and then never recorded at all. Seen repeatedly in the BTCS regtest run, where fast blocks make duplicate candidates common. (`internal/pool/coinpool.go`.)
- **A pool could stop mining for days and say almost nothing.** A DigiByte install lost its block index to a reboot on 13 September and was still dead on the 17th. A corrupt block database makes the daemon exit in well under a second on every start, so the health monitor's three restarts an hour could never help; after them it logged `digibyted failed 3 times this hour - manual intervention required` once a minute into a file, which is the only place the failure appeared. The dashboard was up, the login banner showed the stratum in red without saying why, and Sentinel -- which would have alerted -- was not enabled on that machine. Three changes:
  - The health monitor now reads the daemon's own `debug.log`, recognises `Error opening block database` and `Corruption: checksum mismatch`, and says so instead of only counting failures.
  - It then hands off to a new `bin/daemon-reindex.sh`, which performs the recovery an operator would: it reads the daemon's `ExecStart`, adds `-reindex` through a systemd drop-in, starts the daemon, and removes the drop-in again -- a drop-in left in place would reindex on every future start and never finish. It runs **once** per daemon, recorded in a marker file it names in the log, because a database still unreadable after a reindex is a hardware question. On a pruned node a reindex re-downloads the chain, which the log says plainly. `touch $INSTALL_DIR/config/no-auto-reindex` switches it off. The script is root-owned and runs through one sudoers entry, since the pool user may invoke it but must not be able to edit it.
  - The login banner now lists each enabled coin daemon and its state, so `DGB ● failed` sits beside the stratum line, and `upgrade.sh` reports the state of every service and coin daemon before it starts -- naming a corrupt block database when it finds one -- so an operator upgrading a dead pool learns it at the moment they are certainly watching. (`install.sh`, `upgrade.sh`.)
- **An upgrade started while the pool was still coming up recorded it as stopped.** `stop_services` asked `systemctl is-active --quiet`, which is false for a unit in `activating` -- and `spiralstratum` waits for its coin daemon in `ExecStartPre`, so a node that is catching up sits there for minutes. The upgrade then left that service out of `SERVICES_WERE_RUNNING`, so its summary reported `spiralstratum  not started (wasn't running)` for a pool the operator had running, and a rollback would not have brought it back. Both the upgrade and the rollback now count `activating` and `reloading` as running. `spiralctl status` had the same blind spot and printed a red `Stopped` for a pool that was starting; it now says `Starting — waiting for a coin node to be ready`. Seen on a v2.7.1 to v3.0.0 upgrade of a DigiByte install. Two related annoyances in the same run: verification warned about `spiralsentinel` being inactive when it had been stopped before the upgrade and is not enabled -- it now says so plainly instead -- and the Stratum V2 opt-in prompt, the one place the upgrade waits for an answer, now states that it is waiting and that the operator should type y or n and press ENTER, because a run piped through `tee` can swallow the prompt line and look like a hang. (`upgrade.sh`.)
- **An upgrade halved a `dbcache` the operator had raised on purpose.** `rightsize_daemon_resources` caps dbcache per coin -- 4096 for DGB, BTC, BCH, LTC and DOGE -- to stop a box running several daemons from exhausting its RAM. It applied that cap flat, with no regard for how many daemons the box actually runs or how much memory it has, while `install.sh` auto-sizes those same five coins to as much as 8192 on a machine with the RAM for it. The installer and the upgrade therefore contradicted each other: a single-coin pool was written 8192 at install and cut to 4096 by its next upgrade. Found on a DigiByte pool with 15 GB of RAM running one daemon, where the backup taken before the v2.6.3 upgrade holds `dbcache=8192` and the config's mtime is two minutes later; the change was made with no backup file and no warning, since the backup-and-warn logic was added afterwards. The cap is now measured against what the box can afford -- 55% of total RAM divided among the coin configs that exist -- and lowers a value only when that share is genuinely smaller, so a five-coin machine is still protected while a single-coin machine keeps the headroom it was installed with. The upgrade also puts back what the old cap took: a `dbcache` sitting at exactly 4096 -- the value the cap wrote -- on one of those five long chains is restored to what `install.sh` would have written, where the budget allows. It restores only from that exact value, so an operator who deliberately chose 2048 keeps it, and it never raises a short chain. Two sizing errors found alongside it: the systemd memory ceiling was `dbcache + 3GB`, which does not account for the in-memory block index -- ~2.5GB on a 24M-block chain like DigiByte, measured at 9.7GB in use against an 11G cap with only 4GB of that being dbcache, so the daemon would have hit the ceiling mid-sync -- and is now `dbcache + 5GB`, applied to an upgraded box through a drop-in rather than by rewriting the operator's unit; and Bitcoin Silver had 1GB of headroom above the same 2048 cache that Bitcoin II, Bitcoin Cash II, Syscoin and eCash each get 2GB above, now matched. The function had no test coverage at all; it now has eight assertions covering the cap, the restore, the two values it must not touch, and the memory ceiling. (`install.sh`, `upgrade.sh`, `tests/test_upgrade_regression.sh`.)
- **The regtest harness could not be driven unattended, so no one ever ran the full coin sweep.** `regtest.sh` covers fifteen solo coins and six merge-mining pairs from one script and will download a missing coin daemon itself, but two things stopped it running from cron, CI or a `nohup` loop -- which is the only practical way to test every coin. It built the pool binary with a bare `go build`: `install.sh` installs Go to `/usr/local/go/bin` and exposes it through `/etc/profile.d/go.sh`, which only a login shell reads, so an automated run died with `go: command not found` *after* it had already downloaded and installed the coin's daemon. And its PostgreSQL step refused outright when `[[ ! -t 0 ]]`, printing the commands to run by hand -- correct on a box that prompts for sudo, but a pool user with passwordless sudo needs no terminal, and the check never tried. The harness now falls back to `/usr/local/go/bin` when `go` is not on `PATH` and fails with a clear message if it still cannot find it, and the database step tries `sudo -n` first, keeping the by-hand guidance for a box that really does prompt. Found while running the sweep across all fifteen coins on a live VM: nine coins failed in seconds on the first, then all of them on the second, before either was fixed. (`scripts/linux/regtest.sh`.)
- **The regtest harness's maturity wait could never finish early, so every coin burned twenty idle minutes.** Step 8b waits for the pool's own blocks to pass maturity, then tests `SELECT COUNT(*) ... WHERE status = 'confirmed'` against the number of blocks mined. A block does not sit in `confirmed`: `executePendingPayments` reads `GetConfirmedBlocks` and marks each one `paid` within the same thirty-second payment cycle, which the harness's own log line documents -- "confirmed reads 0 almost every time". The number it compared was therefore almost always zero, the early exit never fired, and the loop always ran to `MATURITY_WAIT_SECS`: 1200 seconds per coin, roughly five hours across a full sweep, inside a forty-five minute per-coin budget that a slower chain could then overrun and be recorded as a timeout it did not deserve. The check now counts `status IN ('confirmed', 'paid')`, which holds in one direction only and that is the direction needed -- the status guard refuses `pending -> paid` from the processor, so a paid block has passed maturity by definition. Measured on Bitcoin Silver: step 8b now exits in 91 seconds reading `confirmed=0, paid=13`, the exact state the old condition could not see. The error-path coin list also advertised Syscoin as a solo coin, which `setup_coin` refuses outright -- it is merge-mining only, and both usage blocks now say so. (`scripts/linux/regtest.sh`.)
- **Two coins in the regtest harness could not install their own daemon.** The harness downloads a missing coin daemon itself, from a URL and an expected archive directory recorded per coin, and neither was right for Bitcoin Cash II: the URL asked for `bitcoincashII-v27.0.2-linux-x86_64.tar.gz` where the release publishes `bitcoincashII-27.0.2-linux64.tar.gz` -- a version prefix that is not there and an architecture suffix that upstream does not use -- so the download 404'd instantly and the run ended at the preflight check with "build from source", which was never necessary. Its archive directory was wrong too (`bitcoincashII-27.0.2`, actually `bitcoincashII-27.0.2-linux64`), so a corrected URL alone would still have found no binaries to install. Catcoin declared `Catcoin-Linux`, but that zip has no top-level directory at all; it only installed because a later fallback looks in the working directory, and the declaration is now `.`, which is what the archive actually is. `install.sh` was checked and has the correct Bitcoin Cash II URL, so no real deployment was affected -- this was the test harness alone. Every one of the fifteen coin archives was then fetched and its top-level directory compared against what the harness expects: all fifteen URLs resolve and all fifteen directories now match. (`scripts/linux/regtest.sh`.)
- **A Bitcoin Cash II pool could not fetch a single block template.** `BitcoinCashIICoin.GBTRules` returned no rules, on the reasoning that BCH2 follows Bitcoin Cash consensus and so needs no SegWit rule. The daemon disagrees: Bitcoin Cash II Core is built on Bitcoin Core 27, and v27.0.2 answers `getblocktemplate` with error -8 -- "getblocktemplate must be called with the segwit rule set" -- to an empty rules list, to `{}`, and to no parameters at all. Every template request therefore failed, the job manager had nothing to build a job from, and every miner pointed at a BCH2 pool would have idled forever. The rule is now declared, which is what the daemon asks for; whether the coinbase carries a witness commitment is a separate decision and `SupportsSegWit` is left alone until a regtest block proves which way that one goes. The regtest harness carried the identical assumption in `GBT_RULES` for the same coin, so it could not have caught this until its own daemon install was repaired first. Confirmed against a live regtest daemon, all four call shapes. (`internal/coin/bch2.go`, `scripts/linux/regtest.sh`.)
- **The pool rejected every eCash address its own daemon generates on a test network.** `ECashCoin.decodeCashAddr` stripped only the mainnet `ecash:` prefix. eCash uses a different CashAddr prefix per network -- `ectest:` and `ecregtest:` -- and the prefix is fed into the checksum, so an address from a testnet or regtest node could neither be stripped nor verified: the pool refused it with "invalid pool address 'ecregtest:qq2v…': invalid address length: 52 - blocks would be rejected" and the coin never started. Bitcoin Cash already handled both of its prefixes this way; eCash was missed. Mainnet was unaffected, so this cost nothing in production and everything in testing -- eCash could not be validated end to end at all. Both test prefixes are now recognised and checksummed against the prefix the address actually carries. (`internal/coin/ecash.go`.)
- **The regtest harness tested whatever binary happened to be lying around.** It built the pool only `if [[ ! -f "$POOL_BINARY" ]]` -- if a binary existed, from any point in the past, that was what every later run exercised. On the test box the binary under test was a day old, so fixes made that day were absent from the runs that were supposed to prove them: a change could be verified green while the code that passed was the code from before the change. It now builds on every run; Go's build cache makes a no-op build about a second. (`scripts/linux/regtest.sh`.)
- **Bitcoin Cash shared Bitcoin's data directory in regtest.** `bitcoind-bch` is Bitcoin Cash Node's `bitcoind` behind a symlink, so it defaulted to `~/.bitcoin` and opened Bitcoin Core's chainstate, dying at startup with "Error initializing block database. Please restart with -reindex" on any box where Bitcoin had run first. The cleanup step meanwhile reset `~/.bitcoin-bch`, a directory the daemon never used, so nothing was ever actually cleaned. Fractal Bitcoin, eCash, Bitcoin Cash II and Bitcoin Silver already carry this override -- with a comment reading "Without this, FBTC would collide with BTC's data directory" -- and Bitcoin Cash was simply left out. The daemon-restart path had the same hole for three coins and now overrides the directory for all of them. (`scripts/linux/regtest.sh`.)
- **eCash could not fetch a block template, and could not create a wallet.** Two separate faults in the same coin. Bitcoin ABC kept the "not connected" guard on `getblocktemplate` that Bitcoin Core skips on regtest, so with no peer the daemon answered error -9 and the pool had nothing to build a job from; eCash is now in the list of coins the harness gives a second, connected daemon. And every eCash run died at wallet creation with "Invalid amount for -fallbackfee=<amount>: '0.0001'", because XEC carries two decimal places and that amount does not exist on the network -- 0.01 is the smallest it accepts, and the fee is now chosen per coin. Neither touched production: `fallbackfee` appears in no shipped config, and a real node has peers. (`scripts/linux/regtest.sh`.)
- **PepeCoin's regtest config pointed the pool at the wrong daemon port.** The harness starts pepecoind with RPC on 18572; `config-pep-regtest.yaml` told the pool 18570. The pool dialled a port nothing was listening on, logged "Pool pending (node not ready)", and never opened its stratum port, which read as a pool startup failure rather than a config typo. Every other coin's config agrees with the harness; PepeCoin was the only one that did not. (`config/regtest/config-pep-regtest.yaml`.)
- **The regtest harness overwrote Bitcoin Core with eCash's daemon.** Installing a missing coin daemon falls through to a generic step that copies the archive's `bin/*` into `/usr/local/bin`. Bitcoin ABC ships its binaries under the ordinary Bitcoin names, so installing eCash replaced `/usr/local/bin/bitcoind` -- Bitcoin Core v31.1.0 -- with Bitcoin ABC v0.33.12, along with `bitcoin-cli` and `bitcoin-tx`. The Bitcoin run started eight seconds later against eCash's daemon and died with `createwallet` error -4, which read as a second, unrelated failure and was not. eCash now installs to `/usr/local/lib/ecash` with `ecashd` and `ecash-cli` symlinks, the pattern Bitcoin Cash, Bitcoin Cash II, Bitcoin II and Fractal Bitcoin already use here and that `install.sh` uses in production, so no deployment was affected. (`scripts/linux/regtest.sh`.)
- **A Docker deployment served `/metrics` to anyone who could reach the host.** The stack is built for Bearer auth on that endpoint and wires it end to end: `prometheus.yml` scrapes with `bearer_token_file: /spiralpool/data/.metrics_token`, the stratum entrypoint writes `$SPIRAL_METRICS_TOKEN` into that file at mode 600, and `config.docker.template` sets `authToken` from the same variable. The one broken link was `generate-secrets.sh`: its `add_if_missing` helper only acts on a variable that is *absent*, and `SPIRAL_METRICS_TOKEN` ships in `.env.example` present but empty -- so it was skipped, the entrypoint wrote an empty token file, and in `prometheus.go` the check is guarded by `if m.cfg.AuthToken != ""`, which means an empty token disables authentication rather than denying access. `docker-compose.yml` publishes `9100:9100` on every interface, and a published Docker port is not filtered by `ufw`, so the firewall rule the native installer writes for that port -- localhost and the LAN only -- would not have applied either. The same software installed natively sets a token and firewalls the port; the two paths disagreed about the same endpoint. `generate-secrets.sh` now fills a variable that is present-but-empty as well as one that is missing, and generates the metrics token with the other secrets; re-running it leaves an existing token alone. Found while checking a Docker install on a live VM, where `docker compose config` and the generated `.env` were verified before and after. `DASHBOARD_API_KEY` is deliberately left empty: it gates optional programmatic access only, and session authentication is controlled separately by `DASHBOARD_AUTH_ENABLED`. (`docker/generate-secrets.sh`.)
- **The upgrade's sandboxed build never ran; every upgrade compiled as root.** `deploy_stratum` builds the stratum binary as the unprivileged pool user and falls back to a root build if that fails. Three separate faults sat on top of one another, and any one of them alone was enough to send every run down the fallback. `install.sh` puts Go in `/usr/local/go/bin` and the upgrade adds it to its own `PATH`, but `sudo` replaces `PATH` with `secure_path`, so the build got `go: command not found`. Behind that, the release is normally extracted into the operator's home directory, which is mode 750 and which the pool user cannot traverse -- the `chown -R` the function performed could not help, because the block is the parent directory, not the files inside it (`cd: Permission denied`, reproducible with `sudo -u spiraluser ls /home/<operator>`). And behind that, the build output was written into a root-owned `mktemp` directory the pool user could not write. The warning it printed, `Sandboxed build failed, trying as root...`, read as an occasional hiccup rather than what it was: the sandbox had never once run on any install where the source sits in a home directory, and the Go toolchain plus every module in the dependency graph compiled with full privileges. The source is now staged into a directory both users can reach, Go is resolved to an absolute path before privileges are dropped, and the binary is written inside that staging directory -- for the stratum build, `spiralctl`, and the sentinel `pip` refresh, which read its requirements file straight out of the operator's home and had been failing the same way (soft-fail, so it silently kept the old venv). Verified on a live v3.0.0 upgrade: every `compile` process runs as `spiraluser`, the module cache lands in that account's home, and no fallback warning is printed. The `chown -R` of the operator's source tree is gone with it -- it was taking ownership of a directory in the operator's home to no purpose, since the source extracts world-readable and Go needs only to read it. In the same pass: rewriting a daemon config left it mode `0600`, while `install.sh` creates these files `0640`, so an upgrade silently tightened a file the operator had not asked it to touch and the two scripts then disagreed about the same path; the rewrite now restores `0640`. (`upgrade.sh`.)
- **The regtest daemon-down checks read the pool log by line offset and missed what it wrote.** Both checks counted the log's lines, then read everything past that count. On a BTCS run the pool reacted to the daemon being stopped exactly as intended -- `RPC call failed after all retries`, `connect: connection refused`, ZMQ degraded, two Sentinel alerts, 87 matching lines in all -- and both checks reported nothing, scoring the step `PARTIAL (3/4)`. They now mark the log by size and read from that byte, which cannot drift, and the failure message says how many new lines it examined and from where. (`scripts/linux/regtest.sh`, `regtest-bc2.sh`, `regtest-dgb.sh`.)
- **Starting a regtest run while one was still going wrecked both.** The harness sent the old pool a SIGTERM, waited two seconds and carried on. The pool shuts down gracefully and takes longer than that, and until it exits it still holds its block WAL lock and still writes to the database. The new run could not create its coin pool ("failed to acquire WAL file lock"), gave up after 30 seconds, and in the meantime had truncated the block tables under the run that was still mining -- which made that run log a status-update failure for a block whose row had just been deleted. The harness now waits for the old pool to exit, up to 15 seconds, and forces it down after that. (`scripts/linux/regtest.sh`.)
- **The regtest payment check looked for a state the pool had already left.** Checks 5 and 6 of the payment pipeline want a block in `confirmed` so they can test the `confirmed` -> `paid` transition and that `paid` is terminal. On regtest a block matures in seconds, so by the time the check runs the pool has carried it to `paid` itself, and both checks were skipped with "No confirmed block at height 2" -- reported as `PARTIAL (6/8)` on a run where the pipeline had in fact worked end to end (2,428 blocks paid). The check now accepts a block the pool has already paid, and check 6 still proves `paid` cannot go back to `pending`. (`scripts/linux/regtest.sh`, `regtest-bc2.sh`, `regtest-dgb.sh`.)
- **The regtest V2 protocol test could not pass on a fast chain.** The test miner mines one job to completion, but cpuminer keeps finding regtest blocks while it works, and each block replaces the pool's jobs. In the BTCS run the pool sent 31 newer jobs during the 6 seconds the V2 miner spent on its share, so the job was long gone from the channel's history and the share came back `invalid-job-id`. The harness now pauses cpuminer for the V2 test, waits for the tip to stop moving before starting it, and resumes it afterwards. Without that wait a block submitted in the moment before the pause lands a second later and invalidates the job the V2 miner has just been given, which came back as `stale-share`. (`scripts/linux/regtest.sh`.)
- **A V2 job could reach a miner before the channel it names.** A new channel joined its session before `OpenMiningChannel.Success` was written, so a block arriving in that window sent a job for a channel the client had not been told about. The channel's job lock is now held until the Success is written. (`internal/stratum/v2/server.go`.)
- **One undecodable theme file took down the entire theme list.** `/api/themes` reads every `static/themes/*.json` with a bare `open(theme_file, 'r')`, so each file is decoded with the interpreter's locale encoding rather than the UTF-8 it is actually written in. `chrome-warfare.json` carries an em dash in a CSS comment, and on any host whose locale encoding is not UTF-8 that byte raises `UnicodeDecodeError` — which is neither a `json.JSONDecodeError` nor an `IOError`, so it walked straight past the `except` written to let one bad theme be skipped, escaped the route, and returned 500 for the whole list. The operator gets no themes at all instead of the twenty-five that decode perfectly well, and the handler that was supposed to contain the damage is the reason it spread. `/api/themes/<theme_id>` had the identical pair of defects. Both now open the files as UTF-8, which is what they are, and both catch `UnicodeDecodeError` alongside the errors they already caught, so a genuinely malformed theme is skipped rather than fatal. Caught by `tests/test_dashboard_routes_authenticated.py`, which drives every authenticated route and asserts none raises — the pool's own Linux hosts run a UTF-8 locale and never hit it, so nothing else would have. (`src/dashboard/dashboard.py`.)
- **`upgrade.sh --check` ignored `--local` and asked GitHub instead, so the pre-upgrade check on an untagged release answered “nothing to upgrade”.** `--check` is the one command an operator runs *before* upgrading, to find out what is about to happen; it also has the most reassuring possible failure mode. It exits near the top of `main()` into `check_for_updates`, which only ever queried the GitHub releases API — `--local` was accepted and silently discarded, and `PROJECT_ROOT` is not even populated on that path, because `detect_source_directory` does not run until hundreds of lines later. Against a release that is not tagged yet, the newest thing on GitHub is the version already installed, so `--full --local --check` on a 2.7.1 host with the 3.0.0 tree unpacked beside it returned `"latest_version": "2.7.1", "update_available": false` — confident, well-formed and wrong, from the command whose entire job is to say otherwise.
