The real upgrade was never affected: it has its own `--local` branch reading `$PROJECT_ROOT/VERSION`, so `sudo ./upgrade.sh --local` always targeted 3.0.0 correctly — the defect was confined to the preflight. `check_for_updates` now resolves the source tree and reads its `VERSION` when `--local` is given, and keeps the GitHub query as the default so Sentinel's polling is unchanged. `detect_source_directory` logs on stdout, so it is called with stdout redirected to stderr: without that the `[INFO] Source directory:` line lands in the middle of the JSON and breaks the only consumer it has. Verified by reverting the fix, which fails three of the four new assertions. (`upgrade.sh`, `tests/test_upgrade_regression.sh`.)
- **Every Bitcoin Cash fallback peer pointed at a port nothing was listening on.** All fifteen `addnode` entries for BCH — in `install.sh`'s config template, again in `upgrade.sh`'s `COIN_PEERS`, and a third time in the config `scripts/linux/pool-mode.sh` writes when an operator switches the pool to a different coin — used `:8433`. That is Spiral Pool's own remapped BCH listen port, chosen so a multi-coin box does not collide with Bitcoin on 8333; it is not what other people's nodes run. The table's own comment says so in as many words: "Port numbers are the PUBLIC network ports (what other nodes run), not our remapped ports". Probed against the live network, **0 of 15 answered on 8433 and 12 of the same 15 answered on 8333**, so the entire BCH fallback list had never once connected and never could. It went unnoticed because peers are a third-line backup: a node finds the network through DNS seeds first and a compiled-in fixed-seed list second, both of which work for BCH (six of its seven DNS seeds resolve), so nothing ever looked wrong. Corrected to `:8333` in all three files, leaving each script's `port=8433` listen directive and its matching firewall rule alone, which were always right. Re-probed after the change: 12 of 15 reachable. The three that answer on neither port are left in place rather than pruned — a single probe says a node is down now, not that it is gone. Worth stating plainly that **no static guard could have caught this**: both files agreed with each other, the syntax was valid, and every existing version- and drift-check passed. Only touching the network exposed it. A new guard in `tests/test_script_integrity.sh` covers the part that is checkable — that neither `upgrade.sh` nor `pool-mode.sh` ever writes a peer `install.sh` does not also ship, so a fresh install, an upgraded one and a mode-switched one cannot end up with different sets — and it says in its own comment what it cannot see. The guard caught a real omission on its first run: the first pass at this fix corrected `install.sh` and `upgrade.sh` and missed the third copy entirely, because a grep for the peers found the two files anyone would think to look in. Against that half-fixed tree the extended guard fails with all fifteen `pool-mode.sh` peers named. A wrong port in every copy at once stays invisible to it; a wrong port in one copy no longer does. **What an existing BCH pool sees:** `ensure_daemon_peer_config` is add-only by design, so the upgrade writes all fifteen corrected `:8333` entries but leaves the old `:8433` ones in place — the config ends up holding both. The stale lines are inert and the daemon simply never connects to them; they are left rather than deleted because removing lines from an operator's config is the riskier operation, and the same caution already governs `coin-upgrade.sh`'s DigiByte prune path. They can be removed by hand at any time, and a fresh install never has them. (`install.sh`, `upgrade.sh`, `scripts/linux/pool-mode.sh`, `tests/test_script_integrity.sh`.)

- **`spiralctl config validate` reported on a Sentinel config file the service had never read, and `spiralctl config set` wrote to it.** Sentinel keeps its config in `~/.spiralsentinel/config.json` and falls back to `$INSTALL_DIR/config/sentinel/config.json` when it cannot use the home directory. systemd's `ProtectHome=yes` is exactly that case, so the daemon uses the install copy — while `spiralctl`, run by the operator outside the sandbox, found the home copy first and used that. On the production pool the two files were 6815 bytes dated 18 September and 6872 bytes dated 19 September: not a symlink, not the same file, and both holding a `discord_webhook_url`. Every check therefore answered about whichever copy it happened to select, and a green `✓ Sentinel config valid` proved nothing about the running service; worse, `config set` edited the copy the service does not read, so the change reported success and never took effect. **Resolving the path again in each tool cannot fix this**, which is why it survived: each tool resolves it correctly *for itself*, and the answer legitimately differs between a sandboxed daemon and an operator's shell. The daemon now records the file it actually opened in `$INSTALL_DIR/data/sentinel-config-path`, and the readers consult that before falling back to their own order, so a pool whose Sentinel has not been restarted since the upgrade behaves exactly as before. Only the daemon publishes: a `--status`, `--test` or `--reload` run resolves the path as the invoking user, and publishing that would aim every other tool at the wrong file — the same fault one level down. `spiralctl` had **nine** copies of the resolution, not the five a first grep found, one of them hardcoding a literal `/home/spiraluser`, and one walking every systemd unit to locate the pool user's home, which also resolves to the copy the daemon is not using. All nine now call one resolver. The dashboard needed no change: it hit this in an earlier release and already orders the install path first, and that fix was never carried across to `spiralctl`. `scripts/reset-hashrate-baseline.py` had the same defect against `state.json` and is fixed the same way. A guard fails the suite if the daemon stops publishing the path, if it is published from anywhere but the daemon branch, or if `spiralctl` goes back to building the path itself; mutation-checked by reintroducing a second literal path, which it names. (`src/sentinel/SpiralSentinel.py`, `scripts/spiralctl.sh`, `scripts/reset-hashrate-baseline.py`, `tests/test_script_integrity.sh`.)

- **`multi_port.tls_port` loaded a certificate and opened no listener.** The multi-coin port takes an optional `tls_port`, and the config reached the scheduler intact -- validation passed it, the coordinator read it, the certificate was loaded. What never happened was the listen: `startMultiPort` built the port's `StratumConfig` by copying stratum settings off the first enabled coin and left `TLS.ListenTLS` empty, and an empty `ListenTLS` is exactly how the server is told *not* to serve TLS. So an operator who set `tls_port: 3334` got a running pool, a valid certificate, no warning anywhere, and nothing accepting connections on 3334; miners pointed at it simply failed to connect, with the pool reporting itself healthy. The config building is now a pure function, `buildMultiPortStratumConfig`, so it can be tested without standing a pool up, and it fills in the listen address. It also refuses to start when `tls_port` is set and the coin it inherits from has no `certFile`/`keyFile` to serve, which previously produced the same silent nothing. Proved with a real handshake rather than a config assertion: a test client completes a TLS handshake against the port when `ListenTLS` is set, and finds nothing listening when it is empty. (`internal/pool/coordinator.go`, `internal/pool/multiport_tls_test.go`, `internal/stratum/server_multiport_tls_test.go`.)
- **`spiralctl miner group` and `miner tag` wrote to a file nothing reads.** Both stored a flat `{ip: name}` map in the Sentinel config. The dashboard keeps fleet groups and tags in `dashboard/data/miner_groups.json` and `miner_tags.json` in its own schema, and Sentinel's `group_offline`/`group_online` alerts read *that* store. So grouping miners from the command line appeared to work -- the command reported success, `group list` read back what it had written -- while the dashboard showed no groups and the group alerts had nothing to fire on. Both subcommands now write the dashboard's store in the dashboard's schema, with its validation (one group per miner, name rules, the 500/20 caps), writing through a `.tmp` file with mode 0644 and `os.replace` so a concurrent dashboard read never sees a partial file, and as the pool user when invoked as root. Proved by running the shipped logic against temp files and then re-running the *old* logic through the same assertions, which fails them. (`scripts/spiralctl.sh`.)
- **`spiralctl node start|stop|restart all` silently skipped the eCash daemon.** The `all` branch of `get_daemons` listed fourteen of the fifteen daemons; `ecashd` was absent, so every bulk node operation passed over eCash without naming it or failing. Nothing downstream noticed, because the loop iterates the list it is given. `ecashd` is restored to the list, and the `spiralctl node` usage string -- which had also lost `dgb-scrypt` and `xec` -- now names all sixteen coin arguments. A cross-check now matches the `all` list against the reachable daemons exactly. (`scripts/spiralctl.sh`.)
- **A multi-coin port would accept coins on different mining algorithms and rotate a miner onto work it cannot do.** `multi_port.coins` is validated for weights, port conflicts and symbol spelling, but never for algorithm, so a config mixing `DGB` (SHA-256d) with `DGB-SCRYPT` or `LTC` passed cleanly. Miner hardware is algorithm-specific: a SHA-256d ASIC rotated onto a scrypt coin is handed work it can never solve and its entire share submission is rejected until the rotation moves on. Validation now resolves each symbol through the coin registry and rejects a mix, naming the algorithms and the coins on each side; a symbol the registry does not know (a coin added at runtime) is skipped rather than rejected. Proved fails-before/passes-after by reverting the check and watching the test fail. (`internal/config/v2.go`, `internal/config/multiport_algo_test.go`.)
- **The documented off switch for the SimpleSwap link did not exist, in any form.** `NOSEC.md`, `docs/setup/OPERATIONS.md` and `docs/reference/SENTINEL.md` all described a `/etc/spiralpool/simpleswap.conf` holding `SIMPLESWAP_ENABLED`, chmod 600, written by an installer prompt, and gave the operator `sed` commands to flip it. None of it was real: `install.sh` contained no mention of SimpleSwap, nothing anywhere read `SIMPLESWAP_ENABLED`, and `_build_simpleswap_surge_field()` was appended to **every** `sats_surge` alert unconditionally. An operator who did not want a third-party exchange link in their alerts could follow the documentation exactly, create the file, set it to `false`, restart Sentinel, and keep receiving the link -- with no error to say why. The only real way to stop it was to mute the surge alert and lose the notification too. The switch now exists as `simpleswap_enabled` in the Sentinel config, defaulting to `true` so nothing changes for an existing install and for a config file written before the key existed; it is a toggle in the installer's Sentinel menu, and `spiralctl config set simpleswap on|off` / `config get simpleswap` read and write it. Turning it off drops the link and leaves the surge alert firing. The documentation is rewritten to describe what is there. (`src/sentinel/SpiralSentinel.py`, `install.sh`, `scripts/spiralctl.sh`, `tests/test_simpleswap_toggle.py`.)

- **Seven Sentinel config keys were written by the installer, read by Sentinel, and declared nowhere.** `install.sh` writes `alerts_enabled`, `health_monitoring_enabled`, `update_check_enabled`, `update_check_interval`, `auto_update_mode`, `hashrate_crash_enabled` and `high_odds_enabled` into every generated config, and Sentinel reads all seven -- but none appeared in `DEFAULT_CONFIG`, the structure that is supposed to hold and document the defaults. Behaviour was never wrong: each is read as `CONFIG.get(key, <default>)`, so a config file lacking one behaves correctly. What was wrong is that the defaults existed only at the call sites, so the master switch for every alert but `block_found` had no declared default anywhere a reader would look. All seven are now declared, with values matching their call sites exactly, and a guard fails the suite if the installer ever writes an eighth without one. (`src/sentinel/SpiralSentinel.py`, `tests/test_spiralctl_docs_match.py`.)
- **The Windows installer contradicted itself about how much disk a coin needs.** Its `$CoinConfig` table said Bitcoin Cash II needs 20 GB and Bitcoin Silver 15 GB; the menu printed on the screen the operator is looking at while choosing said 15 GB and 8 GB, as did its own `--help`, `WINDOWS_GUIDE.md`, `OPERATIONS.md` and `CLOUD_OPERATIONS.md`. Two further disagreements sat between the documents: `OPERATIONS.md` alone said Bitcoin II needs 5 GB against 10 GB in four other places, and it and `CLOUD_OPERATIONS.md` said Myriad 6 GB against 8 GB in the installer and the Windows guide. Each file is internally valid and an operator reads exactly one of them before provisioning a disk, so nothing surfaced it. All four outliers are corrected to the figure the other sources agree on. `install.sh` is deliberately untouched: it states raw chain size and adds its own headroom to compute `REQUIRED_GB`, the threshold its preflight enforces -- a different quantity, and one validated by real installs, so aligning it would mean changing the number that gates installation. (`install-windows.ps1`, `docs/setup/OPERATIONS.md`, `docs/setup/CLOUD_OPERATIONS.md`, `tests/test_coin_storage_consistency.py`.)

- **Every published disk figure was stale, four of them badly enough to fail an install.** Measured on 2026-09-21 against Blockchair's `blockchain_size` statistic, and the DigiByte project's node guide for DGB: Bitcoin is ~780 GB and was published as 600, Litecoin ~240 against 150, Dogecoin ~190 against 80, and **eCash ~160 GB against 20 -- understated eightfold**. Bitcoin Cash (~220 against 250) and DigiByte (~40 against 80) were overstated, which is merely wrong rather than harmful. This is not only a documentation defect: `install.sh` sizes its preflight from the same numbers, so `REQUIRED_GB` demanded 25 GB for a 160 GB chain and passed a disk that cannot hold it -- the operator finds out hours or days into the sync, when it runs out. The preflight is raised for BTC (700 → 850), LTC (120 → 270), DOGE (80 → 210) and XEC (25 → 180); it is deliberately **not** lowered for BCH or DGB, whose existing thresholds already exceed the measured chain, because loosening a gate on the strength of an external figure is the one change that can only do harm. The published figures are corrected in all six surfaces, and `docs/setup/OPERATIONS.md` now records what was measured, when, and from where -- and says plainly that **BCH2, BC2, BTCS, NMC, SYS, XMY, FBTC, PEP and CAT are unverified estimates**, because no public size statistic exists for those chains and pretending otherwise is how the last set of numbers survived this long. Note that the cloud egress block in `install.sh` had said DGB ~45 GB all along, against ~80 GB in the storage tables: the one place that was close to right disagreed with every other, and nothing compared them. (`install.sh`, `install-windows.ps1`, `docs/setup/OPERATIONS.md`, `CLOUD_OPERATIONS.md`, `WINDOWS_GUIDE.md`, `DOCKER_GUIDE.md`.)
- **Twelve documented Sentinel settings were declared nowhere, and one was documented as the wrong type.** `docs/reference/SENTINEL.md` carries 118 rows of `key | type | default | description`, and nothing compared them to `DEFAULT_CONFIG`. Twelve keys -- `hostname_override`, `thermal_shutdown_enabled`, `thermal_shutdown_sustained_sec`, `hw_error_rate_threshold`, `expected_fleet_ths_disabled`, `price_crash_enabled`, `price_crash_pct`, `payout_check_interval`, `missing_payout_days`, `revenue_decline_pct`, `zmq_stale_threshold` and `ha_replication_lag_threshold` -- were read with an inline default at one call site each and absent from the structure that is supposed to document them; all twelve are now declared, at the values their call sites already used. The type error is the sharp one: `smtp_to` was documented as a **list** defaulting to `[]`, and Sentinel reads it as a comma-separated **string** (`CONFIG.get("smtp_to", "").strip()`). An operator following that table writes a JSON array, and `.strip()` on a list raises `AttributeError` at module scope -- the daemon does not start. The same document says "comma-separated `smtp_to`" forty lines further down, so it contradicted itself. A guard now resolves all 118 rows against `DEFAULT_CONFIG`, both that the key exists and that the stated default is the one the code uses, mutation-checked by changing the documented disk-warning threshold from 85 to 90 and watching it fail. (`src/sentinel/SpiralSentinel.py`, `docs/reference/SENTINEL.md`, `tests/test_doc_facts.py`.)

- **Fractal Bitcoin was budgeted at 10 GB against an actual ~3.1 TB — three hundred times under.** Fractal's own node documentation asks for "2TB (Based on the current scale, the data is growing by approximately 2TB per year. Please plan for expansion accordingly.)", but that is a recommendation the chain has outgrown, and taking it as the chain size would repeat the mistake being fixed. Derived from the chain instead: 2,139,820 blocks at a mean 1.45 MB across the full history (mempool.fractalbitcoin.io `sizes-weights`) is **~3.1 TB**. Two independent checks on that derivation: 30-second blocks at that size yield ~1.5 TB/year, agreeing with the "~2TB per year" the same document states, and height × 30s implies 743 days against 742 actually elapsed since launch. Spiral Pool published 10 GB in six places and sized its preflight from the same number, so enabling FBTC on any ordinary disk passed every check and then filled the volume — taking every other coin's daemon down with it, since they share it. The figure is corrected everywhere, the preflight now demands 3,300 GB for an unpruned FBTC, and `docs/setup/OPERATIONS.md` says plainly that FBTC should be pruned unless the disk is really there: a pruned node caps near 5 GB and merge mining works fine on one, because the pool needs templates and submission, not history. (`install.sh`, `install-windows.ps1`, `config/coins.manifest.yaml`, `docs/setup/OPERATIONS.md`, `CLOUD_OPERATIONS.md`, `WINDOWS_GUIDE.md`.)
- **The disk preflight ignored pruning entirely.** `validate_docker_disk_requirements` summed full-chain sizes whether or not the operator had chosen pruning, which `prune=5000` caps at roughly 5 GB per coin. The error was invisible while the figures were small and became absurd as soon as they were corrected: a pruned Fractal Bitcoin install would have been told it needed 2,100 GB for a chain that would occupy five. The sum is now recomputed per enabled coin when `PRUNE_ENABLED` is set — 6 GB each, plus Syscoin's NEVM sidecar state, which is not pruned with the UTXO chain. Exercised by extracting the real arithmetic and running it both ways: FBTC alone asks 2,110 GB unpruned and 16 GB pruned; all fifteen coins pruned ask 125 GB. (`install.sh`.)
- **Every morning a security update landed, the post-update restart bounced Sentinel, the dashboard and the stratum for packages none of them load.** `apt-post-update-restart.sh` runs after `apt-daily-upgrade` and restarted all three pool services whenever `/var/log/dpkg.log` showed a package upgraded that day whose name merely *contained* `python3`, `openssl`, `zlib` and the like. On a live DigiByte pool that meant restarts on consecutive mornings for `python3-jwt`/`python3-requests` (system Python modules a venv Sentinel never imports) and `libevent-openssl` (a libevent add-on nothing restarted here links), each one a fresh "SENTINEL ACTIVATED" in Discord and a stratum restart; `-dev` header packages qualified too. A service is now restarted only when one of its processes, gunicorn worker included, still has a shared library mapped from a file the upgrade replaced -- the test `needrestart` uses -- and the log names the libraries. Pure-Python modules are not mapped, so an upgrade of one alone now waits for the next reboot. The same log showed the stratum restart failing on four of five runs with no reason, because every `systemctl restart` sent its stderr to `/dev/null`; its message is now logged. That stratum failure itself is not yet diagnosed. (`scripts/linux/apt-post-update-restart.sh`.)

### Added — per-coin storage figures and on-demand measurement
- **Each coin's storage figure now lives in `config/coins.manifest.yaml` with its provenance, and `spiralctl coin storage` measures the real thing.** The manifest calls itself the canonical source for coin definitions but held no size, so six surfaces each carried their own copy and the guard added earlier could only prove they agreed with each other — which they did, while all being wrong. Each coin now declares `chain_gb` with `measured` and `source`, and a coin that declares neither fails the suite: **BCH2, BC2, BTCS, NMC, SYS, XMY, PEP and CAT are marked `source: estimate`**, because no public size statistic exists for those chains and a number with no provenance is indistinguishable from a guess. For the rest, `spiralctl coin storage` reads `size_on_disk` from each running daemon and prints it beside the published figure, flagging any chain that has outgrown it — pruned nodes identified and excluded, stopped daemons reported rather than skipped, so a missing row never reads as a passing one. That is the part no static check could do: cross-checking documents proves they agree, and only the node knows the truth. (`config/coins.manifest.yaml`, `scripts/spiralctl.sh`, `docs/reference/spiralctl-reference.md`, `docs/setup/OPERATIONS.md`, `tests/test_coin_storage_consistency.py`.)

### Verified on the 2.7.1 production pool

Findings from pointing this release's diagnostics at the live DigiByte pool still running v2.7.1, rather than at a test tree. Two confirm fixes listed above, one is a gap this release does not close, and one is an open question that is recorded here rather than guessed at.

- **An orphaned block did hold the miners' LEDs for its full two hours, in production.** Block 24,235,495, found 18 September at 19:32:07 and orphaned, started a celebration due to end at 21:32:07 and nothing could shorten it: v2.7.1's `block-celebrate.sh` takes no `--stop`, records no block hash, and Sentinel had no way to say the reward was gone even though it detected the orphan within minutes. This is the field case for the withdrawal mechanism added above, observed in production rather than inferred. The stratum's own countdown broadcasts (`BLOCK CELEBRATION! 1h58m0s remaining` down to `3m0s`) are what date it precisely.

- **Two `block-celebrate.sh` processes for one block is correct behaviour, not a fault.** Both the stratum and Sentinel launch the script independently for the same find — at 18:11:16 and 18:11:43 for block 24,240,943 — and the lock's extend path handled it exactly as designed: the second run found a live PID, pushed the deadline out and exited without touching the LEDs. The arithmetic proves it rather than assuming it, since `celebrate.deadline` held `start + 7227` seconds — 7200 plus the 27-second gap between the two triggers. The second live PID is a child subshell of the first, which shares its command line and so looks like a duplicate under `pgrep -af`.

- **The celebration script's diagnostics were never discarded.** An earlier draft of this entry said they were, reasoning from the stratum's `exec.Command(...)` leaving `Stdout` and `Stderr` unset. That reasoning stopped one step too early: `block-celebrate.sh` redirects its own stderr in `main()` with `exec 2>>"$CELEBRATE_LOG"` whenever it is not attached to a terminal, so what the launcher does with the handles is irrelevant. The redirect was added in b7defcc and shipped in v2.7.1. The production pool's `/spiralpool/logs/celebrate.log` held 33,404 bytes going back to 30 August, and it is what answered the next item. The one real gap is that `log()` stamps `%H:%M:%S` with no date, so entries can be dated only by correlating them against block heights and the file's mtime.

- **Resolved: the LEDs stayed lit for roughly ten hours because the restore reported success on a write the miner had already refused.** Every path that should have darkened them is correct, which is why inspection alone could not find it — the fault is not in the restore logic but in the reporting around it. The celebrate log for block 24,235,495 carries the sequence: at 21:22:14 `192.168.1.14 rejected ledset (reply: no response) — LED effects will not display`, then at 21:32:25 `LED off on 192.168.1.14` and `CELEBRATION COMPLETE - LEDs RESTORED`. So `cleanup` ran, and its writes were refused, and it logged success regardless. `set_led` returns 0 unconditionally — a deliberate choice, so that a transient `nc` timeout cannot abort a multi-hour celebration running under `set -e` — and `restore_idle_led` writes `LED off on <ip>` on the very next line without consulting the reply. The `LED_WARNED` guard, which holds the rejection warning to once per run so a celebration does not emit thousands of them, is why the final refusal ten minutes later was silent. The miner held the celebration colours until they were cleared by hand at about 07:40 with `ascset|0,ledset,0-0-0-0-0-0`. **Fixed.** `set_led` still returns 0 whatever the miner answers, because that is correct — a two-hour celebration under `set -e` must not die on one dropped packet — but it now records whether *this* write was accepted, and the restore reports that instead of assuming. A refused restore is logged as a refusal, names the miner, and prints the command to clear it by hand. The once-per-run warning is deliberately not the mechanism: `LED_WARNED` exists so a celebration does not emit thousands of identical lines, which means that by the time the restore runs the warning has usually already fired and every later refusal is silent — which is exactly what happened. `cleanup()` also called the restore with `2>/dev/null`, and since every log function writes to stderr that discarded the whole of its output; the redirect is removed, because the signal path is the one where nobody is watching the miners. Fourteen assertions cover it, including the 18 September sequence reproduced exactly — a refusal after `LED_WARNED` has already fired — and the inverse, a miner that refused earlier but accepted the restore, which must not be reported as a failure. Mutation-checked by removing the reply check, which fails seven assertions. The tests were added to the existing `tests/test_celebration_led.sh` rather than to a separate `test_celebration_led_restore.sh`, where the near-identical name would make them easy to miss. They run first in that file, because the sections after it replace `set_led` with a recorder and the reply check must exercise the real function. (`scripts/linux/block-celebrate.sh`, `tests/test_celebration_led.sh`.)

Two of the fixes listed above came out of this exercise rather than out of review: the celebration withdrawal mechanism, and `upgrade.sh --check` silently ignoring `--local` — which surfaced only because the pre-upgrade check was actually run against a 2.7.1 host, where it reported that no upgrade was available while the 3.0.0 tree sat unpacked beside it.

### Changed — Stratum V2 follows the specification (rebuild steps 2–4)
- **Encryption is now the specification's Noise handshake.** Spec-conformant SV2 miners and proxies can connect; clients of the old, non-standard handshake cannot. The protocol is `Noise_NX_Secp256k1+EllSwift_ChaChaPoly_SHA256`:
  - public keys travel as 64-byte ElligatorSwift encodings, and key agreement is BIP324's x-only ECDH
  - the server proves its static key with a certificate that the pool's authority key signs with BIP340 Schnorr
  - each frame is sent as an encrypted 6-byte header, then its payload in encrypted blocks of at most 65,519 bytes

  The previous implementation used compressed 33-byte keys, sent no certificate, and framed every message with a 2-byte length that no other SV2 software reads. ElligatorSwift and Schnorr are implemented from BIP324 and BIP340 and pass every official test vector those BIPs publish. The vectors are in `internal/stratum/v2/testdata`, with their provenance.
- **Miners can now pin the pool.** Keys used to be generated in memory at every start, so no miner could verify it was talking to this pool. The authority and static keys are now created once, when a V2 port first starts:
  - They are kept in `stratum-v2/` next to `config.yaml`, as owner-only files; `global.stratum_v2_key_dir` overrides the location. Docker keeps them in its bind-mounted config directory, so they survive container recreation.
  - `sudo spiralctl v2 pubkey` prints the authority public key for miners and proxies: hex, then the base58 form that Stratum Reference Implementation configs such as the translator's `authority_pubkey` take.
  - `sudo spiralctl v2 keygen --rotate` replaces the keys and keeps the old files as backups. Keys it creates as root are given the owner and group of the config directory, so the pool can still read them.
  - **`spiralctl v2` failed on installed systems with `Unknown command: v2`.** The `spiralctl` on the PATH is the shell wrapper (`scripts/spiralctl.sh`), not the Go binary that implements `v2`, and the wrapper had no `v2`. It now hands `v2` to the Go binary in `/spiralpool/bin`, and says so if that binary is missing. Found on the first Linux test install.
  - When Stratum V2 is enabled, the install summary shows the base58 authority key under the connection details. If the pool has not created the key yet, it shows the command that prints it instead.
  - If a coin's keys cannot be loaded, that coin's V2 port stays off and the error is logged; V1 is unaffected.
  - The server signs a fresh certificate for every handshake, valid for an hour either side of its clock.
  - `v2testminer -authority <key>` verifies the certificate.
- **Message conformance:**
  - Messages addressed to a channel set the `channel_msg` bit in `extension_type`.
  - `SetupConnection` carries `device_id`; a client that omits it is still accepted.
  - **`SetupConnection.Success` echoed the client's flags back.** Its flags mean the server's requirements, so a client that set REQUIRES_STANDARD_JOBS (bit 0) was told the pool requires a fixed version (bit 0), which forbids version rolling. The server now sends 0.
  - Another protocol, REQUIRES_WORK_SELECTION (named in the error's flags) or a version mismatch now gets `SetupConnection.Error`, and the connection is closed.
  - Messages of unimplemented extensions are ignored.
  - **A channel opened with a `max_target` below the pool's starting target was refused** with `max-target-out-of-range`. The Stratum Reference Implementation's translator opens every channel with a `max_target` derived from its hashrate, so it could not mine at all. The channel now starts at `max_target`, as the reference pool does; the spec allows either.
- **Vardiff for V2 channels.** Channels follow the coin's vardiff settings and target share time.
  - When a channel's difficulty changes, the server sends `SetTarget` and then the current job again. Each share counts at the difficulty of the job it was mined on.
  - `UpdateChannel` with a smaller `maximum_target` moves the channel to that target. An unknown channel gets `UpdateChannel.Error`.
- **Extended channels, with per-miner payout.**
  - `OpenExtendedMiningChannel` gets a 4-byte extranonce prefix plus 8 extranonce bytes the miner controls; asking for more than 8 gets `unsupported-min-extranonce-size`.
  - `NewExtendedMiningJob` carries the merkle path, and the coinbase prefix and suffix around the extranonce. The suffix pays the address in the channel's user identity.
  - `SubmitSharesExtended` goes through the same validator. An extranonce of the wrong size gets `invalid-extranonce-size`, and a share sent on the other kind of channel gets `invalid-channel-id`.
  - Group channels, `SetExtranoncePrefix` and job declaration are not implemented.

  (`internal/stratum/v2/noise.go`, `keys.go`, `ellswift.go`, `schnorr.go`, `server.go`, `session.go`, `types.go`, `encoding.go`; `internal/pool/coinpool.go`, `internal/pool/coordinator.go`; `internal/config/v2.go`; `cmd/spiralctl/cmd/stratumv2.go`, `stratumv2_owner_unix.go`, `stratumv2_owner_windows.go`, `root.go`; `cmd/v2testminer/main.go`; `scripts/spiralctl.sh`; `install.sh`.)

### Updated — coin daemons
- **Litecoin 0.21.5.6 → 0.21.5.8.** Carries 0.21.5.7's soft-forking MWEB rule, active at height 3,172,640: a kernel that signals extra data with an empty payload is rejected. That height has passed (tip 3,178,438 on 2026-09-15), so a pool on 0.21.5.6 can produce blocks the network rejects. 0.21.5.7 went only to pools and was never published. The Docker image's SHA256 is `43200c9f9d65ebc126ea5833ca9429e144c4b3273da6bb9f4e89fd7450ab1be9`. It matches `SHA256SUMS.asc`, whose signature verifies against David Burkett's key (`D356 21D5 3A1C C6A3 4567 58D0 3620 E9D3 87E5 5666`), and both download hosts serve identical bytes.
- **Fractal Bitcoin 0.3.0 → 0.4.0.** A consensus-tightening hard fork at height 2,100,000 adds an extra one-time subsidy halving. The chain has passed it (tip 2,121,738), and a 0.3.0 node builds templates claiming the pre-fork subsidy, so its blocks are rejected. 0.4.0 is the final release; the old pin was held back while it was still labelled a release candidate. Upstream publishes no checksum file, so the download is verified against a pinned SHA256 (see below).
- **Bitcoin II 29.1.0 → 31.1.0.** A hard fork at height 57,750 (tip 59,271) brings three rule changes:
  - ShockWave per-block difficulty
  - replay-protected signatures
  - data restrictions: one OP_RETURN of at most 83 bytes per transaction, no bare multisig, no inscription envelopes

  A 29.1.0 node is not following BC2 mainnet. The pool's coinbase is compatible without code changes: its only OP_RETURN is the 38-byte witness commitment. Three things about the release changed how it installs:
  - the asset is `BitcoinII-v31.1-Linux-CLI.tar.gz` — a `v` prefix, no patch digit, no architecture token, and no Linux arm64 build
  - the daemon is renamed from `bitcoinIId` to `bitcoinII-d`
  - there is no checksum file, so the download is verified against a pinned SHA256 (see below)

  Every install path installs `bitcoinII-d` under the established `bitcoinIId` name, so the systemd unit, symlinks, spiralctl and dashboard are unchanged. Ports, address prefixes, network magic and genesis are identical to 29.1.0, and no config Spiral Pool ships uses any option 31.1.0 dropped.

  The 31.1.0 binaries need **glibc 2.38**, the highest requirement of any coin in this release. Ubuntu 24.04 (2.39), Ubuntu 26.04 and Debian 13, the supported platforms, all meet it; Ubuntu 22.04 does not.
- **eCash (Bitcoin ABC) 0.33.10 → 0.33.12.** Maintenance releases with no user-visible change; no network upgrade is scheduled. The Docker image's SHA256 is `d77d9c59d021b52dc7f4d7f2156bcc3ba057703a413158457f901e43154d582b`. It is byte-identical on github.com and download.bitcoinabc.org and matches `fabien-sha256sums.0.33.12.asc`, but that file's signature was not checked.
- **Syscoin 5.1.0 → 5.1.2.** Non-mandatory patch releases: network-message validation hardening and managed sysgeth startup fixes, with no consensus, activation-height or wire-format change. The bundled sysgeth stays 5.1.0, and the tarball layout is unchanged. The Docker image's SHA256 is `cc4fb10d9c0794fc80c673db1636a7bc36892e3954facb94446db35070149e48`, matching the release's `SHA256SUMS.asc`. Despite the name, that file carries no PGP signature, and the Dockerfile's provenance comment now says so; it previously claimed a signed file.
- **DigiByte stays on 9.26.5.** 9.26.6 changes the DigiDollar consensus rules at block 24,490,000 (about 1 November 2026), but only `v9.26.6rc1` exists, and upstream says release candidates are not for mainnet. See **Planned**.
- **Namecoin stays on 28.0.** nc30.2, nc31.0 and nc31.1 are published as source only, and namecoin.org still offers binaries only up to 28.0.
- **Bitcoin Silver `v1.0.2` / source commit `ff5c3c3` → `version31.1.3`.** Upstream replaced its 2024 line with releases rebased on Bitcoin Core 31.1, and now publishes Linux binaries, so all three install paths use the same release: `install.sh` and `coin-upgrade.sh` download `bitcoinsilver-31.1.3-x86_64-linux-gnu.tar.gz` and the Docker image no longer compiles from source (a 20–60 minute build).
  - **No consensus change on mainnet.** 31.1.2 and 31.1.3 carry two fixes described upstream as consensus-code bugs; both are inert on mainnet — one sets regtest's fixed difficulty, the other clears a Taproot activation-delay field that is unused while Taproot is always active. Ports, address prefixes, network magic and genesis are unchanged, and upstream reports no reindex from the previous build.
  - **A legacy (BDB) wallet blocks the upgrade,** as it does for Bitcoin II: the Core 31 base skips legacy wallets at load, so `coin-upgrade.sh` stops and prints the `migratewallet` commands before swapping the binary.
  - 31.1.0 broke syncing for new nodes; a node stuck on it needs `blocks/`, `chainstate/` and `indexes/` wiped. 31.1.1 fixed that.
  - The binaries link libzmq, libevent and SQLite and need glibc 2.34, which Ubuntu 24.04 and 26.04 and Debian 13 all have; `libsqlite3-0` was added to the installer's package list.
- **Downloads are verified for the three coins that publish no checksum file.** Bitcoin Silver, Fractal Bitcoin and Bitcoin II are now checked against a SHA256 pinned in `install.sh`, `coin-upgrade.sh` and their Dockerfile, and a mismatch refuses the install instead of unpacking the archive. Each pin is the digest GitHub records for the release asset, matched against an independent download of it. Only Litecoin, Bitcoin, eCash and Syscoin publish checksum files of their own.
- Pins moved in `install.sh`, `coin-upgrade.sh` (targets, risk notes, BC2 and BTCS download and install), `scripts/linux/pool-mode.sh`, `docker/Dockerfile.litecoin`, `docker/Dockerfile.fractalbitcoin`, `docker/Dockerfile.bitcoinii`, `docker/Dockerfile.bitcoinsilver`, `docker/Dockerfile.ecash`, `docker/Dockerfile.syscoin` and `tests/test-coin-configs.sh`. `scripts/linux/regtest.sh` was brought up to the same versions; its LTC, FBTC and XEC pins had been left at 0.21.4, 0.2.9 and 0.31.12, and its BTCS entry still expected a source build. `scripts/linux/regtest-btcs.sh` no longer tells operators to build Bitcoin Silver from source.

### Added — coin daemon version alerts
- **A daemon left behind its target version was never mentioned again after the upgrade that noticed it.** `upgrade.sh` prints a risk-coloured pending-upgrade table, but only at the end of a pool upgrade, only to the console, and only once — so an operator who deferred an upgrade was never reminded of it again. `COIN_RISK` records what that can mean: a Bitcoin II node below 31.1.0 "is not following BC2 mainnet", and a Litecoin pool on 0.21.5.6 or older "can produce blocks upgraded nodes reject". Both keep running, keep reporting healthy and keep finding blocks while it happens, so nothing surfaces unless the operator thinks to ask. `check_chain_identity` already covered Bitcoin by detecting the symptom — the wrong chain at block 961,632 — but nothing covered the other coins, where the symptom is invisible from inside the node. Sentinel now runs `coin-upgrade.sh --list` every six hours and alerts through the existing notification path, naming each daemon, its installed and target versions, its risk class and the exact command to act on it. MAJOR entries are worded as a consensus problem; PATCH and MINOR are worded as routine, so the alert does not train operators to ignore it. It also reports **newer stable releases published upstream**, which is a second and different question. `COIN_TARGET` in `coin-upgrade.sh` is a static table shipped with each Spiral Pool release, so asking only "is this daemon at its target?" answers "yes" forever no matter what a coin's developers publish afterwards — the consensus release landing months later was still invisible. `coin-upgrade.sh` now carries a `COIN_UPSTREAM` table of release feeds and two new read-only modes, `--check-upstream` and `--list-upstream`, and Sentinel folds the answer into the same alert. Those entries are deliberately **advisory and carry no install command**: a target also carries a download URL shape, sometimes a pinned SHA256, and a `COIN_RISK` entry stating what the upgrade costs, none of which is known for an untested release. Namecoin demonstrates why that separation matters: its newest release is `nc31.1`, but namecoin-core ships no binaries for it, which is why the target is 28.0; "install latest" would chase a release that cannot be installed. Only stable releases count: GitHub's `/releases/latest` already skips prereleases, and on top of that a tag must normalise to a plain dotted number, so `v29.2.0rc1`, `v0.21.5-rc1`, `v1.0.0-beta` and `nightly-20260101` are all ignored rather than guessed at. Verified against all fifteen live release feeds: every tag shape in use resolves correctly, including `version31.1.3`, `nc31.1` and `v0.18.1.0`. Answers are cached for 24 hours and only installed coins are queried, so a typical pool makes one or two calls a day rather than sixty. A check that could not run produces silence rather than "nothing new" — and so does a check that ran but could not reach one particular feed, which is a distinction the first version did not make. The script exits 0 when some feeds answer and others do not, and `--list-upstream` printed only the rows it found, so a partial failure arrived at Sentinel as an empty list: indistinguishable from a working check that genuinely had no news, and a feed broken for weeks would have looked exactly like a quiet one. `--list-upstream` now names those coins on an `unreachable <COIN>` line — two fields, where a release row is three, so any parser expecting three ignores them — Sentinel keeps them apart from the release rows, logs them once per cooldown even when there is nothing else to report, and the alert names them under "Could not check" rather than folding them into the all-clear. Found by running the check on a live pool rather than by reading it. Naming the dead feeds is only half the answer, though, because nothing was watching for the case where they stay dead — so a second alert, `coin_version_check_failing`, fires when a feed has not answered for a configurable number of consecutive checks (`coin_version_check_fail_threshold`, default 4, which at a six-hour interval is a full day of being unable to ask). The count is per coin, resets the moment that feed answers again, and is persisted, since a Sentinel that restarts daily would otherwise never reach any threshold. A check that could not run at all counts against nothing: that says the question was never asked, not that a particular feed is down. Alerting on the first miss was rejected deliberately — firing on every transient GitHub failure trains operators to mute the alert, including when a consensus release lands. A guard fails the suite if a coin gains an upgrade target without a release source to check. A daemon whose version could not be read is also never reported as behind: `get_installed_version` answers the literal `unknown` or `error` in those cases, and `list_upgrades` filters only `not_installed`, so both would otherwise have arrived as an installed version and rendered a red "can follow the wrong chain" verdict on a node that may be perfectly current. They are logged and dropped, and if every row was unreadable the answer is "unknown" rather than "nothing pending". The cooldown is keyed on the *set* of pending daemons rather than on the alert type, so a newly released upgrade is not swallowed by a cooldown opened for an unrelated routine one, and a delivery failure restores the previous stamp rather than buying a day of silence for a message nobody received. Off via `coin_version_check_enabled: false`, or at runtime with `spiralctl alerts disable coin_upgrade_available`. A guard in `tests/test_script_integrity.sh` now checks that every alert Sentinel raises is a name that command accepts. Two in this release were not: this one, and `automation_failed` from the new miner automation — both would have shipped unmutable, and the second is the alert that fires when a scheduled sleep, wake or power action fails, so an operator with a flapping rig could not have silenced it. The guard reads the alert names from Sentinel's per-type tables rather than from `send_alert()` call sites, because several sites pass the name in a variable: the first version grepped for `send_alert("literal")` and passed on the very bug it was written to catch. (`src/sentinel/SpiralSentinel.py`, `scripts/spiralctl.sh`, `docs/reference/SENTINEL.md`, `coin-upgrade.sh`, `tests/test_coin_version_alert.py`, `tests/test_script_integrity.sh`.)
- **No version logic was duplicated to do it.** `coin-upgrade.sh --list` already emits `COIN INSTALLED TARGET RISK`, and deliberately skips the banner, the root check and the ENV check, so it needs no privileges; Sentinel parses that and nothing more. The comments around `get_installed_version` and `_ver_matches` record what comparing versions correctly cost — ask the binary rather than a cache, keep build suffixes so a Knots build never compares equal to Core, pad to four components so `31.1` matches `31.1.0` — and `install.sh` still carries a note about a release where three files disagreed on Litecoin's current version. Reimplementing any of that in Python would have recreated the silent failure this release exists to remove, inside the tool meant to report it.

### Fixed — coin-upgrade.sh
- **`--coin <X> --reindex` did nothing for every coin except BTC once the binary was at target.** The MAJOR warning tells operators to rerun with `--reindex` if the daemon fails to start. That is exactly when the new binary is already installed, and the "already at target — nothing to do" shortcut returned before the reindex. `--reindex` now takes the full path for every coin, as it already did for BTC.
- **BC2: a legacy (BDB) wallet would silently stop loading.** BitcoinII 31.1 is built on Bitcoin Core 31.1, which skips legacy wallets at load. The on-disk wallet scan written for BTC now takes a coin, and a BC2 upgrade refuses to start while a legacy wallet file is present, printing the `migratewallet` commands. Wallets Spiral Pool creates are descriptor wallets, so this catches imported ones.
- **BC2: a node that rejected post-fork blocks under 29.1.0 could stay stuck after the binary swap.** The rejection can be persisted in the block index, and a new binary does not clear it. After a BC2 upgrade, `verify_bc2_fork_chain` compares block 57,750 with `0000000000000000283f16daab1be22eb25a53bac57cf92af1a484163325e9e1`. That hash was fetched from explorer.bitcoin-ii.org and cross-checked against the neighbouring blocks on bitcoinii.ddns.net. If the node is not on it, the check runs `reconsiderblock` on every invalid chain tip and on the fork block, then polls for two minutes. A node still syncing gets a warning, and a node on a different block 57,750 fails with the reindex command.
- How the four upgrades behave in `coin-upgrade.sh` and `upgrade.sh`:
  - **Version detection:** each new daemon's `--version` (`Litecoin Core version v0.21.5.8`, `Bitcoin Core daemon version v0.4.0`, `BitcoinII daemon version v31.1.0`, `Bitcoin ABC version v0.33.12-…`) parses to its target, so the post-install check does not roll back a good install.
  - **What `upgrade.sh` does:** it never touches coin daemons. It deploys the new `coin-upgrade.sh` and lists the pending upgrades.
  - **Config:** `coin-upgrade.sh` changes no daemon config (the DigiByte pruning offer is the one existing exception and does not apply here).

### Fixed — dashboard and pool API
- **Upgraded installs kept their original dashboard icons, web-app manifest and service worker.** `install.sh` copies the whole dashboard, but `upgrade.sh`'s `update_dashboard` copied only `static/css`, `static/js` and `static/templates`. It now also copies `static/icons`, `static/manifest.json` and `static/service-worker.js`. (`upgrade.sh`.)
- **Avalon Nano 3S and Avalon Q power profiles sent a workmode command their firmware does not take.** The dashboard sent the A10-era `ascset|0,workmode,<n>` to every model. Canaan's API reference for the Nano 3S gives `ascset|0,workmode,set,<n>`, with 0 = Low, 1 = Mid and 2 = High; the Avalon Q takes the same form for Eco, Standard and Super. Both models now get the `set` form, and every other model keeps the old one. The Nano 3S profiles also mapped *balanced* to mode 0 and *high* to mode 1, and wrote frequency and voltage values Canaan does not document for it; they now map to 0, 1 and 2 with no frequency or voltage writes. The original Nano 3 and the Avalon Mini 3 are unchanged, because no source confirms their mode numbers. (`src/dashboard/dashboard.py`.)
- **`GET /api/pools/{id}/miners` listed every active wallet address to anyone who could reach port 4000.** Both API servers now require `X-API-Key` on it whenever `admin_api_key` is set, as every Linux install and upgrade does. Lookups of a single address (`/api/pools/{id}/miners/{address}` and the portal) stay public. The list's callers now send the key:
  - Sentinel's zombie detection, per-coin fetch and Telegram `/miners` command send `pool_admin_api_key`
  - `spiralctl miners` and `spiralctl workers` send `admin_api_key` from `config.yaml`

  A pool with no key configured keeps the old public list, rather than silently losing Sentinel's miner checks. That applies to Windows Docker installs, whose `docker\.env` was written with an empty `ADMIN_API_KEY`: `install-windows.ps1` now generates one, and an existing Windows install can set `ADMIN_API_KEY` in `docker\.env` and restart. (`internal/api/server.go`, `internal/api/server_v2.go`, `src/sentinel/SpiralSentinel.py`, `scripts/spiralctl.sh`, `install-windows.ps1`, `docs/reference/REFERENCE.md`, `docs/reference/SENTINEL.md`.)

- **`GET /api/stratum/connect` returned 500 to every caller, authenticated or not.** It calls `get_stratum_address()` to reuse that endpoint's logic, but did so inside a fresh `app.test_request_context()`. That context carries no session and no `X-API-Key`, so `api_key_or_login_required` — which wraps `get_stratum_address` too — rejected the inner call and returned a redirect to `/login`. `get_json()` on a redirect is `None`, and the next line called `.get("success")` on it. The endpoint never worked; its own docstring says the dashboard's Pool Configuration modal uses it to auto-fill miner settings. It now calls the view in the live request context, which this endpoint's own decorator has already authorised, and treats a non-JSON answer as a 502 rather than an exception. (`src/dashboard/dashboard.py`.)
- **`GET /api/combined` — the endpoint the whole dashboard polls — raised `AttributeError` whenever no coin was configured yet.** `fetch_block_reward()` passes the detected primary coin down its fallback chain, and that value is `None` on a fresh install before `config.yaml` names a coin. `coin_rpc()` and `fetch_live_block_reward()` both called `.upper()` on it. The function was written to tolerate this — its later fallbacks are already guarded with `if primary_coin` — so only those two steps were missing the check; both now treat a missing symbol as "no data" and let the existing final fallback supply the default. (`src/dashboard/dashboard.py`.)
- **Both were invisible because dashboard testing had only ever reached the login page.** Walking all 93 authenticated `GET` routes is now a test: a 500 there is always an escape into the blanket `@app.errorhandler(Exception)` and never a deliberate answer, while 502 and 503 are what an unreachable pool API and a not-ready probe are supposed to return. (`tests/test_dashboard_routes_authenticated.py`.)

### Fixed — regtest harness and release packaging
These are defects in the validation harness and the release archive rather than in the pool, so they are not counted among the pre-existing bugs above. They are listed because each one let a test report success it had not earned.

- **eCash's regtest pool API port collided with DigiByte's standard RPC port, and the check passed on the collision.** `config-xec-regtest.yaml` set `api_port: 14022`, which is DigiByte's documented RPC port throughout this project. On any host also running a DigiByte node — the ordinary case for this software — check 8/8 connected to `digibyted` instead of the pool and accepted its `405 Method Not Allowed` as proof the pool's API was healthy, because the check accepted any status from 200 to 499. Every other coin answers `404` there, from the pool itself. Moved to 14024 in both the harness and the shipped config, and a guard in `tests/test_script_integrity.sh` now fails if any regtest pool port lands on a coin daemon's standard RPC port, or if a coin's harness and config disagree on any of its four ports (RPC, stratum, API, metrics) — not just the one that bit. (`scripts/linux/regtest.sh`, `config/regtest/config-xec-regtest.yaml`, `tests/test_script_integrity.sh`.)
- **Pepecoin's port remap was applied to the harness but only half applied to its config.** The RPC port moved to 18572 and the API port to 14028 in `regtest.sh`, while `config-pep-regtest.yaml` still carried 18570 and 14026. The API check therefore probed a port nothing was listening on and reported `HTTP 000`, which accounts for Pepecoin's `flagged=1` entirely. (`scripts/linux/regtest.sh`, `config/regtest/config-pep-regtest.yaml`.)
- **The worker-name payout check compared an address against itself on Dogecoin and Pepecoin.** Step 8f proves the setting works by mining to a second address and confirming the coinbase pays it rather than the configured wallet. For legacy-wallet coins the harness reused `$MINING_ADDRESS` — the address the config already pays — because those daemons have no `createwallet`. The comparison was then between a value and itself, so `[4/5] SOLO MINING VERIFIED` passed whether or not the toggle did anything, on the two coins whose address handling has the most history. A legacy wallet cannot hold a second *wallet*, but `getnewaddress` works, so it now takes a distinct second address from the same wallet — still an address the operator owns, which is the single-operator model the setting is licensed for. Verified against real `dogecoind` and `pepecoind` regtest daemons, and the addresses they generate are pinned in a test, because Pepecoin has shipped a version-byte defect once already. (`scripts/linux/regtest.sh`, `src/stratum/internal/coin/legacy_wallet_regtest_addr_test.go`.)
- **The Stratum V2 step stopped at the first accepted share, so nothing ever proved a V2 block reached the daemon.** A pool's share target is deliberately easier than the network's, so a run that ends on `SHARE ACCEPTED` exercises the share path and stops exactly where it starts to matter: block detection, block assembly and the `submitblock` round trip were never reached. That left the one part of V2 that actually pays the operator resting on the argument that it shares V1's code — true, and not the same thing as having been run. The test miner takes `-untilblock`, which mines to the network target from `SetNewPrevHash` so the first submission is necessarily a block, and the harness then asserts the chain tip advanced. The attribution is clean because the step already pauses cpuminer and waits for the tip to settle before it begins, so nothing else can move the height; a share that meets the network target and leaves the tip where it was is now a failure with its own message, rather than a pass. SHA-256d only: regtest is difficulty 1 either way, which is ~4.3 billion hashes — seconds to minutes for Go SHA256d, and hours for scrypt at roughly a thousandth the hash rate — so scrypt keeps the share-level assertion and says so. Run on Bitcoin Core 31.1 regtest: share accepted, tip 8 → 9. (`scripts/linux/regtest.sh`, `src/stratum/cmd/v2testminer/main.go`.)
- **The harness printed `RESULT: PASS` while exiting 1, and could exit 0 while printing `FAIL`.** The banner keyed only on blocks found and lifecycle checks, ignoring `EXIT_CODE` set by earlier steps — so a failed Stratum V2 test produced a green banner and a non-zero exit, which a driver keying off the exit code recorded as a failure the reader had just been told was a pass. The `PARTIAL PASS` and `FAIL` arms also never set `EXIT_CODE` at all, so a genuine failure could exit 0. Both directions fixed. (`scripts/linux/regtest.sh`.)
- **A failing HA VIP test still produced `RESULT: PASS` and exit 0.** The same class of defect as the entry above, in a step that entry did not reach. Step 8g scores eight checks and prints its own verdict, but its failure branch only logged an error: `HA_VIP_PASS` is consulted by the summary solely when `HA_ONLY=1`, so on an ordinary run nothing read it and `EXIT_CODE` was never set. The summary table had no HA VIP row either, so the only failing step was also the only step missing from the table an operator reads. Found on a Bitcoin regtest run that printed `HA VIP failover test failed` at 4/8 and then `RESULT: PASS`. The failure now sets `EXIT_CODE`, and the table carries an HA VIP row gated on whether the step ran at all — `HA_VIP_TOTAL` is assigned unconditionally, so keying the row off it would have reported `FAIL (0/8)` for a step skipped by `HA_VIP_TEST=0`, by finding no blocks, or for want of sudo, trading one false report for another. (`scripts/linux/regtest.sh`.)
- **The DigiByte SHA-256d guard presented an inference as a test result.** The harness refuses `dgb` because DigiByte's daemon mines only Scrypt in regtest, and told the reader the SHA-256d path "IS verified working" on the grounds that DGB-SCRYPT passed and it is the "same code path, just different algorithm string". It is not. `DigiByteScryptCoin` embeds `DigiByteCoin` and overrides nine methods, so a Scrypt run does exercise the shared base — address handling, GBT rules, coinbase construction — while running the Scrypt versions of `HashBlockHeader`, `DifficultyFromTarget`, `ShareDifficultyMultiplier` and `MultiAlgoGBTParam`: precisely the SHA-256d code it was cited as evidence for. The message now states what is covered and what is not — unit tests reach the first three, `MultiAlgoGBTParam` has no test anywhere in the tree, and DGB SHA-256d has never been mined end to end. The `8/8 HA VIP` figure it quoted was dropped, having no date or run behind it. The command it offered for the Scrypt run, `COIN=dgb-scrypt ./scripts/linux/regtest.sh`, could not work either: `COIN` is assigned from `$1`, so the variable was overwritten and the script then failed argument validation. (`scripts/linux/regtest.sh`.)
- **The Stratum V2 test could fail on a block burst through no fault of the protocol.** Each ZMQ block notification makes the pool force one `cleanJobs` broadcast, and those notifications keep draining for a few seconds after the chain height itself stops moving — so a share submitted in that window came back `stale-share`. The pool's behaviour is correct: jobs are invalidated on a new prev-hash or a forced refresh, never on a routine template refresh. The harness was starting the test too early, and now retries once after letting the pool go quiet. (`scripts/linux/regtest.sh`.)
- **A `.gitignore` rule excluded a security test from the repository and from every release archive.** `*_password*` is meant to stop a credential dump being committed, but it is an unanchored glob, so it also matched `tests/test_config_redacted_passwords.py` — the test asserting that config passwords are redacted. The file had never been committed and no clone ever received it, while it ran locally and counted towards the suite. The credential patterns stay deliberately broad and now exempt source extensions, so a legitimate file is never silently excluded for naming the thing it tests. (`.gitignore`.)

### Documentation
- **`docs/setup/UPGRADE_GUIDE.md`:** a v3.0.0 section on what changes for a running pool (Stratum V2 opt-in, the new handshake and authority key, the coin daemon upgrades).
- **`docs/reference/DASHBOARD.md`:** the explorer and automation pages and their API endpoints.
- **`docs/reference/SENTINEL.md`:** automation (rule files, schedules, re-apply, retries), the `automation_failed` alert, and auto-restart finding miners by IP. Its `--test` example also claimed the flag tests "all configured notification channels (Discord, Telegram, XMPP, ntfy, SMTP)"; `test_notification()` calls `send_discord` and nothing else, and the script's own `--help` has always said so. An operator reading the doc would have taken a passing `--test` as proof that Telegram, XMPP, ntfy and SMTP were working, which is the failure mode this release exists to remove — a check reporting confidently on something it never examined.
- **`docs/reference/MINER_SUPPORT.md`:** what each miner family supports for restart, sleep and power, how to test one miner with `spiralctl miner control`, and the per-miner payout notice.
- **`docs/reference/REFERENCE.md`:** Stratum V2 is opt-in and how to print the authority key; the miner list needs the admin key; the portal endpoint.
- **`docs/architecture/ARCHITECTURE.md`**, **`docs/setup/DOCKER_GUIDE.md`**, **`docs/development/TESTING.md`:** Stratum V2 as rebuilt (Noise handshake, channels, vardiff, where Docker keeps the keys) and its new test files.
- **Every document under `docs/` and the repository root was read against the code it describes, and the mismatches corrected.** The largest class was not drift but damage: removing the QBX coin in v2.5.2/2.5.3 stripped the token and collapsed the surrounding whitespace, leaving text that still reads as prose and is wrong. Confirmed against `git show 15ca124:<file>` and repaired everywhere it landed -- nine lines in `docs/reference/MULTI_COIN_PORT.md`, three in `OPERATIONS.md`, two each in `UPGRADE_GUIDE.md` and `DOCKER_GUIDE.md`, and one each in `SentinelConfig.md`, `CLOUD_OPERATIONS.md`, `TRADEMARKS.md` and `spiralctl-reference.md`. Among them a `docker compose --profile up -d` line that had lost its profile name and taken the `sys` command out with it, an empty row in the cloud egress table, and `| , community |` in the trademark table. eCash was the repeated casualty of that cleanup and is restored to seven lists it had been dropped from. Also corrected: ten `file:line` citations in `SECURITY_MODEL.md` and `ARCHITECTURE.md` that pointed at unrelated code -- every underlying *value* was right, so the line numbers are replaced with symbol names that do not rot; the Windows installer's coin menu, which skipped 16, numbered SYS 17 and listed the coins in an order the installer does not use; Debian 13 missing from the described install flow; the `sats_surge` documentation described above; and seven stale counts (`install.sh` at ~41,900 lines, not ~36,500; `SpiralSentinel.py` at ~23,800, not ~20,700 -- three thousand lines of drift since the last time that figure was corrected; 52 router patterns, not 47/48; 285 Go test files, not 240; 23 Python test files, not 22; 3,889 test functions, not 3,500+; `test_safe_num.py` at 42 tests, not 48).
- **Five documents and the installer sent operators to `TERMS.md` Section 5D, which does not exist.** The SimpleSwap disclosure is 5C; the headings ran 5A, 5B, 5C, 5E with no D at all, while the survival clause in Section 13 listed Single-Operator Architecture as 5D -- so the heading letter was the original typo and it had propagated. `NOSEC.md`, `README.md`, `WARNINGS.md`, `docs/reference/SENTINEL.md` and `docs/setup/OPERATIONS.md` now cite 5C; Single-Operator Architecture is renumbered 5E → **5D**, closing the gap, with its references in `README.md`, `WARNINGS.md` and the survival clause updated. `install.sh` prints the same citation to the operator during install and had the same error -- markdown-only checking would not have found it. The v1.1.0 changelog entry that records the section's creation keeps its original wording and notes the renumber. Section 5C is also retitled from "SimpleSwap.io Integration" to "SimpleSwap.io Swap Alert Links", because there is no integration: Spiral Sentinel composes a URL and puts it in a notification, holding no account, no credentials and no API client, and never contacting SimpleSwap.io. `TERMS.md`, `PRIVACY.md` and `WARNINGS.md` also said the feature was disabled by default and opt-in at install, which was never true. A dead anchor in `docs/setup/DOCKER_GUIDE.md`, left by a retitled WSL2 heading, is repointed. (`TERMS.md`, `PRIVACY.md`, `WARNINGS.md`, `NOSEC.md`, `README.md`, `CHANGELOG.md`, `install.sh`, `docs/`.)
- **`THIRD_PARTY_LICENSES.txt`:** BIP324 and BIP340, whose published test vectors are included under `src/stratum/internal/stratum/v2/testdata/`. The ElligatorSwift, ECDH and Schnorr code is written from the BIPs' algorithms; no reference code is included.

### Release identity
- **A dashboard theme for the codename: `V3.0 Spiral Covenant`.** The Codename series ran V1.0 Black Ice, V1.1 Phi Forge, V1.2 Convergent Spiral and V2.0 Phi Hash Reactor, and stopped there. Blood crimson on altar black, with candle gold and votive violet as the secondary accents, bone-pale text, `Cinzel Decorative` for display, and a sharper 3px radius than the rounded codename themes before it. The background keeps the golden-angle (`137.5deg`) gradients the series uses and adds a vignette. Body text stays `Inter` rather than a display serif, because a dashboard is read at a glance. It is a new file only — themes are enumerated from `static/themes/*.json` by `/api/themes`, so it needs no template change, and `upgrade.sh` copies every shipped theme into an existing install without disturbing an operator's own. All colour, gradient, font and effect keys match the existing codename themes exactly, so no variable falls back to the default palette. Original work, so it takes no entry in `THEME-LICENSES.txt`, which covers only palettes derived from community projects. (`src/dashboard/static/themes/spiral-covenant.json`.)
- **Codename: Spiral Citadel → Spiral Covenant**, marking the major version. It changes in the install and upgrade banners, the backup and restore utilities, the version the stratum API reports (`3.0.0-SPIRAL_COVENANT`), Sentinel's `__codename__` and reported version, `README.md` and the documentation footers. Mentions of earlier releases keep Spiral Citadel.
- The version string moves to `3.0.0` across `VERSION`, the installer and upgrade scripts, the Go binaries' built-in version (`spiralctl`, and the HA agent's `SpiralPoolVersion`), and the notice and documentation footers. Many of the strings replaced read `2.7.0`, not `2.7.1`, because they had not been moved for v2.7.1: `LICENSE`, `README.md`, `TERMS.md`, `WARNINGS.md`, the notices (`CONTRIBUTING.md`, `EXPORT.md`, `NOSEC.md`, `PRIVACY.md`, `SECURITY.md`, `TRADEMARKS.md`), `THIRD_PARTY_LICENSES.txt`, `assets/PROVENANCE.txt`, `install-windows.ps1` and most documentation footers. The `2.7.0` references that remain are historical (`CONTRIBUTORS` and test comments).

### Tests
- **Go:** added
  - `internal/jobs/solo_coinbase_test.go` (rewritten): payout scripts validated per coin; each miner paying its own address across plain, witness and witness-plus-oracle coinbases; invalid names paying the pool; the coinbase split for every builder; the bounded cache
  - `internal/shares/per_miner_coinbase_test.go`: merkle root differs per miner, and the assembled block pays the finding miner with a coinbase txid equal to the header's merkle root
  - `internal/stratum/v1/handler_payout_test.go`
  - `internal/stratum/per_miner_notify_test.go`: the real server over `net.Pipe`, covering the first job after authorize and authorized-only broadcast
  - `internal/scheduler/per_miner_payout_test.go`: smart-port notify pays the wallet shares are validated against
  - `internal/auxpow/createauxblock_test.go`: a fake aux node asserts `createauxblock` is called with the configured address

  - `internal/api/portal_test.go`, against an in-memory store:
    - a lookup aggregates only the pools the address mined
    - an idle address gets an empty list
    - malformed addresses are refused before any query
    - no database gives 503; a database error gives a 500 that doesn't leak its text
    - the per-IP limiter trips for one client but not another
    - non-GET is refused
    - the page and assets carry the CSP, with no inline script
    - `portal.js` contains no HTML-parsing sinks

    Five deliberate breakages each fail it: no address check, no activity filter, `innerHTML` in the script, no portal limiter, and a leaked database error. `GetBlocksByMiner` has no automated test, since the database integration tests need PostgreSQL.
  - `internal/stratum/v2/pipeline_test.go`: a real Stratum V2 client over TCP, against the server wired to the real `shares.ValidatorV2`. It covers:
    - a V2 block pays the channel's address, and its header matches the header the miner hashed byte for byte
    - each channel's merkle root commits to its own extranonce prefix and payout address
    - refresh vs. new-prevhash job sequencing, with a block on the new job
    - an unknown job ID is rejected before validation
    - `max_target` is enforced
    - reject-reason mapping

    Four deliberate breakages each fail the suite: prevhash byte order, a root that ignores the payout address, the wrong extranonce slice, and future jobs sent as active.

  `go build`, `go vet` and `go test` pass across auxpow, coin, daemon, config, jobs, shares, stratum, stratum/v1, stratum/v2, pool, scheduler and protocol. One exception: `TestDuplicateTrackerMemoryGrowth` in `internal/shares` failed once, at 106 MB against its 100 MB heap limit, while the pool suite ran in parallel. It passes on its own, both on this tree and on a clean checkout of the previous commit, so it is load-sensitive rather than a regression.
- **Python:** `tests/test_explorer.py` (29 tests). Against a fake node it covers:
  - summary with pool-found blocks, and a node that is down
  - block with body and coinbase payout; a pruned block (header plus note); the boolean-`verbose` fallback; malformed and unknown IDs
  - transaction from the mempool, needing a block hash, and passed its block
  - the address-scan descriptor and timeout; descriptor injection refused; scan failure

  Through the Flask app it covers login required, a real route querying the right coin, unknown coin and bad input, and the busy-scan lock, plus no HTML-parsing sinks in `explorer.js`. Six deliberate breakages each fail it: a loose address pattern, a route without login, no scan lock, no verbose fallback, no pruned note, and `innerHTML` in the script. The existing `test_safe_num.py` and `test_restart_cooldown.py` still pass.
- **Go:** `internal/api/miners_auth_test.go`, through both servers' real routers: the miner list refuses a missing or wrong key and serves the right one; it stays public with no key configured; a single-address lookup never needs the key.
- **Python:** `tests/test_avalon_workmode.py` (6 tests) checks the exact `ascset` sent for each Nano 3S and Avalon Q profile, and that older models keep the plain form. `tests/test_pool_miners_auth.py` (5 tests) checks Sentinel sends the key, sends no header when none is set, and that every miner-list call in Sentinel and `spiralctl.sh` passes it. That last check caught `spiralctl workers` still calling without the key during development.
- **Go, Stratum V2 steps 2–4:**
  - `internal/stratum/v2/ellswift_test.go` and `schnorr_test.go` check the published BIP vectors: every BIP324 ElligatorSwift decode and inverse-mapping vector, BIP324's ECDH values, and every BIP340 signing and verification vector.
  - `noise_test.go` (rewritten) covers the handshake:
    - the handshake messages are 64 and 234 bytes
    - the certificate verifies; it is refused for another authority, another static key, an altered or out-of-window certificate, or an unknown version
    - flipping a byte of act 2 in transit breaks the handshake
    - a 70,000-byte frame takes 22 + 65,535 + 4,497 bytes on the wire
    - incomplete writes and oversize frames are refused
  - `keys_test.go`: key files are owner-only, never overwritten and stable across loads, and bad files are refused.
  - `conformance_test.go` runs over TCP against the real share validator:
    - the channel bit is set on channel messages, and every V2 test now checks it on every message the server sends
    - the new messages round-trip, and `device_id` is optional
    - `SetupConnection.Success` flags are 0; the three refusals each close the connection
    - `UpdateChannel` brings `SetTarget` and a new job, and the next share is validated no harder than the target sent
    - fast shares raise vardiff difficulty 4× with `SetTarget`, and each share counts at its job's difficulty
    - a block built from an extended channel's own coinbase and merkle path has the header the miner hashed and pays the channel's address
    - wrong extranonce sizes and wrong channel kinds are refused
    - a new block reaches an extended channel as a future job
  - `cmd/spiralctl/cmd/stratumv2_test.go`: keygen creates the keys once, `pubkey` matches in hex and base58, and `--rotate` keeps backups.
  - `keys_test.go` checks the base58 key form against the authority key in the Stratum Reference Implementation's example translator config.
  - `pipeline_test.go`: a standard or extended channel opened with a `max_target` below the starting target gets `max_target`, and the validator enforces no more than that. Restoring the refusal fails the test. The shared test job's coinbase declared a 4-byte scriptSig although shares insert 12 extranonce bytes into it; its length byte now counts them, as the job manager's does, so the assembled coinbase is a valid transaction.

  Twelve deliberate breakages each fail a test:
  - the channel bit left off
  - the setup flags echoed back
  - a refused setup left open
  - the wrong ECDH role
  - the certificate digest without the static key
  - swapped transport keys
  - no payload chunking
  - no oversize check
  - an extended coinbase paying the pool
  - an ignored extranonce
  - `UpdateChannel` ignoring the maximum target
  - vardiff that never retargets

  Two of them first went uncaught: the refused-setup test counted a read timeout as a close, and the oversize-frame test hung instead of failing. Both tests were fixed, and both breakages now fail.
- **Go, Stratum V2 interoperability (rebuild step 5):** `internal/stratum/v2/interop_test.go` serves an external client when `SPIRAL_SV2_INTEROP_ADDR` is set. It was run against the Stratum Reference Implementation (`sv2-apps` v0.7.0), built from source:
  - **`mining_device`, standard channel:** 402 of 402 shares accepted. The run covered the Noise handshake with certificate verification against the base58 authority key, `SetupConnection`, 3 vardiff `SetTarget`s and 10 new-prevhash jobs.
  - **Translator proxy, extended channel,** with an SV1 CPU miner behind it: the first runs found the `max_target` refusal and the invalid test coinbase described above.
  - **Final run, both clients mining at once:** 38 of 38 translator shares and 206 of 206 `mining_device` shares accepted. That run covered vardiff `SetTarget` on both channel kinds and 13 new-prevhash jobs. The translator checks every share against the extended job it rebuilt from the pool's coinbase prefix, suffix and merkle path, and every share it forwarded passed the pool's validator.
- **Python, miner automation:**
  - `tests/test_miner_automation.py` (33 tests): rule validation, including rules a miner cannot follow; overnight windows; sleep, wake, retry back-off and power re-apply; saved state; the Avalon schedule migration.
  - `tests/test_miner_control.py` (34 tests): each driver against real HTTP and TCP mock devices. The Antminer mock verifies HTTP digest auth the way a miner does, cgminer replies are NUL-terminated, and Whatsminer frames are length-prefixed with the token checked. Eleven cover `spiralctl miner control`: the device is built from the same files Sentinel reads and arguments override them; a command reaches a mock Avalon and the output never contains the password; a refusal, an unknown miner and an unsupported action fail, the last two without contacting the miner; power takes a level or whole watts; malformed commands are usage errors; the module runs as a script; and `spiralctl` runs it as the pool user. Six deliberate breakages each fail them: the password printed, a file's type beating `--type`, credentials not loaded, watts passed as text, the Avalon model not read, and `spiralctl` running it as root. A separate test sends sleep and wake to a mock Nano 3S and requires both refused without a request reaching it; turning Avalon sleep back on fails it and seven others. On the real Nano, `info` still told the operator to set the Avalon model when `--model` was given, because the hint tested for sleep rather than power modes; `info` with a model is now tested too, and the old condition fails it.
  - `tests/test_sentinel_automation.py` (10 tests): restart routing and fallback, lookup by IP, settings reload, per-miner opt-out, and a full rule run with saved state and the failure alert.
  - `tests/test_automation_routes.py` (11 tests): login required, no password ever returned, rules a miner cannot follow refused with the file kept, credentials, the migration, quiet hours, and every install path deploying the modules.

  Seventeen deliberate breakages each fail a test, including:
  - the overnight window's day
  - the re-apply after reboot
  - the Antminer no-pools guard
  - the Whatsminer token
  - the LAN-only address check
  - restart routing
  - sleeping miners raising offline alerts
  - a PUT without capability checks
  - a GET that leaks credentials
  - a missing Docker copy

  The credentials file-mode check runs only on Linux, so it was not exercised on the Windows development machine.
- **Go, pre-existing bug fixes:**
  - `internal/shares/block_above_share_target_test.go` mines a hash that meets a regtest network target but misses a share difficulty of 1e12, and requires both validators to accept it as a block with block hex to submit.
  - `internal/stratum/v2/openchannel_race_test.go` opens 50 channels while a goroutine broadcasts jobs continuously, and requires `OpenMiningChannel.Success` to be the first message on every one.
- **Python, pre-existing bug fixes:**
  - `tests/test_config_redacted_passwords.py` (3 tests) saves back what `GET /api/config` returns and checks the stored passwords survive, that a real new password still replaces one, and that the placeholder is never written — for a device whose password is stored, and for one whose is not.
  - `tests/test_sentinel_names_bound.py` parses Sentinel and fails if any function reads `ALL_MINER_TYPES` before assigning it.

  Each of the five fixes above was reverted in turn and its test fails: both validators (rejecting the block as low difficulty), the Sentinel lookup, the `ha_manager.py` copy, the dashboard placeholder, and the V2 channel lock (with the window widened by a sleep, the miner gets `NewMiningJob` before its Success).
- **Go, multi-coin port fixes:**
