  - `internal/config/multiport_algo_test.go` rejects a `multi_port.coins` set that mixes mining algorithms and accepts one that does not, resolving each symbol through the coin registry rather than a hardcoded list.
  - `internal/pool/multiport_tls_test.go` covers the config the multi-coin port builds: a `tls_port` produces a TLS listen address, no `tls_port` stays plaintext, a `tls_port` with no certificate to inherit is refused at startup, and a config with no enabled coin is an error rather than a silent default.
  - `internal/stratum/server_multiport_tls_test.go` completes a real TLS handshake against the port when `ListenTLS` is set, and asserts nothing is listening when it is empty — the assertion that would have caught the original defect, which a config-shape check could not.
- **Python, Sentinel and the documentation guards:**
  - `tests/test_simpleswap_toggle.py` (5 tests) proves the SimpleSwap link is present by default, present when the key is absent from an older config, gone when the key is false, and that the surge alert still fires without it.
  - `tests/test_doc_crossrefs.py` resolves every link target, `#anchor` and "Section N" citation across all 35 markdown files and 58 shell, Python and PowerShell sources.
  - `tests/test_doc_facts.py` recomputes the counts the documentation states — source line counts, test-file and test-function totals, router patterns, dashboard routes and themes — and resolves all 118 documented Sentinel defaults against `DEFAULT_CONFIG`.
  - `tests/test_coin_storage_consistency.py` requires the manifest, both installers and all four documents to publish the same per-coin disk figure, and every coin to declare where its figure came from.
  - `tests/test_spiralctl_docs_match.py` checks documented commands are dispatched, advertised config keys are handled in both directions, and every key the installer writes has a declared default.

  Each of these guards was mutation-checked against the defect it exists for, including the three real historical errors in the stated counts and the two self-contradicting storage figures. Three of the extractors silently matched nothing on the first attempt, so each now asserts it parsed something before comparing.
- **Shell:** `tests/test_upgrade_regression.sh` goes from 142 to 218 assertions:
  - 8 for `rightsize_daemon_resources`, which had no coverage at all before this release, driven against scratch configs with the box's RAM and systemd directory both overridden: a single-coin box with headroom keeps `dbcache=8192`; a five-coin box on the same RAM is still capped to 4096; a config left unchanged gets no backup file; an overridden one is backed up before it is rewritten; a value the old cap cut to exactly 4096 is restored; a short chain's 2048 and the separate `maxconnections` cap are both left alone; and the systemd memory ceiling is raised to `dbcache + 5GB` alongside the restore
  - 4 running `stop_services` against a fake `systemctl`: an active and an `activating` stratum are both recorded as running, an inactive one is not, and the rollback path decides the same way. Reverting the fix fails the `activating` case
  - 6 checking `update_dashboard` copies `static/css`, `static/js`, `static/templates`, `static/icons`, `manifest.json` and `service-worker.js`; removing the new `static/js` copy fails it
  - 12 for the V2 opt-in migration: V1/TLS untouched, ports closed, backup and marker written, a re-enable survives the next upgrade
  - 6 for the wallet-recovery descriptor list, run through the same membership test the recovery code uses: DGB, BTC, XEC, FBTC, BTCS and BC2 are all covered, and dropping BC2 fails it
  - 27 for `coin-upgrade.sh`, run against the real extracted functions: version banners for LTC, FBTC, BC2, BTCS, XEC and SYS, the BC2 asset name, the `bitcoinII-d` → `bitcoinIId` install from a tarball laid out like the release, the BTCS release asset name and its `bin/` layout, an archive whose checksum is not the pin being refused, the legacy-wallet scan for both BC2 and BTCS, and the fork check against a mocked `bitcoinii-cli`

  The coin-upgrade assertions were mutation-checked, and each reverted fix fails its test: old asset name, no rename on install, no `reconsiderblock`, old LTC target, and the BC2 checksum call removed. `bash -n` passes on every edited script.
- **Shell, `tests/test_script_integrity.sh`:** 25/25. It checks that version pins agree across install.sh, Docker, coin-upgrade.sh and the harness, and it gained fifteen guards this release:
  - the three checksum pins agree across install.sh, Docker and coin-upgrade.sh (changing one fails it)
  - `install.sh`'s `verify_sha256` accepts the pinned hash and refuses a mismatch or an unreadable file
  - every daemon config `install.sh` writes with `rpcpassword=$<COIN>_RPC_PASSWORD` is preceded by `ensure_rpc_password` for the same coin (all 30)
  - every coin the solo menu can select has an entry in the solo wallet setup (all 14)
  - every installable coin has an entry in the `spiralpool-wallet` generator, so option [2] can create its address (all 15). The coin list comes from the guard file's coin map, which the first guard already checks against `install.sh`.
  - the installer's own code never uses `$LOG_FILE`, which only its generated scripts define
  - the sync status box shown before the live view lists every installable coin (all 15)
  - both dashboard units have no ordering on stratum, disable gunicorn's control socket and contain no `\-` escapes, and `start_services` starts the health monitor after the dashboard. Breaking each of the four fails it.
  - the regtest harness uses no production database login and kills nothing by a pattern that matches the real pool. Breaking each of the three fails it.
  - `install.sh` and `docker/Dockerfile` pin the same `GO_VERSION` and `POSTGRES_VERSION`, and every `postgres:` image in `docker/` is that same major. Breaking each of the three — a drifted Go ARG, a removed ARG, an image left on an older major — fails it.

  Each of the last five was broken on purpose and fails: removing one coin's `ensure_rpc_password`, renaming the solo `BTCS` entry, dropping `btcs` from the generator, putting back the `tee -a "$LOG_FILE"`, and removing Bitcoin Silver from the status box. The solo-entry guard at first reported five coins as missing, because each entry contains a nested `case` and it stopped reading at the first inner one; it now tracks nesting, as the generator guard does.
- **Shell, `tests/test_coin_upstream.sh`:** 57/57, new this release. It runs `coin-upgrade.sh`'s real upstream-detection functions — extracted from the shipped file, not copied — against a stubbed `_http_get`, so no test touches a release feed: those change without warning, rate-limit, and go down, and none of that should turn the suite red. It covers both source types, because not every project publishes on GitHub: the GitHub releases API, and an HTTP directory index for a project like Bitcoin whose binaries come from bitcoincore.org. Nine release-candidate tag shapes are rejected (`v29.2.0rc1`, `v0.21.5-rc1`, `31.0rc2`, `v1.0.0-beta`, `v2.0.0-alpha.1`, `v31.1-pre`, `v31.1.0-dev`, `nightly-20260101`, `v31.1.0+build5`) and the four real tag prefixes in use are accepted (`v`, `version`, `nc`, and a bare four-part number). It also pins the behaviours that make the feature safe rather than merely present: `10.0` beats `9.99` as integers rather than strings, a cached answer survives the feed going away, a failed fetch is never written to the cache as an answer, and a total outage prints "Could not check" rather than the all-clear.

  Mutation-checked: reverting the directory-index match to its first version — which stopped at the first non-digit, so `bitcoin-core-32.0rc1/` yielded a clean-looking `32.0` — fails two of them. That was a real bug caught by writing these tests, and it is the worst shape this feature can fail in: a release candidate laundered into a stable version number and announced as a new release.
- **Shell, `tests/test-dgb-926-upgrade.sh`:** 36/36 on the DigiByte VM, against a real v9.26.5 daemon — version pins, the pinned download hash, a regtest boot in both full and pruned configurations, and the full→pruned config migration. It gained the assertion that mattered most and had been missing: **a pruned node is asked for a block template using the pool's own rule set, `segwit` + `digidollar-oracle`, and returns one.** Before this the pruned case only proved the daemon starts and reports `pruned: true` — which says nothing about whether it will still hand the pool a block to mine — and the full-node template check asked for `segwit` alone, so the request `digibyte.go` actually builds had never been made against a real daemon in any configuration. Both now assert it. What this does not reach: regtest has no active DigiDollar deployment, so `default_oracle_commitment` is empty throughout and the copy-into-coinbase path is exercised there only by the four unit tests in `internal/jobs`. **The end-to-end case was instead confirmed on mainnet, from a block the production pool actually found.** DGB block 24,240,943 — mined on 18 September 2026, two months after DigiDollar activated at 23,869,440 — carries three coinbase outputs: the 250.72839494 DGB payout, the SegWit witness commitment (`6a24aa21a9ed…`, the BIP-141 magic), and a second zero-value `OP_RETURN` pushing 191 bytes with no witness magic — the MuSig2 oracle price bundle, copied verbatim by `decodeOracleCommitment`. The network accepted the block and it paid out. No harness can manufacture that case; it had to be found in production, and it was. Worth noting the support itself is not new here: DigiDollar-aware mining shipped in **v2.6.0** (`40ff837`), so every pool from 2.6.0 onward has been mining it correctly — this release adds the pruned-node proof and the stats readout, not the capability.

- **Shell, `tests/test_celebration_led.sh`:** 10 assertions become 24. v2.7.1 recorded that the LED *effect sequences* carried no automated coverage; they now do. All seven — `flash_color`, `pulse_color`, `solid_color`, `fast_pulse`, `rainbow_loop`, `strobe_two`, `color_chase` — are driven with `set_led` and `sleep` stubbed, and the exact write sequence is asserted. These are the part of the celebration where a mistake is perfectly silent: the script succeeds, the miner accepts the command, and the only symptom is an LED doing the wrong thing in a garage nobody is watching at the moment a block lands. The mode number carries the whole risk, since `set_led`'s second argument selects 1=solid, 2=flash, 3=pulse, 4=rainbow, and two pairs sit one value apart and look nothing alike: `solid_color` against `flash_color`, and `pulse_color` against `fast_pulse`, which share mode 3 and differ only in speed (30 against 90). Also pinned: `strobe_two` writes two LEDs per cycle and always ends on its second colour, so no miner is left holding the first; `color_chase` walks its eight spectrum colours in order, since a reshuffle still writes eight valid colours and still looks like nothing is wrong; every effect addresses the IP it was handed and writes full brightness, a zero there being indistinguishable from a dead miner. Mutation-checked twice — changing `flash_color` to mode 1, and duplicating a `color_chase` step so orange is skipped — each of which fails the suite and is caught by exactly one assertion.
- **Shell, `tests/test_apt_post_update_restart.sh`:** 8/8, new. It runs the shipped script against a fake `/proc` and cgroup tree with `systemctl` stubbed: nothing restarts when no service holds a replaced library, a stale gunicorn worker restarts only `spiraldash`, a replaced Python extension module restarts Sentinel, deleted non-library mappings (`memfd`, `/dev/shm`, a replaced binary) are ignored, and a failed restart logs systemctl's reason. All eight fail against the pre-fix script given the `libevent-openssl` upgrade that triggered the report.

- **Shell, `tests/test_docker_integrity.sh`:** 8/8, new this release, and the first automated coverage the Docker deployment has ever had. Every check is static — no daemon, no image build, no network — so it runs on any machine, including ones that will never run the containers. That is a deliberate trade: it cannot say the stack works, only that the files agree with each other and with the installer. The build itself was checked by hand for this release, for the first time. `docker/Dockerfile` built cleanly: `ubuntu:26.04`, Go 1.26.8, the stratum compiled with CGO, `spiralctl`, and the dashboard's Python environment. The image runs and reports `Spiral Stratum Pool 3.0.0`. `generate-secrets.sh` filled 22 passwords, and `docker compose config` parsed the stack. One gap found: the image stamps `Git Commit: unknown`, because `ARG GIT_COMMIT` defaults to that and nothing passes it, so a built image carries no provenance. It checks that `docker-compose.yml` parses and defines 21 well-formed services, that every `depends_on` names a service that exists, that the 13 coin versions pinned in the Dockerfiles match the versions `install.sh` installs, that every long-running service declares a restart policy, and that the environment Sentinel is handed is an environment Sentinel reads. It also pins the Docker degradation contract: `Dockerfile.sentinel` deliberately does **not** ship `coin-upgrade.sh` — that script reads coin versions by executing daemon binaries which live in other containers, so it would answer `not_installed` for every coin and turn a missing capability into a confident wrong answer — and the guard asserts the `upstream_ok` branch survives, since without it a Docker Sentinel would count every coin as a failed feed and eventually raise the version-check-failing alert about a check it was never able to run.

- **Fixed — a Docker pool could never learn that a coin daemon had been released.** Sentinel's upstream release check runs `coin-upgrade.sh --list-upstream`, and `_collect_upstream` skipped any coin whose binary it could not execute. In the Sentinel container there are no coin binaries at all — the fifteen daemons each live in their own container — so every coin was skipped, the mode printed nothing, and Sentinel could not tell that from a healthy check that found no news. A Docker pool was therefore told "nothing newer" about every coin, forever, in exactly the silence a working check produces. This is the failure mode this release exists to remove, reproduced one layer down in the deployment nobody here runs day to day. Four things now line up, and the new guard checks each: `Dockerfile.sentinel` ships `coin-upgrade.sh` at the path Sentinel resolves and installs `curl`, which `python:3.14-slim` does not carry and without which every release feed reports unreachable; `docker-compose.yml` sets `SPIRALPOOL_INSTALLED_COINS`, defaulting to the compose profiles the stack was started with, since those names are the coin tickers; and `_coin_present_for_upstream` honours it. The override is read in exactly one place, so a wrong value can at worst produce a release notice for a coin the pool does not run — it can never cause an upgrade, which under Docker is a rebuild of that coin's image. Only the read-only half of the script is reachable there: `--list-upstream` returns before `check_root` and never touches a daemon, and a guard asserts that ordering holds. Verified end to end on a machine with no coin binaries, which is the container's situation exactly: without the override the check returns in 1.15s having skipped everything, and with it takes 4.61s querying six real release feeds and reports correctly that nothing newer exists. (`coin-upgrade.sh`, `docker/Dockerfile.sentinel`, `docker/docker-compose.yml`, `docs/setup/DOCKER_GUIDE.md`, `tests/test_coin_upstream.sh`, `tests/test_docker_integrity.sh`.)

- **Fixed — the Docker container passed Sentinel thirteen settings it never read.** `docker-compose.yml` hands the sentinel service a `<SYMBOL>_WALLET_ADDRESS` for all sixteen coins, and Sentinel read three of them: `BCH2`, `BTCS` and `XEC` happened to be written as `os.environ.get(...)` in `DEFAULT_CONFIG` while the other thirteen were plain `"YOUR_<X>_ADDRESS"` placeholders. `WEBHOOK_HEADERS` was the same story — a real config key with no environment override behind it. So an operator who set `BTC_POOL_ADDRESS` in `.env` got a container that dutifully forwarded it and a monitor that ignored it: no error, no warning, and a wallet nobody was watching. Per-coin addresses are now resolved from the coins list by symbol, so the list cannot drift again as coins are added (`DGB-SCRYPT` is looked up as `DGB_SCRYPT_WALLET_ADDRESS`, since environment variables cannot carry a hyphen), and `WEBHOOK_HEADERS` is parsed as JSON — a malformed value is ignored with a warning rather than silently replacing configured headers with none, which would have sent authenticated webhooks unauthenticated. Verified by running the new guard against the pre-fix file, where it reports all thirteen by name. (`src/sentinel/SpiralSentinel.py`, `tests/test_docker_integrity.sh`.)

- **Wallet address verification and loading:**
  - `tests/test_wallet_fallback.sh` goes from 41 to 98 assertions. It runs the generator's verification block, extracted from `install.sh`, against a fake coin CLI in 11 cases: valid and owned; invalid; daemon unreachable; owned by another wallet; ownership unknown; an old daemon reporting `ismine` from `validateaddress`; a manual address that is invalid, unreachable, or not in the node wallet; and an HA-pulled address, valid or invalid. It runs both `verify_generated_address` helpers through six cases each, checks that ownership is asked of the named wallet, and checks that every generation site calls the helper (2 in `wait-for-node.sh`, 3 in `pool-mode.sh`).
  - It runs the health monitor's service checks against a fake `systemctl`: an optional service or coin daemon that is "activating" is left alone, one that is inactive or failed is restarted, and stratum returns while starting before its not-running restart. Four breakages each fail it: the check removed from each of the three sites, and "activating" no longer recognized.
  - It also runs the health monitor's wallet check, extracted from `install.sh`, against a fake daemon that remembers loaded wallets. An unloaded wallet is loaded once with `load_on_startup`, in both wallet directory layouts. A loaded wallet, a node with no pool wallet, and a daemon that is down are left alone. An old daemon gets the wallet loaded by name, and a wallet that will not load warns once. It also checks that the daemon health check calls it.
  - `tests/test_upgrade_regression.sh` runs `upgrade.sh`'s new refresh block against a scratch install: old copies of both scripts are replaced, the monitor matches `install.sh`'s script byte for byte, a second run changes nothing, and a release whose monitor fails `bash -n`, or from which nothing can be extracted, leaves the installed copy in place.
  - `tests/test_generate_wallet_verification.py` (7 tests) drives the dashboard's Generate wallet endpoint against a fake node: the config is written only for a valid, owned address, including an old daemon's `ismine` and the default-wallet fallback.

  Ten deliberate breakages each fail them: the generator accepting an invalid address, only warning on unknown ownership, or holding a manual address to ownership; `wait-for-node.sh` ignoring ownership or not verifying the aux-chain address; `pool-mode.sh` ignoring validity or not naming the wallet; and the dashboard accepting unknown ownership or ignoring validity. The aux-chain breakage passed at first, because only the helper was tested and not its callers; the call-site check was added for it. Eight more fail the monitor and upgrade tests: the wallet not saved for startup, a loaded wallet reloaded, the check not called, a warning every minute, no fallback for old daemons, `wait-for-node.sh` not copied, a broken monitor not refused, and an unchanged monitor rewritten.
- **Linux, first test install** (a fresh VM, solo Bitcoin Silver, Stratum V2 enabled):
  - The installer built the stratum and passed both of its Go test phases, the core packages and the HA and payment tests under the race detector. A failing run stops the install to ask, and this one did not.
  - Bitcoin Silver 31.1.3 started and synced to the network tip (height 222,898) within about five minutes.
  - The install then hit the solo wallet bug described under **Fixed — pre-existing bugs**: no wallet prompt, an empty RPC password and a stalled RPC wait. The password was then set by hand, so the rest of that install does not count as a clean run.
  - `spiralctl v2 pubkey` failed with `Unknown command: v2` (see **Changed — Stratum V2 follows the specification**).
- **Linux, second test install** (a fresh Ubuntu 26.04.1 VM, solo Bitcoin Silver):
  - The first two stratum builds failed with `permission denied` on every package directory in the copied source. The cause was not found; by the time it was checked the directories were readable, and the next run built.
  - The solo wallet prompt for Bitcoin Silver appeared.
  - 1837 of 1838 Go tests passed. `TestMetricsClassificationDeadlineExpiry` failed because its 100 ms timer fired at 120 ms, and the test allowed at most 110 ms. The test is unchanged since v1.0; its upper bound is now 5× the deadline, because a late timer only means the machine is busy and would otherwise make a new operator's install stop and ask. The lower bound, which catches a timer firing early, is unchanged.
  - The install reached the sync live view and Bitcoin Silver began syncing. An error printed just before that screen was cleared by it before it could be read, and the installer keeps no log. Reviewing those steps found the four install bugs listed under **Fixed — pre-existing bugs** after the Bitcoin Silver entry; which of them printed that error was not confirmed.
- **Linux, Bitcoin Silver regtest** (`regtest-btcs.sh` on the second VM, HA VIP test skipped): passes end to end after the fixes above. Nine blocks mined through stratum, submitblock, ZMQ and the database; block lifecycle 6/6; payment pipeline 8/8; HA fencing 4/4; daemon-down resilience 4/4; SOLO payout verified, with the coinbase paying the miner's own address and not the pool's; and the Stratum V2 test miner's share accepted over the Noise handshake against a real coin daemon rather than a test job source. Over a 2,400-block run the pool logged no failed block status update. Two things this did not establish: the daemon-reconnect check passed on its fallback (the pool was still serving stratum) rather than by matching a recovery line, though the pool plainly had reconnected -- it mined again a minute later -- and the patterns it looks for now include what the pool writes on recovery; and only Bitcoin Silver was run, so the other coins' regtests remain untried on this release.
- **Linux, live upgrade runs** (`upgrade.sh --full --local` on the DigiByte VM, four consecutive runs): the upgrade path is now exercised on a real install rather than only in the harness. A `dbcache` set to 4096 was restored to 8192 with a timestamped backup taken first, the systemd drop-in was written at `MemoryMax=13G` to match, and `daemon-reindex.sh` was installed root-owned with its sudoers entry. The next run over the same box changed nothing -- `All daemon resource limits OK`, no config rewritten, the reindex script skipped as already matching -- which is the idempotency check the harness cannot make. The preflight reported `spiralstratum: activating` and `digibyted: active` before starting, the summary said `Starting` rather than `Stopped` for a pool still waiting on its node, and the sentinel line read `was stopped before the upgrade and is not enabled` instead of warning. After the build fixes, every `compile` process ran as `spiraluser` with its module cache under that account's home and no fallback warning, and the operator's source directory kept its own ownership. Coin daemons are never stopped by an upgrade, so the node stayed synced throughout all four runs. Both gaps this entry used to record are now closed. A clean **v2.7.1 to v3.0.0** upgrade ran on the final build against a single-coin DigiByte box: version detection read 2.7.1 and targeted 3.0.0 from the local tree, the Go toolchain was upgraded in place from 1.26.4 to the required 1.26.8 before the build rather than building against the older one, PostgreSQL took the same-major patch branch instead of the dump-and-restore migration, the Stratum V2 opt-out prompt closed port 3334 on a `n` answer, five fallback peers were inserted into `digibyte.conf`, and the coin version cache was seeded. The pool then started and mined -- miners connected and shares were accepted -- which is the post-upgrade mining this entry previously could not claim. Two things it still does not prove: five accepted shares demonstrate that the new binary serves work and validates it, not that it is stable under load over days; and that `digibyte.conf` carries no `[main]`/`[test]` headers at all, so the peer insertion landing in the top-level section confirms the write works without exercising the section-aware path the fix above was actually written for.
- **Not covered:**
  - the daemon address checks are now proven against a real daemon, not a fake CLI: `verify_generated_address`, extracted from the installed `wait-for-node.sh` and run against a live DigiByte node, verified an address the node's wallet owns (exit 0), refused a valid address the wallet does not own (exit 1), and refused a fabricated one with the daemon's own encoding error (exit 1). The wallet used was created for the check and removed afterwards; the pool's configured address is held off that node, so it is correctly reported as not owned there
  - the timer test change was checked on the development machine only (3 runs), not re-run on the VM
  - the fixes from the first test install (solo wallet entries, `ensure_rpc_password`, the V2 key in the install summary, `spiralctl v2` in the wrapper) are checked only by the guards above, apart from the wallet prompt seen in the second install
  - no wallet has been generated against a real node for any coin: `spiralpool-wallet` and its address checks have not run live
  - no complete Linux install yet; no `ufw` rules checked, no `pool-mode.sh` run, and no coin daemon other than Bitcoin Silver and DigiByte started
  - the Docker images were not built
  - the `pool-mode.sh` Python change was reviewed but not executed (no interpreter on the development machine)
  - the `--reindex` shortcut change has no automated test
  - two real miners were commanded through `spiralctl miner control`:
    - an Avalon Nano 3S: `power low`, `normal` and `high` and `restart` were accepted, and sleep was refused as described above
    - a NerdQAxe++ on firmware v1.1.0: `power normal` wrote its default 600 MHz / 1150 mV, which the miner applied after restarting, replacing the operator's 625 MHz. `power low` was refused without changes, because this firmware reports no eco preset (`ecoFrequency` 0).

    Every other family is untested on hardware. The Automation page has since been opened in a browser and renders: schedules, the miner table with its per-device capabilities, and the auto-restart settings.
  - Stratum V2 was checked against the Stratum Reference Implementation's software clients, and then against real SV2 firmware — a NerdQAxe++ on a live DigiByte node, over an extended channel, with shares accepted. One miner, one coin, no soak test.
  - Bitcoin Silver 31.1.3 has run and synced only in the first test install above. RPC was not seen answering there even after the password was set by hand (the daemon was still reloading when checked), and no wallet was generated and no share or block mined against it

### Planned for v3.0.0
- **DigiByte 9.26.6** once a final release exists. It must be deployed before block 24,490,000 (about 1 November 2026); upstream commits to final files at least 14 days before activation (about 18 October).
- **Stratum V2 on real hardware — done, see below.** A NerdQAxe++ on NerdQ firmware v1.1.0 mined SV2 against a live DigiByte node and had shares accepted, over an extended channel, at a difficulty the pool derived from the device. Block submission is not implemented separately for V2. A V2 share goes through `HandleMultiPortShare` — the same validation, block detection, assembly and submission path as V1. A unit test mines a nonce meeting both the share and network targets over an extended channel, then asserts the assembled block's first 80 bytes match the header the miner hashed, and that it pays that channel's miner and nobody else. The regtest harness also gained a check that a V2 block reaches the daemon, described below, and it has been run: on Bitcoin Core 31.1 regtest, a V2 share mined to the network target moved the chain tip from height 8 to 9 with cpuminer paused, so nothing else could have moved it. The `submitblock` round trip from a V2 share is therefore a direct observation rather than an argument from shared code with V1. Observed on SHA-256d, for BTC. DigiByte SHA-256d cannot be tested this way — its daemon only mines Scrypt in regtest — and no real SV2 miner has been observed landing a block, only having shares accepted.

A V2 block pays the configured wallet unless `payout_from_worker_name` is on. The gate is the same as V1's: the job manager attaches a `PayoutScript` only when the switch is set, and `CoinBase2For` falls back to the default coinbase without one. No V2 path lets a miner on the stratum port name itself after an address and take the reward.

The miner declares a `nominal_hash_rate` of 1 TH/s while running at 4.98 TH/s, so a difficulty derived from it opens roughly five times too easy. Vardiff corrects this within a minute. Treat the field as an approximation.

V2 remains opt-in. Testing covers one miner on one coin. There is no coverage for a multi-coin pool, a V2 miner across a chain reorg, or more than one channel under load.
- **Native ARM64 packaging**, deferred.

---

## [v2.7.1] - 2026-08-30 - Spiral Citadel

Reporting and celebration release. Nothing here touches share validation, difficulty, routing or payouts; every defect is in what the pool *says about itself*, which is why all of them survived so long. Two made a smart-port coin look idle on `/api/pools` while it was the active rotation slot and genuinely earning, seven left Avalon LEDs showing plain white through a block celebration that was sending correct RGB the entire time, one had Sentinel retry an impossible miner restart on every monitor cycle for as long as the miner stayed offline, and one had Sentinel announce a ZMQ outage on every start and then stay deaf to the real ones. Stratum, Sentinel, dashboard and script changes only: **no database migrations, no config format changes**. Drop-in upgrade from v2.7.0.

The `block-celebrate.sh` LED fixes were applied in place on the v2.7.0 tag before this release was cut, so an operator who pulled that tag recently already has them. The test-endpoint, Sentinel cooldown, ZMQ-alerting and celebration-LED fixes landed afterwards, in place on this tag. **The celebration LED now goes dark when no block is being celebrated** — see `led_idle_state` below if you want the previous restore-what-was-there behaviour.

### Fixed — per-coin statistics
- **A coin pool whose daemon was not ready when the smart port started never had its multi-port session provider wired, so every smart-port worker it served stayed invisible to `connectedMiners`.** `startMultiPort()` installs the `multiPortSessionAdapter` on each CoinPool present in `c.pools` at the moment it runs. Coins that are still starting join later through `retryFailedCoinsLoop`, which registers them with the multi-server and the difficulty monitor — so they are routed real work and genuinely mine — but never called `SetMultiPortSessionProvider`. `CoinPool.GetConnections()` guards its multi-port merge on `cp.multiPortSessions != nil`, so for those coins it returned only the dedicated listener's `ActiveConnections`: zero for any coin nobody connects to directly. The same nil guard suppressed smart-port workers from `GetActiveConnections()`, so `/api/pools/{id}/connections` under-reported for the same pools. The late-start path now wires the provider alongside the multi-server registration. Worth stating because the field report's correlation was confounded: the reporter observed that only coins which had *never taken a dedicated-port connection* were affected, but that is not the mechanism — the actual predictor is whether the coin's daemon was ready at smart-port startup. The two travel together in practice, since a large chain (BCH, BTC, FBTC) is far more likely to still be starting than a fast small one, and a coin with its own direct traffic was typically up first. A coin with dedicated miners would have shown the same defect had its daemon come up late. This is the same user-visible symptom as [#6](https://github.com/SpiralPool/Spiral-Pool/issues/6), fixed in v2.5.x by introducing `MultiPortSessionProvider`; that mechanism was correct, and what is fixed here is a path along which it was never attached. (`internal/pool/coordinator.go`; reported by [Pencreus](https://github.com/Pencreus), [#19](https://github.com/SpiralPool/Spiral-Pool/issues/19).)
- **`poolEffort` read 0 permanently for any coin that had not yet found a block, however long it had been submitting shares.** Effort is `(time since last block / expected time to find one) × 100`, and `GetPoolEffort()` returned 0 outright when `lastBlockTime` was zero. That field is only ever set by a block the running process found, or seeded at startup from `GetLastBlockFoundTimeForPool`; a pool that has never found a block has neither, so the zero was permanent — precisely for the pools whose operator most wants the number, namely one still waiting on its first block. Effort now falls back to the pool's start time, treating the round as having begun when the pool did. Two honest limitations: the figure resets on restart until the first block lands, and it is an approximation rather than the true round start, which is unrecoverable because startup pruning clears shares older than 15 minutes. It still returns 0 before `Start()` has run, rather than inventing effort from a zero timestamp. This is independent of the connection-count defect above — the field report proposed that both flowed from the same missing session data, and that is not the case. (`internal/pool/coinpool.go`; reported by [Pencreus](https://github.com/Pencreus), [#19](https://github.com/SpiralPool/Spiral-Pool/issues/19).)

### Fixed — Avalon LED block celebration
- **The dashboard drove the same LEDs as the celebration script, in an incompatible CGMiner dialect, and won.** `broadcast_block_found()` fired its own hour-long LED routine on every block using `ascset|0,led,1` / `ascset|0,led,0`, plain on/off, while `block-celebrate.sh` was driving the same device with `ascset|0,ledset,<mode>-<brightness>-<speed>-<r>-<g>-<b>`. In the dashboard's "RAVE MODE" that on/off pair fires every 50ms, so the RGB sequence never survived long enough to be seen and the LED sat at its default white. The dashboard path was written on the premise, stated in its own docstring, that Avalon LEDs are single-colour; an Avalon Nano 3s on cgminer 4.11.1 answers `STATUS=I ... led set ok` to `ledset`, so that premise is wrong at least for that device. `broadcast_block_found()` no longer drives LEDs, the script owns them, and the docstring is corrected rather than deleted since it is what would stop the next reader from trying. (`src/dashboard/dashboard.py`.)
- **Celebration miner discovery scanned the entire local /24, so two independent pools sharing a LAN each drove the other's miners.** Discovery took the host's own address, masked off the last octet and probed all 254 addresses for anything listening on 4028 — with a lock file in `/run/spiralpool/` that cannot coordinate across machines. Discovery now reads the host's own inventory from `/spiralpool/data/miners.json` and falls back to the subnet scan only when that file is absent or holds no miners. (`scripts/linux/block-celebrate.sh`.)
- **An overlapping celebration killed the running one and could then record a celebration colour as the LED's "original" state.** The script is launched independently by the stratum, by Sentinel and by hand, so a single block reaches it several times. The old behaviour sent SIGTERM to the incumbent, slept a fixed 2 seconds, then re-read the LED to save as the state to restore at the end. The dying process has to kill its children, wait on them, and push a `set_led` with a 2-second `nc` timeout, so it can still be mid-restore when that sleep expires; the new run then captures whatever colour is live — white in five of the twenty-four phases — and faithfully restores to it hours later. A second launch now extends a shared deadline file and exits, leaving the incumbent untouched.
- **Every early exit released a lock the process did not own.** `cleanup()` runs on the `EXIT` trap and removed the lock unconditionally, so a launch that bailed out on quiet hours, `--list`, `--help` or "no miners found" evicted a celebration another process was legitimately running. It now removes the lock only when the file contains its own PID.
- **A miner that did not report `LEDUser` had its LED turned off at the end of every celebration.** `get_led_state()` fell back to `0-100-50-0-253-255`, commented "Default cyan", but the first field is the mode and `0` is off. It now returns empty, and both the normal restore and the signal handler skip restoring a state that was never known.
- **`set_led` discarded the miner's reply, so a rejected command was indistinguishable from a successful one.** Every call redirected to `/dev/null`; a device that rejected `ledset` outright would have produced exactly the observed symptom with no trace anywhere. It now inspects the reply and warns once per miner, deliberately still returning 0 because the script runs under `set -euo pipefail` and a transient `nc` timeout must not abort a multi-hour celebration. Relatedly, the script logs everything to stderr and both automated callers discarded it (Sentinel's `Popen` with `DEVNULL`, the stratum's `exec.Command` with a nil `Stdout`), so no celebration ever left a record; output now appends to `/spiralpool/logs/celebrate.log` when not attached to a terminal.
- **The dashboard's manual test-celebration button ran the on/off path, so a "test" showed plain white and told the operator nothing about what a real block would look like.** `/api/test/block-celebration` drove its own copy of the `ascset|0,led,1` / `led,0` routine — the same incompatible dialect that was overwriting the RGB sequence on a real block find — against the Avalons in the dashboard config. It now launches `block-celebrate.sh` the way Sentinel and the stratum do, detached, with `--duration`, `--miners` set to the configured Avalon IPs and `--force` when the caller passes `{"force": true}`, so the test exercises exactly the code a block runs. The two dashboard-side celebration routines and their thread guards are deleted rather than left dormant; the script owns the LEDs. A test fired while a celebration is already running now extends that celebration's deadline instead of replacing it, which the response says. On a host without the script — Windows, or an incomplete install — the endpoint reports that plainly instead of silently doing nothing.

### Fixed — celebration LED discovery and idle state
- **`is_cgminer` probed with a command Avalon MM firmware rejects, so a subnet scan discovered none of them.** The check sent the plain-text `version` and required the reply to contain `"CGMiner"`. An Avalon Nano3s on cgminer 4.11.1 (MM319) answers that with `STATUS=E,Code=14,Msg=Invalid command` — while answering the JSON dialect `{"command":"version"}` in full, and while accepting plain-text `ascset|0,ledset,...` with `Code=118 led set ok`. So the device is completely drivable and was simply never identified. Installs were unaffected only because the v2.7.1 discovery fix made `get_miners` prefer `/spiralpool/data/miners.json`; anyone falling through to the subnet scan got a celebration that reported success and drove zero miners. The probe now falls back to the JSON form when the plain-text one does not identify the device. (`scripts/linux/block-celebrate.sh`.)
- **`get_led_state` read the LED with a command Avalon MM firmware rejects, so a celebration ended by deliberately leaving the LED lit.** Same dialect problem as the discovery probe: `stats` in plain text draws `Code=14 Invalid command` from a Nano3s, while `{"command":"stats"}` returns the `LEDUser[...]` field normally. An absent field is indistinguishable from "this miner reports no LED state", and the v2.7.1 change had — correctly, on its own terms — made that case *skip* the restore rather than guess mode 0. The two combined into the opposite failure: a real celebration logged `Saved state for 192.168.1.14:` with nothing after the colon, ran its full three hours, then logged `No saved LED state for 192.168.1.14 — leaving LED as-is` and exited cleanly, stranding the miner on `LEDUser[3-100-90-0-255-255]` — byte for byte what phase 22 (`fast_pulse 0 255 255`) writes — for six hours until it was cleared by hand. Nothing crashed and no signal was missed; the script did exactly what it was told. `get_led_state` now falls back to the JSON dialect before concluding a miner has no LED state. (`scripts/linux/block-celebrate.sh`.)
- **There was no way to ask for the LED to simply be dark between blocks.** The end of a celebration only ever restored the pre-celebration state, which in practice is usually just whatever the previous celebration left. A new `led_idle_state` key in the Sentinel config, read by the script the same way quiet hours already are, now decides: `"off"` (the default) puts the LED into mode 0 when the celebration ends, `"restore"` keeps the old behaviour. Both the normal end-of-celebration path and the signal handler go through one `restore_idle_led` helper, so they can no longer disagree. The two modes are asymmetric on purpose: with `"restore"` a state that was never captured is left untouched rather than guessed — guessing is what the v2.7.1 `"Default cyan"` fallback did, whose leading `0` was mode OFF rather than a colour — whereas with `"off"` there is nothing to know and the LED simply goes dark. That also makes `"off"` immune to the read failure above, since it never needs to know the previous state. **This changes behaviour on upgrade**: LEDs that used to return to their prior state now go off. (`scripts/linux/block-celebrate.sh`, `src/sentinel/SpiralSentinel.py`.)

### Fixed — Sentinel auto-restart
- **A miner that could not be restarted remotely was retried on every monitor cycle, indefinitely.** The offline auto-restart gate compared `auto_restart_cooldown` (default 30 minutes) against `get_last_restart_time()`, and that timestamp is written only after `restart_miner()` returns success. A device whose restart always fails — no restart API, wrong credentials, firmware that ignores the command — therefore never recorded anything for the cooldown to measure, so the gate stayed open and Sentinel re-attempted the restart, and logged the failure, every cycle for as long as the miner stayed offline. The attempt is now recorded in a separate `miner_restart_attempts` map before it is made, and the gate reads the later of attempt and success. Failed attempts stay out of `miner_restart_times`, so restart statistics, the self-healer badges and the excessive-restart alert continue to count only restarts that actually happened. The map persists across restarts and is pruned with the other per-miner state.

### Fixed — Sentinel ZMQ alerting
- **`stratum_zmq_connected` was registered, exported and scraped, but never once written — so Sentinel announced "ZMQ CONNECTION LOST" on every start and then went deaf to the real thing.** `Metrics.SetZMQConnected()` had no caller outside its own unit tests. Both ZMQ status handlers — `CoinPool`'s via `nodeManager.SetZMQStatusHandler`, `Pool`'s via `zmqListener.SetStatusChangeHandler` — updated `stratum_zmq_health_status` and stopped there. A Prometheus gauge that is never `Set` reads 0, so the metric said "disconnected" on every install, permanently, whatever ZMQ was actually doing. Sentinel alerted on `zmq_health >= 3 or zmq_connected == 0`, and the right-hand side was a tautology: the alert fired on the first infrastructure poll after every Sentinel start, quoting whatever health status was live at that moment — usually `Connecting`, the normal state of a freshly started listener that has not yet received its first block notification. The more damaging half is the clear branch, `zmq_health <= 2 and zmq_connected == 1`, which could never evaluate true. `zmq_disconnected_alerted` latched on at that first false alarm and stayed on, so a genuinely degraded or failed ZMQ afterwards was suppressed until the process restarted: a false positive that silently disarmed the true positive it was meant to precede. Both handlers now set the gauge from a new `ZMQStatus.IsConnected()`, which counts `Healthy` and `Connecting` — the socket is open in both, it has simply not seen traffic yet — and excludes `Disabled`, `Degraded` and `Failed`. Sentinel's alert now keys on health status alone, which is the richer signal and was correctly wired the whole time, and clears on anything below degraded. Two other consumers were reading the dead gauge and are fixed by the wiring rather than by any change of their own: the dashboard's `pool_health["zmq_status"]`, which reported `disconnected` on every install, and the Grafana rule `spiralpool-zmq-disconnected`, which was firing continuously — its `for: 2m` window now spans the connecting period instead of being defeated by it. (`internal/daemon/zmq.go`, `internal/daemon/zmq_stub.go`, `internal/pool/coinpool.go`, `internal/pool/pool.go`, `src/sentinel/SpiralSentinel.py`.)

### Fixed — version reporting
- **The dashboard footer still read `v2.7.0`.** A hardcoded string in `dashboard.html`, missed when the release was cut; every other version marker in shipped code — `VERSION`, `install.sh`, `upgrade.sh`, `spiralctl.sh`, `dashboard.py`, `SpiralSentinel.py`, `ha_manager.py` and the `scripts/linux/` set — was already correct. Cosmetic, but it is the version an operator reads back when reporting a bug. (`src/dashboard/templates/dashboard.html`.)

### Tests
- Five added in `internal/pool/coinpool_multiport_stats_test.go`. Both effort tests were verified to fail against the pre-fix code, each returning the reported `0`. The two connection tests pin the contract — that a wired pool counts its own smart-port sessions and does not absorb another coin's — but would **not** have caught the defect, which lives in the coordinator's wiring rather than in `GetConnections()` itself; exercising that path needs a coordinator with a failed-coin retry loop and is not covered here.
- Eleven added in `tests/test_restart_cooldown.py` covering the auto-restart cooldown: that a failed attempt now holds the gate shut for the full cooldown, that the gate takes the later of attempt and success, that legacy scalar and corrupt timestamps are handled, and that failed attempts do not inflate the restart counters or trip the excessive-restart alert. The first of these fails against the pre-fix gate.
- One added in `internal/daemon/zmq_test.go`, pinning which states `IsConnected()` treats as a live socket — in particular that `Connecting` counts, since treating it as disconnected is what produced the false alert. The Sentinel-side condition has no automated coverage: it is four lines inline in the monitor loop rather than a callable function, and extracting it to make it testable would be a larger change than the fix. It was verified by reading against the five health states.
- Ten added in `tests/test_celebration_led.sh`, covering all three celebration fixes: which states `restore_idle_led` writes under each `led_idle_state`, that `is_cgminer` identifies an Avalon Nano3s whose plain-text `version` returns `Code=14`, and that `get_led_state` falls back to the JSON dialect rather than reporting no LED state. The Nano3s cases for both probes were verified to fail against the pre-fix code, returning exactly the field symptoms — undiscovered miner, empty saved state. The script guards `main()` behind a `BASH_SOURCE` check, so the test sources it and calls the functions directly with the CGMiner API stubbed.
- The LED *effect sequences* still carry no automated coverage. The deadline, lock-ownership and restore-skip logic were unit-tested during development, and the test endpoint's argument building was checked against a stubbed `Popen` — but the phase sequences themselves were never exercised against hardware and no Avalon is available in CI.

---

## [v2.7.0] - 2026-08-16 - Spiral Citadel

Chain-identity release. On 8 August 2026 Bitcoin split at block 961,632 over BIP-110 ("RDTS", the Reduced Data Temporary Softfork), and Spiral Pool was shipping a Bitcoin Knots build that enforces it. Every operator who installed or upgraded BTC since roughly 8 May was pointed at a daemon that follows a minority chain which has produced only a handful of blocks in total and whose coins are not traded anywhere. Nothing in the software reports a fault in that state: stratum connects, shares validate, vardiff converges, the dashboard shows healthy hashrate. The only symptom is that blocks never arrive, which for a solo miner is indistinguishable from variance — so the expected value is exactly zero with no way for the operator to find out. This release moves all BTC daemon acquisition to Bitcoin Core, refuses to mine a chain it cannot verify, and repairs nodes already stranded on the wrong one. No database migrations, no config format changes. Drop-in upgrade from v2.6.7.

Also carried on this tag: two miner-classification defects on the stratum side. `MinerClassLottery` was calibrated for hardware roughly four times slower than what actually connects, and NerdNOS was not classified at all — both left a miner holding a difficulty its hardware could not work at, and in neither case could vardiff correct it, because every retarget path runs from an accepted share and these miners land none. Reported against DigiByte, where a 15-second block time scales the profile constants down and turns a merely suboptimal value into a failure. Stratum-side only: **no version string change, no migrations, no config changes** — the pool picks the new profiles up on restart.

### Fixed
- **Three code paths shipped the RDTS-enforcing Bitcoin Knots build, and one resolved the version at runtime so it would have drifted onto an enforcing build regardless of any pin.** `docker/Dockerfile.bitcoin` pinned `29.3.knots20260508` with no checksum verification at all; `install.sh` and `coin-upgrade.sh` pinned the same enforcing build. The enforcing and non-enforcing releases differ by **one digit in a datestamp** — `knots20260508` enforces, `knots20260507` does not — and the datestamp is the enforcement marker rather than a release date, so `29.4.knots20260508` also enforces despite the higher base version. That makes any "latest version" lookup actively dangerous, and two existed. `scripts/linux/pool-mode.sh` scraped the bitcoinknots.org directory listing and took `sort -V | tail -1` **unconditionally**, with no checksum, installing straight to `/usr/local/bin/bitcoind` and writing the version cache — so `coin-upgrade.sh` afterwards believed BTC was legitimately on Knots. `install.sh`'s `detect_latest_knots_version()` was functionally the same scrape but opt-in behind `BITCOIN_KNOTS_AUTO_DETECT=1`, with the pinned version used and logged by default. Run today, either selects an enforcing build deterministically. BTC acquisition now pins **Bitcoin Core 31.1** from bitcoincore.org with SHA256 `b80d9c3e04da78fb6f0569685673418cf686fadba9042d926d13fb87ff503f9e` committed in-repo, taken from `bitcoincore.org/bin/bitcoin-core-31.1/SHA256SUMS` and cross-checked byte-for-byte against two independent Guix attestations (`achow101`, `fanquake`) in `bitcoin-core/guix.sigs`. The previous design fetched the checksum from the same host serving the binary, which one compromised host satisfies on both sides. Verification is a **hard gate with no override** in the four production paths — `install.sh`, `coin-upgrade.sh`, `docker/Dockerfile.bitcoin`, `scripts/linux/pool-mode.sh` — and the `ALLOW_UNVERIFIED_BTC_DOWNLOAD=true` escape hatch is removed. `scripts/linux/regtest.sh` and `tests/test-coin-configs.sh` are repointed to Bitcoin Core 31.1 for consistency but, as before, verify no checksum; they build local throwaway chains and are not a production path. Both resolvers are deleted, not bypassed. Note `install.sh`'s BTC download now has a single source (bitcoincore.org) where Knots had two, so a bitcoincore.org outage fails the install rather than falling back. `upgrade.sh`'s `_VC_PREV` map still carries `29.3.knots20260210` — that is a *previous-version* fallback used to seed the version cache when `--version` parsing fails, not an acquisition path, and the value is a non-enforcing build; leaving it makes BTC correctly show as upgradeable.
- **A BTC upgrade could SIGKILL the operator's Bitcoin Cash or Fractal daemon and corrupt its chainstate.** `ensure_daemon_stopped` identified the process with `pgrep -x`/`pkill -x` against `COIN_DAEMON_CMD[$coin]`, i.e. the `/usr/local/bin` symlink name. But BTC, BCH and FBTC all exec a binary literally named `bitcoind` from different directories (see the three `ExecStart` lines `install.sh` writes for BTC, BCH and FBTC), and `pgrep -x` matches the kernel `comm`, so it cannot tell them apart. `systemctl stop bitcoind.service` stopped BTC only; BCH kept running, so the loop never saw the process disappear and escalated to `pkill` (SIGTERM, attempts 4-10) and then `pkill -9` (SIGKILL, attempts 11-20) — against the **Bitcoin Cash** node, mid-UTXO-flush. Once BCH died the check finally passed and the script reported `bitcoind confirmed stopped`, having killed the wrong daemon and verified nothing; BCH then crash-looped on a corrupted LevelDB until systemd's `StartLimitBurst` latched it off. The mirror-image defect also existed: `COIN_DAEMON_CMD[BCH]="bitcoind-bch"` matches no running process at all, so a BCH upgrade read "confirmed stopped" on attempt 1 while the daemon was still live, and `install_binaries` then copied over a running binary with its `cp` exit status unchecked. This was latent: `COIN_TARGET[BTC]` matched the installed Knots build, so `upgrade_coin` hit its already-at-target early return and `ensure_daemon_stopped BTC` had never executed in production. Retargeting BTC to Core makes it reachable. Matching is now anchored to the **resolved binary path** (`pgrep -f "^<path>([[:space:]]|$)"`), and when a path cannot be resolved the function refuses to signal by name if that name is shared with another configured coin, rather than guessing.
- **Swapping the binary is not sufficient to leave the minority chain, and a node that only swaps looks healthier than one that fails.** An RDTS-enforcing daemon marks the majority chain's block 961,632 `BLOCK_FAILED_VALID`, and that status bit is serialised into the block index on disk (`CDiskBlockIndex::SERIALIZE_METHODS` writes `nStatus` verbatim; `LoadBlockIndexGuts` restores it without recomputation). Bitcoin Core reads the same index, honours the inherited verdict, and `FindMostWorkChain` erases the entire branch from `setBlockIndexCandidates` on the flag alone with no work-based override — so the node sits on the minority tip with a far heavier valid chain available and reports nothing wrong. There is exactly one non-test caller of `ResetBlockFailureFlags` in Core, the `reconsiderblock` RPC; no restart hook, no version check, no automatic reconsideration exists. Reproduced on regtest against Bitcoin Core v31.1.0: a node holding a persisted failure flag refused to reorganise onto a chain with three more blocks of work, **still refused after a restart on the same datadir** (the swap), and converged immediately once `reconsiderblock` cleared the flag — with no reindex, no resync, and its pre-split history intact and byte-identical. `coin-upgrade.sh` now verifies `getblockhash 961632` after the swap, sweeps `getchaintips` and reconsiders **every** tip reporting `invalid` rather than one hardcoded hash, then polls for convergence and distinguishes *downloading* from *wedged* before recommending the expensive fallback. Note `-reindex-chainstate` does **not** clear these flags — only a full `-reindex` rebuilds the block index — which is why the `MAJOR` risk class points at the latter. (`coin-upgrade.sh`, `verify_btc_majority_chain`.)
- **`coin-upgrade.sh` reported BTC as up to date and skipped it, so the tool an operator would reach for to fix this told them there was nothing to fix.** `COIN_RISK[BTC]` was `NONE`, meaning "already at target", against a `COIN_TARGET[BTC]` of the enforcing build. `download_BTC()` also went straight from `wget` to `tar -xzf` with no verification of any kind — unlike `install.sh`, which at least attempted it — and hardcoded a `29.x` path segment that would break for any non-29.x version. Now `MAJOR`, targeting Core 31.1, with a mandatory hash gate.
- **Wallet recovery ran the wrong daemon for every coin.** `_recover_salvagewallet` declared its coin→daemon table with `local svc_map=(…)`, which creates an **indexed** array: every `[btc]`-style subscript is arithmetic-evaluated, unset names become `0`, so all fourteen entries collapse onto index 0 and the last one wins. Verified empirically — `btc → ecashd`, `dgb → ecashd`, every key → `ecashd`. Method 4 of the wallet-backup recovery therefore launched the **eCash daemon** pointed at another coin's config and datadir. The `[[ -z "$daemon" ]] && return 1` guard never tripped, because the value was non-empty, merely wrong, and `bash -n` cannot see it — an indexed array with word subscripts is valid syntax. Now `local -A`. Also adds the missing `[cat]` entry, which was absent from the table entirely.
- **The same recovery path passed `-salvagewallet`, removed from Bitcoin Core in 0.19.** Unknown command-line arguments are fatal in Core — this repo documents that model itself — so the daemon exited with a parse error before doing anything, for every Core-derived daemon (BTC, LTC, DGB, SYS, NMC, BCH, BCH2, BC2, BTCS, FBTC, XEC). `2>/dev/null` hid the reason, so the operator saw only `Trying -salvagewallet (BerkeleyDB recovery)... failed`. Combined with the array defect above, method 4 could never succeed for any coin. It now **deliberately does not automate salvage** for modern daemons: `<coin>-wallet salvage` rewrites the wallet file in place, and this code path is only reached because every non-destructive backup already failed — so the wallet is likely damaged and there is no copy. It locates the tool beside the daemon binary, prints the exact commands with a copy-the-wallet-aside step first, and returns failure. The legacy `-salvagewallet` path is retained only for the older daemons (Dogecoin 1.14, PepeCoin 1.1) that still accept the flag, which is the one case that ever worked. The manual-recovery instructions printed on failure were wrong in both halves too: they suggested `-salvagewallet`, and built a config path as `<coin>/<coin>.conf`, which is wrong for every coin whose config is not named after its ticker (BTC's is `bitcoin.conf`, FBTC's is `fractal.conf`). The path is now taken from the CLI invocation rather than reconstructed.
- **`pool-mode.sh` destroyed hand-edited coin configs with no backup.** All thirteen coin branches regenerate their config wholesale with `cat > … << EOF`. Regenerating is intentional — switching pool modes changes ports and merge-mining settings — but any operator customisation (extra `addnode`/`seednode` lines, a raised `dbcache`, custom `maxconnections`, `rpcauth`, `wallet=`, `blocksdir`) was silently and irrecoverably lost, and the only value carried across was pruning, which `get_existing_prune` reads from `coins.env` rather than from the file it was handed. Each branch now takes a timestamped `.bak-<stamp>` copy first, and says so.
- **`upgrade.sh` silently overwrote deliberate `dbcache` and `maxconnections` settings on every run.** The caps exist to stop a node OOMing and remain in force, but an operator who set `dbcache=16384` on a large host had it rewritten with only a `log_info` line and no way back. The edit now takes a timestamped backup of the config before its first change and reports at warning level that an operator value was overridden. (Both edits were already injection-safe — the substitutions interpolate only digits captured by `grep -oP '\K\d+'`, and GNU `sed -i` is temp-file-plus-rename, so neither could corrupt a config or truncate it on an interrupt.)
- **A bare `externalip=` in the Tor config prevented Bitcoin, DigiByte and Litecoin from starting at all.** An empty value is not treated as "unset": the parser stores `""` (`InterpretValue` returns `value ? *value : ""`, and `GetArgs` applies no emptiness filter), the resolve loop passes it to `Lookup()`, which rejects the empty string on an explicit `if (name.empty())` guard, and the `else` branch returns `InitError`. Verified in each project's own source at the exact version installed — Bitcoin Core 31.1 `init.cpp:1803`, DigiByte 9.26.5 `init.cpp:1678`, Litecoin 0.21.5.4 `init.cpp:1510` (the older `bool Lookup` form, same guard) — and independently confirmed fatal in Namecoin 28.0, Syscoin 5.0.5, Myriadcoin 0.18.1.0, Fractal and Bitcoin ABC 0.31.12. With `Restart=always` and `StartLimitBurst=5` in the generated unit this is a crash loop, not a silent failure; it went unnoticed because `TOR_ENABLED` defaults to false. Omitting the option entirely *is* the do-not-advertise behaviour — there is no empty or sentinel form. Ten of the thirteen Tor configs never carried the line, so this restores consistency rather than changing intent. Removal has **no behavioural effect** beyond the daemon starting: `-discover` is soft-set to 0 twice before the `externalip` interaction is reached, once from `-proxy` and once from `listen=0`, and `SoftSetBoolArg` is a no-op once a value is set — so `fDiscover` was already false with the line present. Pre-existing and unrelated to the chain split; surfaced by auditing the configs for the migration. (`install.sh`, BTC/DGB/LTC Tor branches.)

- **Five places parsed `bitcoin.conf` with `grep -oP '^key='`, which is not how Bitcoin Core reads a config file, and one of them could leave a daemon that never starts again.** Core scopes every option under a `[main]`/`[test]`/`[regtest]` header to that network, tolerates whitespace around `=`, and takes the *first* assignment. A bare `^key=` match honours none of that. The worst case was `scripts/linux/pool-mode.sh`: `get_existing_prune` returned **every** match, and the result is interpolated directly into the generated config as `prune=$EXISTING_PRUNE` — so a config with `prune=5000` at the top level and `prune=1000` under `[test]` emitted a bare `1000` line with no `=`, which is a fatal parse error the daemon does not come back from. The same regex missed `prune = 5000` (Core-legal whitespace) and silently rewrote a **pruned** node to `prune=0`, which Core refuses to start against a pruned datadir without a full resync; and it copied a `[test]`-scoped `prune` up to the top level, enabling irreversible block deletion on a full mainnet node — destroying precisely the ability to reorg off the minority chain that this release depends on. `upgrade.sh`'s `rightsize_daemon_resources` had the same defect with a different symptom: a multi-line value made `[[ -gt ]]` throw an arithmetic error, so the `dbcache`/`maxconnections` caps were **silently skipped** and the run reported `All daemon resource limits OK` — a false green on exactly the multi-coin OOM scenario the function exists to prevent. All sites now read the top-level section only, tolerate whitespace, take the first match, strip CR, and accept integers only.
- **`upgrade.sh` appended mainnet peer settings into whatever section a config happened to end in.** `ensure_daemon_peer_config` used `>>`, so on any config ending in `[test]` the `forcednsseed=1` line and all twelve mainnet `addnode=…:8333` entries landed under `[test]`, where mainnet never sees them. Its guard read the whole file, so it then matched the line it had written into the wrong section and reported `All daemon peer configs up to date` on every subsequent run — permanently. The function was added specifically because fresh installs got zero peers; against these configs it never fixed that and said it had. It now inserts before the first section header, and its guards read the top-level section only. `coin-upgrade.sh`'s DGB pruning path had the same shape — file-wide deletes plus an EOF append — and now declines section-bearing configs with manual instructions instead of half-applying an edit and then deleting the transaction index anyway.
- **Two config backups could fail silently and let the edit proceed unbacked-up.** `upgrade.sh`'s `rightsize_daemon_resources` and `coin-upgrade.sh`'s `dgb_enable_pruning_config` both logged a warning on backup failure and continued — overwriting a deliberate operator value, or deleting lines and then removing the transaction index, with no copy on disk. Both now fail closed. Both backups are also `chmod 600` now: these files contain `rpcpassword` in plaintext, and `cp -p` was carrying the original's group-readable 640.
- **A failed daemon start left `coin-upgrade.sh` reporting the coin as healthy.** `systemctl start` was unguarded under `set -e` in both the normal and `--reindex` branches, so a daemon that installed cleanly but failed to start aborted the script mid-statement — skipping `wait_for_daemon`, the config-hazard check, and the chain-identity verification entirely. Because the binary swap had already succeeded, `--check` then read the new version off disk and reported BTC `current` while the daemon was down and the chain had never been verified. In the `--reindex` branch the abort also stranded the one-shot `-reindex` drop-in on disk, which with `Restart=always` is an endless reindex loop. Both are now guarded, the drop-in is always removed, and a failed start reports what did not run.
- **`install.sh`'s offline-install path checked the same location twice and could delete the operator's tarball.** The pre-placed-tarball loop used `$(pwd)`, but `cd /tmp` runs earlier in the same function, so both loop entries resolved to `/tmp` and the documented "drop it next to the installer" location was unreachable — with the failure message printing the identical path twice. `rm -rf /tmp/bitcoin-*` after extraction then matched the staged tarball itself, so the air-gapped workflow worked exactly once. The loop now uses `$SCRIPT_DIR`, cleanup is scoped to the extracted directory, the staging `cp` is guarded (unguarded, it aborted the whole installer under `set -e`), and an unreadable file is reported as unreadable rather than as a checksum mismatch.
- **HA binary replication accepted any binary that was not provably Knots.** The test was a negated grep, so a truncated, wrong-architecture or unexecutable replicated binary produced no output, passed, and got symlinked into `/usr/local/bin` — after which the install reported success and the daemon failed to start. It now requires a positive `Bitcoin Core` identification and discards anything else in favour of the verified download. Relatedly, the Knots probe read only the first line of `--version`; it now reads the whole output, since this is the one check whose failure mode is mining a worthless chain. The `/usr/local/bin` symlinks are also refreshed on the already-at-target path, which previously skipped them — leaving `coin-upgrade.sh --check` reporting BTC as not installed after a repair re-run that was supposed to fix exactly that.
- **The Sentinel chain-identity alert could write thousands of identical ERROR lines a day.** The alert cooldown is deliberately stamped only on successful delivery so a suppressed alert is retried, but the journal line sat outside that gate — so a persistently suppressed alert (quiet hours, maintenance mode, HA replica) logged on every monitor cycle indefinitely. The log line is now throttled on its own latch. The alert also rendered `Block 961,632: unreadable` on the BIP-110-enforcement path, because the block hash was never queried there; enforcement alone is conclusive, but the embed shows the field and "unreadable" reads like the check failed rather than like it found the wrong chain.
- **Smaller correctness fixes.** `install.sh`'s BTC download retry loop reused the variable name `download_with_retry` uses for its own loop, so `max_attempts=3` was really 1 and the retry prompt was unreachable. `pool-mode.sh` wrote config backups keyed on basename alone, so the three coins whose config is named `bitcoin.conf` (btc, bch, xec) overwrote each other's backups; they are now qualified by coin directory. The BTC template emitted no `txindex` line at all, silently dropping `txindex=1` to Core's default of off when a full-archival node's config was regenerated. `assumevalid` was pinned *below* Bitcoin Core 31.1's own shipped value, making initial sync slower than omitting it; the line is removed. `ensure_daemon_stopped`'s fallback matcher could never be reached safely — the guard it depended on compared symlink names that are unique by construction, so it always fell through to a `pgrep -x` that matches the process `comm` and therefore reported BCH as stopped while it was running; the fallback now verifies through systemd and refuses rather than reporting an unverified stop.

- **The version comparison would have silently broken on the next Bitcoin Core release.** Both `coin-upgrade.sh`'s `_ver_matches` and `install.sh`'s already-installed check compared with a trailing `.0` stripped from each side. That is asymmetric: for a future `32.0` target, an installed `32.0.0` strips to `32.0` while the target strips to `32`, so they would not match and every run would re-download and reinstall an already-correct binary — exactly the dead-branch bug the `.0` stripping was added to fix. Both now pad to three components (`X.Y.Z`), which is symmetric. Verified across `31.1.0/31.1`, `32.0.0/32.0`, `31.10.0/31.10` and a genuine mismatch.
- **`install.sh`'s BTC version-cache fallback was unreachable.** `grep … | head -1 || cat BTC.ver || echo unknown` reads as a fallback chain, but a pipeline's exit status is `head`'s, which is 0 even when grep matched nothing — so neither fallback could ever run and an unparseable `--version` yielded an empty string. The result is now tested explicitly.
- **A failed coin upgrade could leave the daemon stopped without saying so.** The upgrade stops the daemon before swapping the binary, but several failure paths — `die` on a malformed archive, an unresolvable binary directory — exit straight through the `EXIT` trap, which cleaned up the work directory and maintenance mode and said nothing about the node being down. The operator saw a one-line error and no indication that mining had stopped. The trap now tracks which service this run stopped, clears it once the daemon is confirmed back up (normal, `--reindex` and rollback paths alike), and otherwise reports the service by name with the commands to inspect and start it.
- **The stratum chain gate failed on a single unreachable RPC call.** The check ran once with a 60-second timeout immediately after the sync gate. Since the sync gate has already proved the daemon answers RPC, arriving here unreachable means a transient failure — a restart, a reloading wallet, a saturated RPC work queue — but the pool refused to start with `CHAIN GATE: refusing to mine`, which reads as an assertion that the node is on the wrong chain, and sends the operator to `coin-upgrade.sh` for a problem that does not exist. It now retries up to five times at six-second intervals, but **only** when the daemon is unreachable; a `minority` or stale verdict is conclusive and still fails immediately.
- **`TRADEMARKS.md` and `THIRD_PARTY_LICENSES.txt` still credited Bitcoin Knots as the shipped node implementation.** The trademark table listed `Bitcoin Knots | Luke Dashjr | Bitcoin node implementation (Docker image, native installer)` with no Bitcoin Core row, and the third-party licence entry read `Bitcoin Core / Bitcoin Knots`. Neither was true after this release. Both now name Bitcoin Core.
- **`tests/test_upgrade_regression.sh` had an assertion that failed against correct code.** Its build-before-stop ordering check grepped `main()` for `stop_services` and matched the *comment* "Must run BEFORE stop_services" (line 357) rather than the call (line 368), so the ordering compared 363 < 357 and failed. Verified pre-existing by running it against `git show HEAD:upgrade.sh`. It now blanks comment text before matching, which preserves line numbering and also handles `deploy_stratum`, invoked as `$UPDATE_STRATUM && deploy_stratum` rather than as a bare statement. The suite goes from 141 assertions with one failure to 142 passing.

- **Nothing anywhere said that a pool-generated wallet has no seed phrase.** Bitcoin Core and its derivatives have never supported BIP39, so a wallet the installer creates has no 12- or 24-word recovery phrase — the exported backup file is the only copy of the keys that will ever exist. The repo had zero occurrences of "seed phrase", "recovery phrase" or "mnemonic" in any script or document, while the installer told operators to "back up the wallet keys". Someone whose expectations come from Electrum or a hardware wallet reads that, waits for words that never appear, and concludes either that they missed a step or that the pool stores a phrase somewhere. Both conclusions cost them the funds. All thirteen coin wallet prompts now state it **before** the choice is made, both post-generation backup boxes state it again once keys are on disk, and `WARNINGS.md` gains a section covering it along with the restore commands for each backup format. The prompts also now point at the better option explicitly: create the wallet on another machine (hardware wallet, Electrum, Sparrow), keep the seed phrase there, and paste only the receiving address into the installer — so the private keys never touch the pool server at all. That path already existed and was already marked "recommended"; it simply never explained *why*.
- **The wallet-backup reminder is re-emitted during a coin upgrade.** `backup_coin` copies wallets before the binary swap, but that copy is on the same machine — it protects against the upgrade, not against losing the box. Anyone upgrading BTC today generated their wallet months ago and has had no reminder since. The upgrade now says so, repeats that no seed phrase exists, and prints a ready-to-paste `scp` command.

- **The upgrade tool trusted its own version cache over the binary on disk, so it could report a Knots node as current and never replace it.** `coin-upgrade.sh`'s `get_installed_version` returned the contents of `config/coin-versions/<COIN>.ver` unconditionally, consulting the daemon only when no cache file existed. The cache is there for daemons whose `--version` omits a number, but checking it first let it shadow reality for every coin. Concretely: an earlier run writes `BTC.ver=31.1`, then a rollback — or an operator following the restore command this same script prints on failure — puts a Bitcoin Knots binary back on disk. `get_installed_version` still answered `31.1`, `upgrade_coin` took its "already current" shortcut, `--check` printed a green tick, and the node went on following the BIP-110 minority chain. That is the silent failure this release exists to remove, reproduced inside the tool meant to remediate it. The binary is now asked first and the cache consulted only when the binary ran but printed nothing parseable; the version string keeps its build suffix so a Knots build can never compare equal to a Core release.
- **`get_existing_txindex` changed the setting it was written to preserve, in five input classes.** The helper added earlier in this release read the config with shell intuition — `case "$v" in 1|true)` — rather than the daemon's parser. Bitcoin Core and Dogecoin resolve a boolean with `InterpretBool`: an empty value is **true**, and otherwise the result is `atoi(value) != 0`. `atoi("true")` is 0, so `txindex=true` is **false** to the daemon while the helper emitted `txindex=1`; conversely `txindex=`, `txindex=01`, `txindex=2` and `txindex=1#comment` are all **true** to the daemon (the last because `GetConfigOptions` truncates each line at the first `#`) while the helper emitted nothing. On DigiByte's and PepeCoin's pre-0.17 bases a txindex change in either direction is a fatal `You need to rebuild the database using -reindex-chainstate to change -txindex` at startup — precisely the outage the helper exists to prevent, now caused by it. The resolution now mirrors `GetConfigOptions` + `InterpretBool` exactly, including comment truncation and `atoi` semantics, and `tests/test_conf_parser_semantics.sh` asserts all of it.
- **`spiralctl coin prune` reported success while pruning nothing, and left the config unreadable by the daemon.** The command stripped existing `prune=` lines and appended `prune=5000` at end of file. On any config carrying a section header the appended line lands **inside that section**, so mainnet is never pruned; the verification step then grepped the whole file, found the line, and confirmed the false success before restarting the daemon and letting the disk keep filling. The same class had already been fixed correctly in `upgrade.sh` (`_add_toplevel`) and `coin-upgrade.sh` (`dgb_enable_pruning_config`) — `spiralctl` was the third site and had neither guard. It now inserts into the top-level scope ahead of the first section header and verifies the **effective** mainnet value. Two further defects in the same block: no backup was taken (both siblings take one, fail-closed), and `mv` installed a temp inode created under `umask 077` by root, leaving the config `root:root 0600` while the daemon runs as the pool user — which then could not read its own config on the restart three lines later. Ownership and mode are now carried across with `chown/chmod --reference`.
- **An existing config no longer inherits the pool-wide prune target.** With `PRUNE_ENABLED=true`, a config whose prune value was unparseable (`prune=`, `prune=abc`) or absent fell through to the global default of 5000. The daemon reads all of those as 0 — archival — so regeneration wrote `prune=5000` onto a node that had been keeping full block data all along and began deleting it irreversibly on next start. An existing config is now authoritative; only a coin with no config yet takes the pool-wide default.
- **Wallet backup was skipped for every coin on a multi-disk install, and marked confirmed so it would never retry.** The guard tested `$INSTALL_DIR/<coin>/`, but `get_blockchain_dir` returns `$CHAIN_MOUNT_POINT/<coin>` when the installer has placed chains on separate disks. On such a host the directory never exists, the guard was always true, and the installer logged "address was manually provided, no pool wallet on server", wrote a `.backup-confirmed-<coin>` marker, and moved on — for all 15 coins.
